Coming full circle
Markus Frembs Institut für Theoretische Physik, Leibniz Universität Hannover, Appelstraße 2, 30167 Hannover, Germany Okinawa Institute of Science and Technology Graduate University, Onna, Okinawa 904 0495, Japan Centre for Quantum Dynamics, Griffith University, Yugambeh Country, Gold Coast, QLD 4222, Australia

Abstract
Contextuality is a key distinguishing feature between classical and quantum physics. It expresses a fundamental obstruction to describing quantum theory using classical concepts. In turn, when understood as a resource for quantum computation, it is expected to hold the key to quantum advantage. Yet, despite its long recognised importance in quantum foundations and, more recently, in quantum computation, the mathematics of contextuality has remained somewhat elusive - different frameworks address different aspects of the phenomenon, yet their precise relationship often is unclear. In fact, there is a glaring discrepancy already between the original notion of contextuality introduced by Kochen and Specker on the one side [J. Math. Mech., 17, 59, (1967)], and the modern approach of studying contextual correlations on the other [Rev. Mod. Phys., 94, 045007 (2022)]. In a companion paper [arXiv:2408.16764], we introduce the conceptually new tool called “context connections”, which allows to cast and analyse Kochen-Specker (KS) contextuality in new form. Here, we generalise this notion, and based on it prove a complete characterisation of KS contextuality for finite-dimensional systems. To this end, we develop the framework of “observable algebras”. We show in detail how this framework subsumes the marginal and graph-theoretic approaches to contextuality, and thus that it offers a unified perspective on KS contextuality. In particular, we establish the precise relationships between the various notions of “contextuality” used in the respective settings, and in doing so, generalise a number of results on the characterisation of the respective notions in the literature. Given the central role contextuality plays in quantum foundations and quantum computation, the unified framework presented here will be widely applicable. In particular, we expect the new tools provided by it to play a key role in quantifying contextuality as a resource, which remains an important open problem towards the efficient use of quantum computers.



A unified framework for Kochen-Specker contextuality

1
Introduction

Planck’s derivation of the spectrum of black body radiation, Einstein’s explanation of the photo-electric effect and Bohr’s phenomenological model for the hydrogen atom all employ the idea of quantised units of energy. The respective phenomena seemed otherwise hard to reconcile with classical physics, and soon ushered in the development of quantum mechanics, whose mathematical form was set in stone by von Neumann
[
178
]
. Yet, while those and many more theoretical breakthroughs - paired with overwhelming experimental confirmation of quantum-mechanical predictions - provide very strong evidence that nature is quantum at its core, they do not rule out the existence of a similarly ingenious description within the realms of classical physics: indeed, a description in terms of classical “hidden variables” is always possible in principle
[
35
,
29
]
.

Still, a decisive blow to the hidden-variable programme
[
71
]
was dealt by Bell’s theorem
[
27
]
, and the much more recent loophole-free verification of stronger than classical correlations in Bell experiments
[
59
,
20
,
86
,
169
]
, that is, of correlations described in terms of “classical states” - in the form of probability measures - on a “classical state space” - in the form of a measurable space, under the additional assumption of relativistic causality
[
182
]
. The study of Bell inequality violations
[
39
]
and the theory of quantum entanglement
[
106
]
form the cornerstone of quantum information science, and lie behind the promise of a (second) technological revolution based on quantum effects. Indeed, entanglement is at the heart of quantum cryptography
[
72
,
32
,
166
,
31
]
, quantum information processing
[
64
,
183
,
33
,
32
,
24
]
and has been shown to be a necessary resource for quantum computation
[
63
,
113
,
164
,
38
,
165
]
.

An even stronger candidate for the resource underlying quantum computers is
contextuality

[
162
,
107
,
80
,
81
]
, a concept closely related to Bell nonlocality. Indeed, a common slogan has that contextuality is a generalisation of Bell nonlocality. This statement refers to contextuality as understood in the “marginal approach”
[
40
]
, which - similar to and motivated by Bell inequalities - studies noncontextuality inequalities
[
125
,
44
,
21
,
188
,
123
,
17
,
52
,
185
]
, and seeks to show their experimental violation by quantum theory
[
25
,
121
,
14
,
92
,
138
,
130
,
181
]
. Yet, this approach is only partially related with the Kochen-Specker theorem
[
188
,
191
,
190
,
76
]
; to make matters worse, there exists by now a patchwork of different approaches, each studying its own notion of “contextuality”
[
108
,
42
,
93
,
43
,
172
,
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
102
,
103
,
8
,
10
,
7
,
57
,
82
,
52
,
11
,
145
,
70
,
147
,
146
]
, whose precise relationship often remains unclear. This not only makes cross-talk difficult, but also hinders progress towards an in-depth, quantitative analysis of contextuality as a resource in quantum computation. In this work, we (begin to) set this record straight.

To this end, it is instructive to compare the original notion of Kochen-Specker (KS) noncontextuality with Bell locality. Both concepts represent minimal, operationally motivated assumptions, under which a separation between quantum and classical physics can be obtained. Yet, while Bell’s theorem shows that certain (entangled) quantum
states
are incompatible with a classical state space description, the Kochen-Specker theorem
[
127
]
is concerned with the problem whether quantum
observables
admit a classical representation (regardless of whether composite or not). Both results demonstrate that quantum theory does not admit an interpretation in terms of classical (local or noncontextual) hidden variables, yet the respective conclusions differ: Bell’s theorem shows that the state space of quantum theory is not (fully) classical, while the Kochen-Specker theorem asserts that there can be no
classical embedding

\epsilon:\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{sa}}\rightarrow L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
of quantum observables into the space of measurable functions on some measurable space
\Lambda
(the “state” or “phase space” of a physical theory), such that functional relations between compatible observables are preserved (see Eq. (
2
)). As we will see in detail in Sec.
3
, for quantum (sub)systems the latter is a strictly stronger result.

Proving the Kochen-Specker theorem therefore is to show that no such embedding exists. The original proof of the Kochen-Specker theorem considers triples of squared spin-
1
observables
S^{2}_{x},S^{2}_{y},S^{2}_{z}
in three orthogonal directions in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which commute
[S^{2}_{x},S^{2}_{y}]=[S^{2}_{y},S^{2}_{z}]=[S^{2}_{z},S^{2}_{x}]=0
, share the same spectrum
\mathrm{sp}(S^{2}_{x})=\mathrm{sp}(S^{2}_{y})=\mathrm{sp}(S^{2}_{z})=\{0,1\}
and satisfy the algebraic constraint
S^{2}_{x}+S^{2}_{y}+S^{2}_{z}=2\mathbbm{1}
, where
\mathbbm{1}
denotes the identity in the space of linear operators
\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{3})
. Under the assumption that a classical embedding for spin-
1
observables exists, these properties imply that for any three orthogonal directions in space, one spin component must attain spin value 0, while the other two attain spin value 1. Such an assignment is also known as a
Kochen-Specker (KS)-colouring
(see Sec.
2.4
). Ref.
[
127
]
constructs a finite set of spin-
1
observables (defined via their spectral projections, represented by 117 rays in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
) that does not admit a KS-colouring. Subsequent proofs have reduced this number: the current record in three dimensions stands at 31 rays (and corresponding spin-
1
observables)
[
62
]
, and for more than three dimensions at 18 rays
[
48
]
; the latter is known to be optimal, the former is not
[
186
]
.

The choice of spin-
1
observables allows to reduce the problem of finding (an obstruction to the existence of) a classical embedding to a KS-colouring. However, the existence of a KS-colouring is only
necessary but not sufficient
for the existence of a classical embedding
[
76
]
. This discrepancy between KS-colourings and proofs of the Kochen-Specker theorem has been thrown into sharp relief by the search for experimental tests of contextuality within the marginal approach. More precisely, it was found that there exist noncontextuality inequalities which are violated by quantum theory, yet which do admit a KS-colouring
[
188
]
. This observation has been met with some surprise, which we take to express the fact that the precise relationship between the constraints on classical embeddings and the restriction to KS-colourings, as well as other similar notions of “contextuality” in the literature remain poorly understood
[
40
]
. In this work, we provide a careful analysis of and resolve various of these differences.

Contributions.
In Ref.
[
76
]
, we obtain a reformulation of Kochen-Specker contextuality (Eq. (
2
)), based on the notion of “context connections” (see Def.
8
), and constraints encoded by these along so-called “context cycles” (see Def.
9
) on the partial order of contexts corresponding to an observable algebra (see Sec.
2.1
). There, we focus on its conceptual motivation and, for comparison with Ref.
[
127
]
, discuss the case of spin-
1
systems only. Here, we generalise the formalism introduced in Ref.
[
76
]
and show that it applies universally, in particular, to any (subsystem of a) finite-dimensional quantum system. In short, our first main contribution in Sec.
2
, is to prove a

(i)

new, complete characterisation of Kochen-Specker (KS) contextuality.

To this end, we introduce the framework of “observable algebras”, which is motivated both by the partial algebras in Kochen and Specker’s original work
[
127
,
126
,
9
]
, as well as the more recent topos-theoretic formalisation in Ref.
[
108
,
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
102
,
103
,
67
]
. This generality enables us to compare KS contextuality with various notions of contextuality in the marginal approach (Sec.
3
), and the closely related (hyper)graph-theoretic approaches
[
57
,
82
,
52
,
11
,
12
]
(Sec.
4
); our second key contribution then consists in establishing

(ii)

observable algebras as a unified framework for KS contextuality.

In particular, we show in detail how existing frameworks embed within that of observable algebras and we precisely differentiate the respective notions of contextuality used in those. A summary of this comparison is given in Tab.
1
. This part both complements and substantially extends the recent review on the subject
[
40
]
.

Finally, by combining our characterisation of KS contextuality with the comparison between different notions of contextuality, in particular, with the notion of state-independent contextuality (SI-C) in the graph-theoretic approach, we identify a

(iii)

necessary criterion for an orthogonality graph to be state-independently contextual (see Def.
26
and Def.
27
), in terms of its chromatic number.

This complements existing partial results in Ref.
[
45
,
51
]
and resolves an open conjecture in Ref.
[
160
]
. The main results of this paper are summarised in the following list.

2
Algebraic characterisation of Kochen-Specker contextuality

In Ref.
[
76
]
we reformulated the notion of Kochen-Specker (KS) contextuality
[
127
]
in new algebraic form, for the special case of spin-
1
quantum systems. This reformulation revealed stronger constraints than those arising from the existence of valuations, which reconciles the notion of KS contextuality with proofs of the KS theorem in the form of violations of noncontextuality inequalities
[
155
,
131
,
125
,
21
,
188
]
in those cases. Here, we lift this reformulation to a complete characterisation of KS contextuality, and generalise it to (and beyond) the setting of (finite-dimensional) partial algebras
[
127
]
. Since Kochen-Specker contextuality was originally defined in this setting, this shows that the tools introduced in Ref.
[
76
]
capture the full extent of KS contextuality. Moreover, it will allow us to carefully compare and contrast it with various related notions of contextuality in the literature, specifically with the “marginal approach”
[
8
,
11
,
40
]
in Sec.
3
and with the graph-theoretic approach
[
52
]
in Sec.
4
.

2.1
Observables, events, contexts

Various notions of “contextuality” exist, making it rather difficult to differentiate between them. One contribution of this work is a thorough comparison between the most commonly used notions, applied to ideal measurements (see Sec.
3
below). In order to do so, we first establish the relevant notation and terminology we will be using.

(Abstract) Observable algebras.
We will introduce the relevant structures by comparison with the familiar setting of quantum theory. There, observables are represented by self-adjoint (Hermitian) operators
O\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
acting on a finite-dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
.
2
2

2

More generally, an observable in quantum mechanics is a self-adjoint operator in a von Neumann algebra
\mathcal{N}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
, that is, a weakly closed subalgebra of the algebra of bounded operators on a (separable) Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Presently, we will only consider finite-dimensional (quantum) systems.
The commutator equips
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
with a reflexive, symmetric, yet generally not transitive relation. Physically, the commutator encodes a notion of
joint measurability
or
compatibility
between quantum observables. Indeed, Heisenberg’s uncertainty relation prohibits an experimenter from measuring non-commuting observables jointly with arbitrary precision. As a consequence, the physical interpretation of algebraic operations between noncommuting operators remains somewhat opaque. However, these operations have a well-defined meaning when restricted to commuting operators.
3
3

3

Indeed, algebraic operations between jointly measurable observables may be considered part of an experimenter’s ability to analyse her experimental data. In this view, they become consistency conditions for formal representations of the observables of a system, as we will emphasise below.
This suggests to restrict algebraic relations to compatible observables.

Definition 1
.

An
abstract observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
over a field
K
is a set with a binary relation
\odot\subset{\mathcal{O}}\times{\mathcal{O}}
(“joint measurability” or “compatibility”), an identity element
\mathbb{I}\in{\mathcal{O}}
such that
\mathbb{I}\odot O
for all
O\in{\mathcal{O}}
, and such that addition, multiplication and scalar multiplication are defined and close on the domain of
\odot
, that is, for all
O_{1},O_{2},O_{3}\in{\mathcal{O}}
with
O_{1}\odot O_{2}
,
O_{2}\odot O_{3}
and
O_{1}\odot O_{3}
, one also has
(O_{1}+O_{2})\odot O_{3}
,
(O_{1}O_{2})\odot O_{3}
and
\lambda O_{1}\odot O_{3}
for all
\lambda\in K
.
4
4

4

Throughout, we will take
K=\mathbb{R}
, and thus usually omit specifying it explicitly.

\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
with the anti-commutator defines an
(abstract) observable algebra
, where the algebraic operations between noncommuting observables are forgotten. A key motivation to generalise from this example is to consider observable subalgebras of quantum theory. For instance, restrictions to subalgebras appear naturally in the context of quantum computation, and experimental setups more generally. Quantum observable algebras satisfy an additional property known as
Specker’s principle

[
171
,
133
,
99
,
83
,
45
]
: any triple of pairwise compatible elements
O_{1},O_{2},O_{3}\in{\mathcal{O}}
with
O_{1}\odot O_{2}
,
O_{2}\odot O_{3}
and
O_{1}\odot O_{3}
is fully compatible, that is,
O_{1}\odot O_{2}\odot O_{3}
. With quantum theory in mind, Kochen and Specker define partial algebras as observable algebras that satisfy Specker’s principle
[
127
]
. Yet, our formalism applies without this restriction.
5
5

5

Another motivation to study general observable algebras is the search for possible extensions to quantum theory, as well as to identify (physically motivated) principles that single out quantum theory amongst such extensions. For instance, Specker’s principle has been suggested as such a principle
[
171
,
99
,
83
,
45
]
. The tools developed in this work and in Ref.
[
76
]
might therefore also prove useful towards the reconstruction paradigm in quantum foundations
[
98
,
97
,
89
,
111
,
140
,
139
]
.

Note that Def.
1
is abstract in the sense that it does not specify the elements of
{\mathcal{O}}
explicitly. To do so, we will represent observables
O\in{\mathcal{O}}
in an abstract observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
as random variables (measurable functions)
O:\Sigma_{O}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
on some measurable spaces
\Sigma_{O}
, such that whenever
O\odot O^{\prime}
there exists a measurable space
\Sigma_{O,O^{\prime}}\supset\Sigma_{O},\Sigma_{O^{\prime}}
(hence,
O:\Sigma_{O,O^{\prime}}|_{\Sigma_{O}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
O^{\prime}:\Sigma_{O,O^{\prime}}|_{\Sigma_{O^{\prime}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
). In this view, the algebraic structure between compatible observables is that of an algebra of measurable functions.

Definition 2
.

An
observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
is an abstract observable algebra whose elements are given as random variables
O:\Sigma_{O}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
\Sigma_{O}
a measurable space for every
O\in{\mathcal{O}}
.

Every observable algebra thus defines an abstract observable algebra; however, different observable algberas may correspond to the same abstract observable algebra. Below, we will restrict to observables with finitely many outcomes, thus leaving topological issues for future work. As a consequence, the measurable spaces
\Sigma_{C}\supset\{\Sigma_{O}\}_{O\in C}
corresponding to any subset of mutually compatible observables
C\subset{\mathcal{O}}
, that is, with
O\odot O^{\prime}
for all
O,O^{\prime}\in C
, will always have finite cardinality.
6
6

6

In the general case, one may want to restrict to measurable spaces such that the space of measurable functions on it has nice algebraic properties. A natural choice is to consider Hyperstonean spaces, which are categorically equivalent to (the dual of) commutative von Neumann algebras
[
149
]
. An alternative choice is to consider (locally) compact Hausdorff spaces and continuous functions (vanishing at infinity), which are dually equivalent to unital (non-unital) commutative
C^{*}
-algebras.

Contexts and their order.
Next, we take an alternative perspective: instead of considering an observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
as a set of individual observables, we collect observables into
(measurement) contexts
, that is, subsets
C\subset{\mathcal{O}}
of jointly measurable ones. What type of subsets? Recall that every self-adjoint operator
O\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
has a spectral decomposition
O=\sum_{i=1}^{\dim(\mathcal{H})}\lambda_{i}p_{i}
for
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
,
p_{i}\in{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
with
p_{i}p_{j}=\delta_{ij}
and
{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
the (lattice of) projections on
\mathcal{H}
. We denote by
\mathrm{sp}(O)=\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i}
the
spectrum
, that is, the set of eigenvalues of
O
. Given any function
h:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
we obtain another self-adjoint operator by
h(O)=\sum_{i=1}^{\dim(\mathcal{H})}h(\lambda_{i})p_{i}
.
7
7

7

For observables with continuous spectra,
h
needs to be a measurable function.
Functional relations of this form may be considered part of the side-processing of our experimental setup, and should thus be preserved in any formalisation of
{\mathcal{O}}
.
8
8

8

For instance, an experimenter is free to relabel the outcomes of observables in her experiment.
This motivates to include all such relations in our description, that is, we represent a
(measurement) context
in quantum theory as a commutative subalgebra
C\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
, and collect all contexts into the partial order
{{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{H})}=(\{C\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}\mid C\mathrm{\ commutative}\},\subset)
.
9
9

9

For infinite-dimensional quantum systems, a natural topology on observable algebras is the weak operator topology, such that contexts are represented by commutative von Neumann subalgebras of
{\mathcal{O}}=\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{sa}}
of some von Neumann algebra
\mathcal{N}
. Alternatively, one may consider compact Hausdorff spaces which are dually equivalent to the category of unital commutative
C^{*}
-algebras
[
102
,
103
,
177
,
100
,
94
]
. Here, we restrict to finite-dimensional algebras, for which all relevant operator topologies coincide. We find it useful to henceforth explicitly distinguish this in our notation by denoting finite-dimensional commutative algebras (contexts) by
C
instead of
V
, and the corresponding partial order of contexts by
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
instead of
{\mathcal{V}({\mathcal{O}})}
, as was used in related previous work
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
67
,
77
,
75
,
78
]
.

{\mathcal{O}}
is generally not an algebra itself. Yet, since addition, multiplication and scalar multiplication close on the domain of
\odot
by Def.
1
, we may still collect jointly measurable observables into contexts in the form of commutative algebras (over
K=\mathbb{R}
), generated by collections of jointly measurable observables.
10
10

10

Under the restriction to random variables with finitely many outcomes, the respective commutative algebra arises with respect to the closure under addition, multiplication and scalar multiplication of its generating elements. More generally, one further has to impose topological constraints.
The definition of the partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{H})}
in Ref.
[
69
,
67
]
thus admits the following generalisation.

Definition 3
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an observable algebra over the field
K
. The set of commutative algebras
C\subset{\mathcal{O}}
over
K
, ordered by inclusion, is called the
partial order of contexts
(or
context category
11
11

11

A partial order is a category with objects the elements in the partial order and morphisms its relations.
)
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
of
{\mathcal{O}}
.

We call a context
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}

maximal
if
C\subset C^{\prime}
for
C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
implies
C=C^{\prime}
, and write
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
for the set of maximal contexts in
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
.

We obtain a “spectral decomposition” for any observable
{\mathcal{O}}\ni O:\Sigma_{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
of the form
O=\sum_{\sigma}\sigma p_{\sigma}
with
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
and where
p_{\sigma}:=\chi_{\Sigma_{\sigma}}:\Sigma_{C}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
is the indicator function of the subset
\Sigma_{\sigma}\subset\Sigma_{C}
defined by
\chi_{\Sigma_{\sigma}}(\sigma)=1
if and only if
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\sigma}\subset\Sigma_{C}
. We will write
{\mathcal{P}(C)}
for the (representation of the) Boolean algebra
{{\mathcal{B}}(C)}=2^{\Sigma_{C}}
in terms of indicator functions on
\Sigma_{C}
, and write the algebraic relations as addition and multiplication (instead of set union and intersection), in analogy with the quantum case. Stressing this analogy, we will similarly refer to the set
\mathrm{sp}(O)=\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i}
in the spectral decomposition
O=\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}p_{i}
of an observable
O\in{\mathcal{O}}
as its
spectrum
and to the elements
p_{i}\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
as
projections
or
events
.

Since every commutative algebra
C\subset{\mathcal{O}}
contains the identity element
\mathbb{I}
, we have
C_{\mathbb{I}}:=\{\lambda\mathbb{I}\mid\lambda\in K\}\subset C
for all
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Hence,
C_{\mathbb{I}}
is the least element of
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Conversely, any partial order of finite-dimensional commutative algebras with least element
C_{\mathbb{I}}
defines an observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}=\bigcup_{C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}}C
, with identity given by (identifying) the maximal indicator functions
\mathbb{I}:=\chi_{\Lambda_{C}}
in every maximal context
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, and by setting
O\odot O^{\prime}
if and only if there exists
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
such that
O,O^{\prime}\in C

[
53
]
.

Event algebras.
Next, we consider the restriction of an observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
to its elementary dichotomic (“yes-no”) observables, equivalently to its
events
or
projections
. These correspond to the elements
p^{O}_{i}
in the spectral decomposition
O=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathrm{sp}(O)}\sigma p^{O}_{\sigma}
for
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
of observables
O\in{\mathcal{O}}
. Note that
\{p^{O}_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma}
generate the Boolean subalgebra
{\mathcal{P}(C)}\cong 2^{\Sigma_{C}}
, given by the restriction of the minimal commutative subalgebra
C\subset{\mathcal{O}}
containing
O
to its projections.
12
12

12

As noted above, we identify the elements
p\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
with indicator functions
\chi:\Sigma_{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and thus write the algebraic operations in
{\mathcal{P}(C)}
additively and multiplicatively.

It is a defining property of a classical system that all observables are jointly measurable, hence, are contained in a single measurement context. In other words, every classical observable can be represented as a measurable function
O\in L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
with respect to a single measurable space
(\Lambda,\sigma)
(the “state space” of the theory). In this case, the events are defined by the
\sigma
-algebra of measurable subsets of
\Lambda
which defines a Boolean algebra
{\mathcal{B}}(\Lambda)
under union, intersection and complement.
13
13

13

One further obtains a complete Boolean algebra after quotienting out the
\sigma
-ideal of null sets.

On the other hand, the spectral decomposition of observables
O,O^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}}
in a general observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
may differ, that is, may involve different events. Still, whenever two observables are jointly measurable
O,O^{\prime}\in C
, they have a common spectral decomposition, hence, describe events in a common Boolean algebra (generated by the elements in)
{\mathcal{P}(C)}
. Instead of a single Boolean algebra, the events of an observable algebra are thus described by a collection of Boolean algebras
[
127
,
96
,
126
,
68
,
53
,
95
,
9
]
.

Definition 4
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an observable algebra. The
event algebra
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
of
{\mathcal{O}}
is the collection of Boolean algebras
{\mathcal{P}(C)}
for all contexts
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, that is,
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}=\bigcup_{C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}}{\mathcal{P}(C)}
.

Our notation
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
is supposed to indicate that its elements are represented as indicator functions on the event spaces
\Sigma_{C}
corresponding to sets of jointly measurable observables. In analogy with Def.
1
, we may also define an abstract event algebra
{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. An (abstract) event algebra is a partial Boolean algebra if it satisfies Specker’s principle. In particular, every orthomodular lattice gives rise to a partial Boolean algebra that is moreover transitive
[
91
]
. For instance, the event algebra
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}})
arises from the lattice of projections
{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
on
\mathcal{H}
by restricting operations to commuting elements only.

Finally, we remark that the information coded into an event algebra can also be cast as a hypergraph, as defined in Ref.
[
82
,
11
]
, with vertices the atoms and hyperedges given non-minimal projections in
{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}})}
(equivalently, by Boolean subalgebras
{{\mathcal{B}}(C)}\in{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}})}
). Conversely, a hypergraph defines an abstract event algebra
{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}})}
once we identify the identity element as
\mathbb{I}=\sum_{v\in e}v
for every maximal hyperedge
e\in E
. From this perspective (and by Lm.
1
below), both formalisms are thus concerned with the same underlying structure. However, whereas the latter studies contextuality in terms of correlations (see also Sec.
3
), our approach is entirely algebraic and thus complementary to it.
14
14

14

In addition, we point out that the hypergraph approach
[
57
,
11
]
is specifically concerned with the study of correlations on composite systems. In this regard, the perspective of contexts seems helpful in that specifying an identity element (hence, a minimal context) for local subsystems automatically ensures that product correlations are non-signalling. Indeed, the Cartesian product of context categories allows for a natural description of Bell’s theorem
[
67
]
; moreover, Ref.
[
77
,
75
]
characterise both quantum and separable quantum states from general non-signalling correlations, under the additional assumption that observables (hence, their partial order of contexts) are locally quantum
[
122
,
180
,
23
]
.

Finite-dimensional and maximal observable algebras.

Definition 5
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an observable algebra with event algebra
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
. An additive function
\dim:{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
with
\dim(p)+\dim(p^{\prime})=\dim(p+p^{\prime})
whenever
p,p^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
with
pp^{\prime}=0
for some
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
is called a
dimension function on
{\mathcal{O}}
.

{\mathcal{O}}
is called
finite-dimensional
if there exists a dimension function on
{\mathcal{O}}
.

A dimension function assigns an integral size to every projection
p\in{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
, and we will write
\mathcal{P}_{k}({\mathcal{O}})=\{p\in{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}\mid\dim(p)=k\}
with
1\leq k\leq d
for the set of
k
-dimensional projections in
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Often, we will not be interested in the exact choice of dimension function, and simply say
{\mathcal{O}}
has dimension
d
if there exists a dimension function such that
\dim(\mathbb{I})=d
. For instance, the (minimal) dimension function on
{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
is given by
\dim(p)=\mathrm{rk}(p)
for every
p\in{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
. Clearly, by restricting to finite-dimensional observable algebras, we ensure that observables have only finitely many outcomes.
15
15

15

However, the converse is generally not true: there exist observable algebras whose elements are finite random variables, yet which do not admit a dimension function (see App.
E
).
Notably, this is a restriction of the type of observable algebra in Def.
1
. Nevertheless, since we will restrict to finite-dimensional observable algebras throughout we will sometimes omit stating this explicitly.

Definition 6
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a finite-dimensional observable algebra of dimension
\dim(\mathbb{I})=d
. Then
{\mathcal{O}}
is called
maximal
if every maximal context
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
is minimally generated by
d
elements, equivalently if there exists a resolution of the identity
\mathbb{I}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}p^{C}_{i}
for minimal projections
p^{C}_{i}\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
with
\dim(p^{C}_{i})=1
.
16
16

16

Since
p^{C}_{i}\in{\mathcal{P}_{1}({\mathcal{O}})}
are minimal, this implies that
p^{C}_{i}p^{C}_{j}=0
whenever
i\neq j
.

We will write
|C|:=|\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}(C)|
for the cardinality of a minimal generating set of
C
.
17
17

17

Here,
p\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
is called minimal, and denoted
p\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}(C)
, if
p^{\prime}p=p^{\prime}
implies
p^{\prime}=p
for all
p^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
. A set of observables, e.g. dichotomic observables, equivalently events is called a minimal generating set if removing any element of the set will generate a strictly smaller context.
Clearly, if an observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
is maximal then all maximal contexts
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
are isomorphic. However, the converse is not true, since a maximal observable algebra also specifies a dimension function which may not exist (see App.
E
). Moreover, not every finite-dimensional observable algebra is maximal, since the (discrete) spectra of observables in an observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
generally do not have the same cardinality. Consequently, they may generate commutative algebras that are not isomorphic. Note that quantum observable algebras
{\mathcal{O}}=\oplus_{i}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{i})
are maximal, with minimal projections given by rank-
1
projections. While we will mostly restrict the discussion to maximal observable algebras in the main text, in App.
B
we show that the characterisation of Kochen-Specker contextuality for (finite-dimensional) observable algebras can be reduced to that of maximal ones.

2.2
Kochen-Specker contextuality for observable algebras

Having introduced the necessary terminology in the previous section, it is straightforward to generalise the notion of Kochen-Specker (KS) noncontextuality in Ref.
[
127
,
76
]
to observable algebras. We reiterate that in their original work, Kochen and Specker considered partial algebras which are observable algebras satisfying in addition Specker’s principle
[
127
]
. We briefly review the motivation behind the original definition of KS contextuality, before reformulating it in terms of an embedding problem for observable algebras. For a more detailed introduction, see Ref.
[
127
,
67
,
76
]
.

Kochen-Specker contextuality as an embedding problem.
Recall that an observable
O\in C\subset{\mathcal{O}}
has a spectral decomposition
O=\sum_{i=1}^{|C|}\sigma_{i}p_{i}
for
\sigma_{i}\in\mathrm{sp}(O)
,
p_{i}\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
and
|C|=|\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}(C)|
. Moreover,
h(O)=\sum_{i=1}^{|C|}h(\sigma_{i})p_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}
for any function
h:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. We call an observable
non-degenerate (for
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
)
if
|\mathrm{sp}(O)|=|C|
and
degenerate
otherwise. It is easy to see that every context is generated by a single non-degenerate observable,
18
18

18

More generally, every abelian von Neumann algebra is generated by a single Hermitian operator.

\displaystyle C(O)=\{h(O)\mid h:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\}\;.

(1)

Note that a generating observable for
C
is not unique, however any two generating observables
O,O^{\prime}
for
C
are related by an invertible function
h:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
O^{\prime}=h(O)
. If
h
is non-invertible on
\mathrm{sp}(O)
, then
\mathrm{sp}(h(O))\subset\mathrm{sp}(O)
and
h(O)
is degenerate.
19
19

19

Observable algebras thus generalise the situation in quantum mechanics, where two self-adjoint operators
O,O^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
commute if and only if there exists another self-adjoint operator
O^{*}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
and (measurable) functions
h,h^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
O=h(O^{*})
and
O^{\prime}=h^{\prime}(O^{*})

[
41
]
.

It is natural to require maps between observable algebras
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
to preserve such functional relationships between observables in the same context, that is,
20
20

20

Again, if we take these functional relations to be part of an experimenter’s ability to reason about and analyse her measurement data, they should be independent of our theoretical model for
{\mathcal{O}}
.
21
21

21

One may raise the objection that we should not identify the observables
g(O)
and
g^{\prime}(O^{\prime})
if
O,O^{\prime}
are generating observables of two distinct contexts
C,C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, since by our interpretation of such functional relations, the observables
g(O)
and
g^{\prime}(O^{\prime})
a priori also correspond to different measurement contexts (that of
O
and
O^{\prime}
, respectively). The response to this objection is that it is natural to identify observables (and their measurement contexts) mathematically whenever they cannot be distinguished experimentally, that is, whenever both yield the same statistics in any state (cf. Ref.
[
173
]
).

\forall O\in{\mathcal{O}},\forall g:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}:\quad\epsilon(g(O))=g(\epsilon(O))\;.

(2)

Following Ref.
[
127
,
76
]
, we call any faithful map
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
that preserves the functional constraints in Eq. (
2
) an
embedding of
{\mathcal{O}}
. Specifically, Kochen-Specker noncontextuality is concerned with the existence of a
classical embedding of
{\mathcal{O}}
, that is, an embedding into a classical observable algebra of the form
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}=L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
for a measurable space
\Lambda
.
22
22

22

If
{\mathcal{O}}
is a finitely generated observable algebra, then
\Lambda
can be taken to be a finite set. Note also that a finitely generated observable algebra with a classical embedding is automatically finite-dimensional (see Fn.
71
). However, for general finite-dimensional observable algebras
\Lambda
need not be finite.

Definition 7
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an observable algebra. Then
{\mathcal{O}}
is called
Kochen-Specker (KS) noncontextual
if it admits a classical embedding. Otherwise,
{\mathcal{O}}
is called
KS contextual
.
23
23

23

The notion of KS noncontextuality is arguably a minimal requirement for a classical representation of
{\mathcal{O}}
. It only refers to the algebraic structure, in particular, it does not also demand that states on
{\mathcal{O}}
be represented classically (cf. Def.
16
), which generically results in a stronger condition (see Sec.
3
).

In fact, any (not just a classical) embedding encodes a notion of
noncontextuality
, namely the representation
\epsilon(O)\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
of
O\in{\mathcal{O}}
(e.g. in terms of a measurable function on some measurable space) does not depend on the context, that is, on other jointly measurable observables: if
{\widetilde{O}}\in C,C^{\prime}
with
C\neq C^{\prime}
, generating observables
C=C(O)
and
C^{\prime}=C(O^{\prime})
and functions
{\widetilde{O}}=g(O)=g^{\prime}(O^{\prime})
, then
g(\epsilon(O))=\epsilon({\widetilde{O}})=g^{\prime}(\epsilon(O^{\prime}))
. Since the identity map is a valid (classical) embedding, every classical system is Kochen-Specker noncontextual. In contrast, the famous Bell-Kochen-Specker theorem demonstrates that quantum theory is Kochen-Specker contextual
[
28
,
127
]
. As one of the most crucial insights into the structure of quantum mechanics, it has sparked a vast array of research in the almost six decades since it was first formulated. In particular, contextuality and nonlocality fuel the promise that quantum computation will one day outperform present-day classical computers
[
162
,
107
,
80
,
37
,
81
]
. In this setting, the embedding problem in Def.
7
(for quantum subsystems) is closely related to the existence of a classical simulation
[
5
,
90
,
114
,
148
,
184
]
. Yet, despite significant progress both on the foundational and computational side, the mathematical structure underlying Kochen-Specker contextuality has remained obscure
[
188
,
40
,
112
]
. In order to further unveil it, we first cast the notion of Kochen-Specker noncontextuality into context form.

Kochen-Specker contextuality as order structure.
The different structures in Sec.
2.1
- observable algebras, event algebras and the partial order of contexts - have given rise to various definitions of contextuality, e.g. for measurement scenarios
[
8
]
, (hyper)graphs
[
52
,
57
,
11
]
, partial orders of contexts etc.
[
108
,
1
,
69
,
103
,
67
]
. This easily creates the impression of various inequivalent notions of contextuality. In Sec.
3
(and Sec.
4
), we will indeed identify two distinct notions of contextuality, namely the algebraic notion of KS noncontextuality and the notion based on (demanding) correlations (to be classical). Despite this distinction, the underlying structures are equivalent (up to redundancies, see App.
D
). In particular, they are independent of the representation in terms of observable algebras, event algebras or context categories.

Lemma 1
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
be finite-dimensional observable algebras with context categories
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})},{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
and event algebras
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})},\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
, respectively. Then
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
is an embedding if and only if
\varphi:{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
is a faithful orthomorphism
24
24

24

Here, by an orthomorphism we mean a map
\varphi:{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
such that
\varphi(\mathbb{I})=\mathbb{I}^{\prime}
,
\varphi(\mathbb{I}-p)=\mathbb{I}^{\prime}-\varphi(p)
for all
p\in{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
as well as
\varphi(p+q)=\varphi(p)+\varphi(q)
for all
p,q\in{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
,
pq=0
.
if and only if
\eta:{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
is a faithful order-preserving map.

Proof.

Clearly, an embedding of observable algebras
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
restricts to a faithful orthomorphism
\varphi=\epsilon|_{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}:{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
. Conversely, every faithful orthomorphism
\varphi:{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
generates an embedding of observable algebras
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
by setting
\epsilon(O)=\sum_{i=1}^{|C|}\sigma_{i}\varphi(p_{i})
for every
{\mathcal{O}}\ni O=\sum_{i=1}^{|C|}\sigma_{i}p_{i}
with
p_{i}\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
and
|C|=|\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}(C)|
.

Since the order relations in
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
are defined by the functional constraints in
{\mathcal{O}}
, every embedding of observable algebras
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
induces a map
\eta:{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
of the form
\eta(C)=\epsilon[C]
. Clearly,
\eta
is faithful. Moreover, let
{\widetilde{C}},C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
be contexts in
{\mathcal{O}}
with generating observables
{\widetilde{O}},O\in{\mathcal{O}}
, that is,
{\widetilde{C}}={\widetilde{C}}({\widetilde{O}})
and
C=C(O)
as in Eq. (
1
), and such that
{\widetilde{O}}=g(O)
. Then it follows from Eq. (
2
) that
\eta
preserves order relations:

\displaystyle\eta({\widetilde{C}})=\epsilon[{\widetilde{C}}]

\displaystyle=\epsilon[\{\widetilde{h}({\widetilde{O}})\mid\widetilde{h}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\}]

\displaystyle=\epsilon[\{\widetilde{h}(g(O))\mid\widetilde{h}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\}]

\displaystyle\subset\epsilon[\{h(O)\mid h:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\}]=\epsilon[C]=\eta(C)\;.

Conversely, from a faithful order-preserving map
\eta:{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
, we construct a faithful orthomorphism (and thus a faithful embedding by the first assertion) as follows. First, we represent the minimal element
C_{\mathbb{I}}\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
by the unique two-element Boolean algebra
{\mathcal{B}}(C_{\mathbb{I}})
generated by a single element which we denote by
\mathbb{I}
and
0=\mathbb{I}^{\perp}
. Second, for every context
C_{\mathbb{I}}\neq C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
such that
C\geq C^{\prime}>C_{\mathbb{I}}
implies
C=C^{\prime}
(here, we use that
{\mathcal{O}}
is finite-dimensional), define a Boolean algebra
{{\mathcal{B}}(C)}
generated by two elements
p_{C}
and
\mathbb{I}
. Proceeding in this manner, by induction in the length of chains in
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, we thus obtain representations of the elements
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
in terms of Boolean algebras
{{\mathcal{B}}(C)}
and their minimal generating set. Similarly, we obtain a representation of the contexts
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
in terms of Boolean algebras
{\mathcal{B}}(C^{\prime})
and their generating elements.

Now, define
\varphi:{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
by
\varphi(p_{C})=p_{C^{\prime}}
whenever
\eta(C)=C^{\prime}
, in particular, we have
\varphi(\mathbb{I})=\mathbb{I}^{\prime}
. By construction, we further have
\varphi(\mathbb{I}-p_{C})=\mathbb{I}^{\prime}-\varphi(p_{C})
for all
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, hence, for all
p_{C}\in{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
, as well as
\varphi(p_{\widetilde{C}}+p_{C})=\varphi(p_{\widetilde{C}})+\varphi(p_{C})
whenever
{\widetilde{C}}\subset C
, hence for all
p_{\widetilde{C}},p_{C}\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
with
p_{\widetilde{C}}p_{C}=0
. Consequently,
\varphi
is an orthomorphism, it is faithful since
\eta
is, and extends to an embedding by the first assertion.
∎

Lm.
1
shows that even though we formulated Kochen-Specker noncontextuality in terms of observable algebras in Def.
7
, we could have equivalently defined it in terms of context categories. In other words, the functional constraints in Eq. (
2
) are fully contained in, and thus a property of the partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
only. What is more, this is true not only for classical embeddings, but applies to embeddings into observable algebras of other forms, too, e.g. observable algebras in quantum theory
{\mathcal{O}}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
. This will become important when we compare the notion of Kochen-Specker contextuality in Def.
7
with other notions of contextuality, specifically with those in the marginal and graph-theoretic approach. We thus echo, yet generalise the insight from Ref.
[
108
,
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
67
]
:
(Kochen-Specker) contextuality is order structure.

But how do we infer whether
{\mathcal{O}}
is Kochen-Specker contextual from
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
only? Observe that the same function
g:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
generally induces different context inclusions for different choices of generating observables of a context
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, that is,
C(g(O))\neq C(g(O^{\prime}))
even if
C=C(O)=C(O^{\prime})
. In particular, when
O,O^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}}
share the same spectrum
\mathrm{sp}(O)=\mathrm{sp}(O^{\prime})
, yet their spectral decomposition is distinct (related by a permutation of the generating projections), then the action of
g
yields observables generating different subcontexts. As we will see (in Thm.
1
) in the next section, the interplay of such relations across contexts fully characterises KS contextuality.

2.3
Complete characterisation of Kochen-Specker contextuality

Lm.
1
asserts that whether an observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
is Kochen-Specker (non)contextual is a property of its partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. In order to extract from
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
the relevant information to solve the classical embedding problem in Def.
7
, we generalise the tools introduced in Ref.
[
76
]
from quantum to maximal observable algebras.

Context connection.
We first generalise Def. 1 in Ref.
[
76
]
.

Definition 8
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a maximal observable algebra of dimension
\dim(\mathbb{I})=d
with partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. A
(context) connection
\mathfrak{l}=(l_{C^{\prime}C})_{C,C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}}
on
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
is a collection of bijective maps
l_{C^{\prime}C}:\mathcal{P}_{1}(C)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{1}(C^{\prime})
with
l_{CC^{\prime}}=l^{-1}_{C^{\prime}C}
and such that

\displaystyle l_{C^{\prime}C}|_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(C\cap C^{\prime})}={\mathrm{id}}\quad\forall C,C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}\;.

(3)

Any context connection thus respects the identification of projections
\mathcal{P}(C\cap C^{\prime})
in subcontexts
C\cap C^{\prime}
for all
C,C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Note that since
|\mathcal{P}_{1}(C)|=|\mathcal{P}_{1}(C^{\prime})|=d
by assumption,
l_{C^{\prime}C}|_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(C)}
is a bijection (see Fig.
1
).

l_{C^{\prime}C}|_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(C\cap C^{\prime})}=\mathrm{id}
for all maximal contexts

.

Context cycles.
Below, we will consider the noncontextuality constraints imposed by Eq. (
2
), and encoded in the inclusion relations of
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
by Lm.
1
, across multiple contexts at the same time. To this end, we generalise Def. 2 from Ref.
[
76
]
.

Definition 9
.

A
context cycle
\gamma
in
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
is a tuple
\gamma=(C_{0},\cdots,C_{n-1})
of
maximal contexts
C_{i}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, together with subcontexts
C_{i\cap i+1}=C_{i}\cap C_{i+1}
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.
25
25

25

Here,
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
denotes the abelian group under addition modulo
n
, in particular,
(n-1)+1=0\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.

Context cycles have been considered elsewhere in similar form, e.g. in the study of sheaf cohomology
[
10
,
54
,
26
,
6
]
. However, our formalism based on context connections and the results we obtain differ from those in these works. In particular, our approach is algebraic in nature, and not in terms of states (empirical models defined on measurement scenarios, see App.
D.1
), and the characterisation of KS contextuality in Thm.
1
is complete, not restricted to cyclic or finitely generated models. In Sec.
3
, we will compare the algebraic and marginal approach to contextuality in more detail.

:

, with

).

Algebraic characterisation of Kochen-Specker noncontextuality.
The main result of this section is a generalisation of Thm. 2 in Ref.
[
76
]
from quantum to general finite-dimensional, but not necessarily maximal observable algebras (see Tab.
1
).

Theorem 1
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a finite-dimensional observable algebra with partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Then
{\mathcal{O}}
is Kochen-Specker noncontextual, that is, admits a classical embedding
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
, if and only if there exists a context connection
\mathfrak{l}=(l_{C^{\prime*}C^{*}})_{C^{*},C^{\prime*}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}}^{*})}}
on the partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}}^{*})}\supset{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
of its maximal extension in Lm.
9
, which satisfies the
triviality
constraints,

\circ_{i=0}^{n-1}l_{C^{*}_{(i+1)}C^{*}_{i}}={\mathrm{id}}\;,

(4)

for every context cycle
\gamma=(C^{*}_{0},\cdots,C^{*}_{n-1})
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}}^{*})
.

A context connections satisfying the triviality constraints in Eq. (
4
) will be called
flat
.

Proof.

(Sketch) The proof of Thm. 2 in Ref.
[
76
]
only uses that maximal commutative algebras have the same cardinality
|C|=|\mathcal{P}_{1}(C)|=d
for all
C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})
, where for quantum observable algebras
{\mathcal{O}}=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
the dimension
d=\dim(\mathcal{H})
is determined by the dimension of the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. The result thus carries over easily to maximal observable algebras. While Thm. 2 in
[
76
]
established that the constraints in Eq. (
4
) are necessary for KS noncontextuality, here we also show that they are sufficient. We provide the details of the proof for the case of maximal observable algebras in App.
A
.

Moreover, by Lm.
9
in App.
B
, every finite-dimensional observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
can be extended to a maximal observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
and, by Lm.
10
,
{\mathcal{O}}
is Kochen-Specker noncontextual if and only if its maximal extension
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
is.
∎

Numerous proofs of the Kochen-Specker theorem construct so-called Kochen-Specker sets (see Def.
10
)
[
127
,
134
,
151
,
192
,
118
,
119
,
48
]
, many of which are easily seen to directly exploit the cyclic nature of the constraints in Eq. (
4
). Moreover, “cyclic contextuality scenarios” have been studied elsewhere
[
17
,
54
,
26
,
70
]
, in particular, Ref.
[
17
,
185
]
characterise cyclic contextuality scenarios in the marginal approach to contextuality. However, as we will see in Sec.
3
, the relevant notion of contextuality differs from Kochen-Specker noncontextuality in Def.
7
. Indeed, somewhat surprisingly, we will show that cyclic scenarios are never Kochen-Specker noncontextual in Sec.
3.3.3
.

Yet, before turning to a detailed comparison between the algebraic and marginal approach in Sec.
3
, we first cast Thm.
1
as a colouring problem, which will allow us to contrast it with the notion of Kochen-Specker colourings which feature in traditional proofs of the Kochen-Specker theorem. Moreover, this reformulation will serve as a preparation for our characterisation of state-independent contextuality graphs in Sec.
4
.

2.4
Kochen-Specker colouring vs d-colouring

Following the original argument by Kochen and Specker
[
127
]
, many proofs of the Kochen-Specker (BKS) theorem
26
26

26

As in Ref.
[
76
]
, by the “Kochen-Specker theorem” for an observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
we mean the assertion that
{\mathcal{O}}
does not admit a classical embedding, hence, a proof that
{\mathcal{O}}
is Kochen-Specker (KS) contextual. We recall that Ref.
[
28
]
first proved that finite-dimensional quantum systems of dimension at least three are KS contextual, while Ref.
[
127
]
inferred this result by constructing a finite KS set, equivalently a finitely generated observable subalgebra
{\mathcal{O}}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
with
\dim(\mathcal{H})=3
.
show the nonexistence of
valuations

v:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that (i)
v(O)\in\mathrm{sp}(O)
for all
O\in{\mathcal{O}}
(“spectrum rule”) and (ii)
v(g(O))=g(v(O))
for all functions
g:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
(“functional composition (FUNC) principle”). It is easy to see that a valuation defines a
Kochen-Specker (KS)
or
2
-colouring
by restriction.

Definition 10
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a maximal observable algebra of dimension
\dim(\mathbb{I})=d
with event algebra
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
and partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. A
Kochen-Specker (KS-)colouring
\zeta_{\mathrm{KS}}
of
{\mathcal{O}}
is a map
\zeta_{\mathrm{KS}}:{\mathcal{P}_{1}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
such that for every maximal context
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
there exists exactly one one-dimensional projection
p\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(C)
with
\zeta_{\mathrm{KS}}(p)=1
.

Conversely, by linear extension any Kochen-Specker colouring induces a map
v:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying (i) and (ii), hence, defines a valuation. Any subset of projections not admitting a valuation, equivalently a KS colouring, is called a
Kochen-Specker (KS) set
.

However, the existence of valuations is only a necessary but not sufficient condition for KS noncontextuality. This was emphasised in the marginal approach to contextuality
[
40
]
(see also Ref.
[
76
]
and Sec.
3
below), where it was shown that there exist noncontextuality inequalities that are violated by every quantum state, hence, yield a proof of the KS theorem, yet for which the involved observables, equivalently their generating one-dimensional projections
\mathcal{P}\subset{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{H})}
, do not constitute a KS set
[
188
]
. In Ref.
[
76
]
, we showed that this is not in contradiction with the notion of KS contextuality, but that the proof of the KS theorem in Ref.
[
188
]
also follows from Eq. (
2
). Motivated by this result, and fostered by Thm.
1
, we now lift this argument and cast the full set of KS noncontextuality constraints in Eq. (
2
) as a colouring problem.

Definition 11
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a maximal observable algebra of dimension
\dim(\mathbb{I})=d
, event algebra
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
and partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. A
d
-colouring of
{\mathcal{O}}
is a map
\zeta:\mathcal{P}_{1}({\mathcal{O}})\rightarrow[d]
such that for every maximal context
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
there exists exactly one one-dimensional projection
p\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(C)
with
\zeta(p)=k
for every
k\in[d]=\{0,\cdots,d-1\}
.

We say that
{\mathcal{O}}
is
d
-colourable if it admits a
d
-colouring. Clearly, a
d
-colouring (for
d\geq 2
) induces a KS-colouring (hence, a valuation) by identifying any one colour with
1
and the remaining
d-1
colours with
0
. Yet, a KS-colouring can generally not be extended to a
d
-colouring. For an explicit example, see Ref.
[
188
,
76
]
.

For ease of comparison with the graph-theoretic approach in Sec.
4
, the following definition and terminology will be useful.

Definition 12

(associated orthogonality graph)
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an observable algebra with event algebra
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Then its
associated orthogonality graph
{G({\mathcal{O}})}=(V,E)
is defined by
V={\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, where
(p,q)\in E
if and only if
p\neq q
and
p,q\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
for some context
C\subset{\mathcal{O}}
.

It is easy to see that the existence of a
d
-colouring of a maximal observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
is equivalent to
\chi(G({\mathcal{O}}))=d
.

Theorem 2
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a maximal observable algebra with
\dim(\mathbb{I})=d
, event algebra
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
and partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Then
{\mathcal{O}}
is Kochen-Specker noncontextual if and only if
{\mathcal{O}}
admits a
d
-colouring, equivalently
\chi(G({\mathcal{O}}))=d
.

Proof.

Assume first that
{\mathcal{O}}
admits a
d
-colouring
\zeta
, and denote by
\zeta_{C}:\mathcal{P}_{1}(C)\rightarrow[d]
the respective colouring in maximal contexts
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. From these we define bijections
l_{C^{\prime}C}:\mathcal{P}_{1}(C)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{1}(C^{\prime})
by
\zeta_{C^{\prime}}\circ l_{C^{\prime}C}=\zeta_{C}
, that is, by identifying those (one-dimensional) projections in
C
and
C^{\prime}
that are assigned the same colour by
\zeta
. Lifting the maps
l_{C^{\prime}C}
to orthomorphisms defines a context connection on
{\mathcal{O}}
: the condition
l_{C^{\prime}C}|_{\mathcal{P}(C\cap C^{\prime})}={\mathrm{id}}
holds since
\zeta
is a
d
-colouring which implies
\zeta_{C}|_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(C\cap C^{\prime})}=\zeta_{C^{\prime}}|_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(C\cap C^{\prime})}
. Moreover, since
\zeta=(\zeta_{C})_{C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}}
is a
d
-colouring,
\mathfrak{l}=(l_{C^{\prime}C})_{C,C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}}
clearly satisfies the triviality constraints in Eq. (
4
), hence,
{\mathcal{O}}
is KS noncontextual by Thm.
1
.

Conversely, assume that there exists a flat context connection
\mathfrak{l}=(l_{C^{\prime}C})_{C,C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}}
(that is,
\mathfrak{l}
satisfies the triviality constraints in Eq. (
4
)). Pick any maximal context
C_{0}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
and any
d
-colouring
\zeta_{C_{0}}:\mathcal{P}_{1}(C_{0})\rightarrow[d]
. For every other context
C_{0}\neq C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, fix a
d
-colouring
\zeta^{C_{0}}_{C}:\mathcal{P}_{1}(C)\rightarrow[d]
by
\zeta^{C_{0}}_{C}\circ l_{CC_{0}}=\zeta_{C_{0}}
. Clearly,
\zeta^{C_{0}}_{C}
is compatible with
\zeta_{C_{0}}
, that is,
\zeta^{C_{0}}_{C}|_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(C\cap C_{0})}=\zeta_{C_{0}}|_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(C\cap C_{0})}
since
l_{CC_{0}}|_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(C\cap C_{0})}={\mathrm{id}}
(by definition of a context connection). Now,
(\zeta^{C_{0}}_{C})_{C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}}
defines a
d
-colouring if the colourings
\zeta^{C_{0}}_{C}
and
\zeta^{C_{0}}_{C^{\prime}}
are compatible for all
C,C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. But this follows since

\displaystyle\zeta^{C_{0}}_{C^{\prime}}\circ l_{C^{\prime}C_{0}}=\zeta_{C_{0}}=\zeta^{C_{0}}_{C}\circ l_{CC_{0}}=\zeta^{C_{0}}_{C}\circ(l_{C_{0}C^{\prime}}\circ l_{C^{\prime}C})^{-1}=\zeta^{C_{0}}_{C}\circ l_{CC^{\prime}}\circ l_{C^{\prime}C_{0}}\;,

where we used
l_{C_{0}C^{\prime}}\circ l_{C^{\prime}C}\circ l_{CC_{0}}={\mathrm{id}}
from Eq. (
4
) for the context cycle
(C_{0},C,C^{\prime})
and
l^{-1}_{CC^{\prime}}=l_{C^{\prime}C}
. Indeed, multiplication by
l^{-1}_{C^{\prime}C_{0}}
from the right yields
\zeta^{C_{0}}_{C^{\prime}}=\zeta^{C_{0}}_{C}\circ l_{CC^{\prime}}
for every
C,C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
which implies compatibility since
l_{C^{\prime}C}|_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(C\cap C^{\prime})}={\mathrm{id}}
.
∎

Thm.
2
makes explicit that the restriction to valuations (equivalently, KS-colourings) in traditional proofs of the KS theorem is a severe restriction on the constraints imposed by Kochen-Specker noncontextuality (see Tab.
2
).

Finally, we generalise Thm.
2
to non-maximal observable algebras.

Theorem 3
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an observable algebra with maximal extension
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
for
\dim(\mathbb{I})=d
. Then
{\mathcal{O}}
is Kochen-Specker noncontextual if and only if
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
admits a
d
-colouring, equivalently
\chi(G({\mathcal{O}}^{*}))=d
.

Proof.

Since
{\mathcal{O}}
is KS contextual if and only if
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
is KS contextual for any (hence, every) maximal extension by Lm.
10
, the result follows immediately from Thm.
2
.
∎

Clearly, generally
\chi(G({\mathcal{O}}))<\chi(G({\mathcal{O}}^{*}))
since
G({\mathcal{O}})
arises from
G({\mathcal{O}}^{*})
by subsequently deleting the vertices corresponding to minimal projections in
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
, and in each step the chromatic number either remains the same or is reduced by one. Nevertheless, in Sec.
4.3
we will see that it is sufficient to consider the chromatic number of subgraphs
G\subset G({\mathcal{O}}^{*})
whenever
\overline{\pi(V)}={\mathcal{O}}^{*}
for
\pi:G\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}^{*})
is a faithful completion (see Lm.
7
).

In summary, the chromatic number of the orthogonality graph associated with any maximal extension of an observable algebra is a complete invariant of Kochen-Specker contextuality (see Tab.
1
).

{\mathcal{O}}

\nexists\chi_{\mathrm{KS}}\ \ (\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{Val}}(G({\mathcal{O}}))=\emptyset
)

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{Ref.}~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{KochenSpecker1967}{}{}]}}}{{\Longrightarrow:}}

{\mathcal{O}}

\Downarrow
