Table 2
:
As first argued by Kochen and Specker, the existence of valuations is a necessary condition for KS noncontextuality
[
127
]
. In turn, traditional proofs of KS contextuality show the nonexistence of valuations, equivalently Kochen-Specker colourings (see Def.
10
)
[
127
,
134
,
151
,
192
,
118
,
119
,
48
]
. However, constructing KS sets is not necessary to prove KS contextuality
[
188
]
. By Thm.
2
, a necessary and sufficient condition for KS contextuality for the case of maximal observable algebras is the existence of a
d
-colouring. This generalises Thm. 4 in Ref.
[
76
]
which considers the case of maximal quantum observable algebras of dimension three. What is more, the characterisation extends to non-maximal observable algebras with respect to their maximal extensions by Thm.
3
.

3
Algebraic vs marginal approach

In this section, we relate the characterisation of Kochen-Specker contextual observable algebras in Thm.
1
to some of the more commonly used formalisms for studying contextuality in the literature. Our focus will be on the marginal approach to contextuality
[
40
]
, which is based on the study of noncontextuality inequalities
[
155
,
131
,
125
,
44
,
21
,
123
,
188
,
185
]
and has been the dominating approach in recent years; it comprises the sheaf-theoretic
[
8
,
10
,
7
]
and (hyper)graph-theoretic
[
57
,
82
,
52
,
11
,
12
]
variants. In App.
D
, we provide a comprehensive formal comparison between the respective frameworks, which permits us to cast the basic ingredients needed for the present exposition in the language of observable algebras defined in Sec.
3.1
. Based on this, our first objective is to precisely disambiguate the two notions of contextuality employed in the algebraic and marginal setting, respectively. In particular, we will translate the notion of KS contextuality into the marginal setting in Sec.
3.2
.

Our second objective is to export the insights gained from Thm.
1
, and to demonstrate their utility by explicitly showing how they allow us to easily recover and go beyond a number of existing key results in the literature. Specifically, we will prove that for acyclic observable algebras the constraints in Thm.
1
are always satisfiable. In this way, we will obtain a new explanation for the existence of noncontextual models such as those devised in Ref.
[
136
,
117
,
61
]
. Moreover, in Sec.
3.3
we will relate this result with Vorob’ev’s theorem (Thm.
6
), which states that acyclicity fully characterises classicality in the marginal approach. In contrast, we highlight that KS noncontextuality does not imply acyclicity, which we demonstrate by studying the general
n
-cycle contextuality scenario
[
17
]
, as well as an explicit example in the form of the well-known CHSH scenario
[
59
]
.

Nevertheless, we will obtain a full characterisation of KS contextuality in terms of graph-colourings in Sec.
4
, which may be seen as complementary to this section. Together, these results provide a unified perspective between the algebraic and marginal approach to Kochen-Specker contextuality. They also highlight the fruitfulness of the concept of
context connection
, and thus of the framework laid out here and in Ref.
[
76
]
.

Finitely generated observable algebras and minimal generating sets.
The main motivation for the generalisation from quantum to more general observable algebras is that in experimental setups one is usually concerned with (finite-size) subsets of measurements only.
27
27

27

Importantly, this comment applies to various architectures in quantum computation and quantum error correction which utilise finite gate sets and measurements.
In other words, we will mainly be interested in observable subalgebras
{\mathcal{O}}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
in quantum theory. The various formalisms in the marginal approach usually restrict to finite measurement scenarios by default (see App.
D.1
for details). In order to compare the characterisation of Kochen-Specker noncontextualy in Thm.
1
to these, we will henceforth also restrict to the ‘‘finiteness’’ of experimental setups.
28
28

28

Most proofs of the Kochen-Specker theorem
[
127
]
(and related formalisations of contextuality) restrict to finitely many observables or events. Nevertheless, proofs involving infinitely many observables do exist
[
28
,
65
]
, and are based on continuity arguments following Gleason’s theorem
[
87
]
. We leave the interesting problem of adding topology to the study of Kochen-Specker contextuality for future work.

Of course, already a single context, that is, a single commutative algebra
C\subset{\mathcal{O}}
contains infinitely many observables. However, the observables in a context
C
are not independent, but are functionally related. More precisely, recall from Eq. (
1
) that every context is generated by a single (although generally not unique) observable with spectral decomposition
O=\sum_{i=1}^{|C|}\sigma_{i}p_{i}
for
\sigma_{i}\in\mathrm{sp}(O)
,
p_{i}\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
and
|C|=|\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}(C)|=|\mathrm{sp}(O)|
.

Recall that we interpret the relations between a generating observable
O
of
C
, and other observables
{\widetilde{O}}=g(O)\in C
for
g:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as part of the side-processing of an experimental arrangement, e.g. a quantum computer. In this view, an experimental setup formally corresponds with infinitely many observables, yet with finitely many generating observables. In other words, experimental setups correspond with observable algebras whose partial order of contexts is finite.

Definition 13
.

An observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
is called
finitely generated (fin. gen.)
if the set of commutative algebras in
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
is finite, equivalently if
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
is a finite set.

Similarly,
{\mathcal{O}}
is called countably infinitely generated if
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
is a countable set.

At first sight, by removing part of the algebraic structure in commutative algebras
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, measurement scenarios in the sheaf-theoretic approach (see Def.
32
) and orthogonality graphs in the graph-theoretic approach (see Def.
21
) achieve a significant reduction in the representation of “contextuality scenarios”
[
82
]
. Yet, as it turns out this seeming reduction generally results in a redundant representation from the perspective of Kochen-Specker contextuality in the first case, and leads to different notions of contextuality in the second (see Sec.
4
). In a nutshell, the measurement labels in measurement scenarios are generally not minimal as generating sets for
{\mathcal{O}}
(see App.
D.1
), while orthogonality graphs lack a notion of identity element which results in states on orthogonality graphs being incompatible with subsystem embedding (see App.
D.2
).
29
29

29

To a lesser extent this is also true for the hypergraph approach
[
57
,
11
]
. However, for single systems hypergraphs are equivalent to event algebras, and thus equivalent to
{\mathcal{O}}
by Lm.
1
.
In any case, up to a choice of generating set the correspondence between observable algebras and measurement scenarios is one-to-one; while the correspondence between observable algebras and orthogonality graphs is one-to-one up to adding an identity element. For the precise statements, we refer to App.
D
.
30
30

30

We reiterate that in this work we only consider the case of ideal observables.
Without loss of generality, and emphasising this unifying trait of our formalism, we will therefore keep the subsequent presentation in terms of finitely generated (and finite-dimensional) observable algebras.

3.1
Basic elements of the marginal approach

The key tenet of the marginal approach is to study contextuality in terms of states.

Definition 14
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an observable algebra with partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. A
state
\gamma
on
{\mathcal{O}}
is a collection of probability distributions
\gamma=(\gamma_{C})_{C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}}
such that

\displaystyle\gamma_{\widetilde{C}}=\gamma_{C}|_{\widetilde{C}}\quad\quad\forall{\widetilde{C}},C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}\mathrm{\ with\ }{\widetilde{C}}\subset C\;,

(5)

where
\gamma_{C}|_{\widetilde{C}}
denotes the marginalised distribution with respect to
{\widetilde{C}}
. The set of all states on
{\mathcal{O}}
is denoted by
\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
.
31
31

31

Equivalently,
\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
is the set of global sections of the probabilistic presheaf over
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Here, we will use an abbreviated version of the notation in Ref.
[
67
,
78
]
, leaving the choice of (probabilistic) presheaf over
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
implicit. More generally, the topos- and sheaf-theoretic approaches to contextuality study the existence of global sections of other presheaves
[
108
,
66
,
67
]
. Amongst other things, this results in a fine-grained notion and corresponding hierarchy of contextuality as shown in Ref.
[
8
]
.

The partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
thus encodes marginalisation constraints on states. The terminology in Def.
14
is standard in the topos approach to quantum theory
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
8
,
67
]
, the hypergraph approach
[
57
,
11
,
12
]
as well as in the literature on quantum logic which studies properties of orthomodular lattices
[
34
,
159
,
116
]
.
32
32

32

A partial Boolean algebra
\mathcal{P}
is an orthomodular lattice
[
116
]
if and only if it is
transitive
, that is, if and only if for all
a,b,c\in\mathcal{P}
,
a<b
and
b<c
implies
a<c

[
91
]
.
In contrast, when contextuality is viewed as a generalisation of nonlocality, a state is often referred to as a
correlation

[
158
,
157
]
; in the sheaf-theoretic approach, a state is also called an
empirical model

[
8
]
. Similarly, the noncontextuality condition in Eq. (
5
) is referred to by various names, including
no-disturbance

[
161
,
77
,
75
]
and the
sheaf condition

[
8
]
.

Definition 15
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a (finitely generated) observable algebra with partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
and event algebra
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
. A state
\gamma=(\gamma_{C})_{C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}}\in\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
is said to admit a
solution to the marginal problem over
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
if there exists an orthomorphism
\phi_{\gamma}:{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}(\Lambda)
for some measure space
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\phi_{\gamma}}
with Boolean
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathcal{B}}(\Lambda)
and probability measure
\mu_{\gamma}\in L^{\geq 0}_{1}(\Lambda)
such that for all
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
and
p\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{C}(p)=\int_{\Lambda}d\lambda\ \mu_{\gamma}(\lambda)\phi_{\gamma}(p)(\lambda)=\int_{\Lambda}d\lambda\ \mu_{\gamma}(\lambda)\chi(\lambda\mid p)=\mu_{\gamma}(\Lambda_{p})\;,

(6)

where
\phi_{\gamma}(p)=\chi(\cdot\mid p)
denotes the indicator function for the (measurable) subset
\Lambda\supset\Lambda_{p}\in{\mathcal{B}}(\Lambda)
representing
p\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
under
\phi_{\gamma}
, defined by
\chi(\lambda\mid p)=1
if and only if
\lambda\in\Lambda_{p}
.

The representation of the marginal problem in terms of the second equality in Eq. (
6
) is common in the literature on the noncontextuality polytope
[
155
,
123
]
. There,
\Lambda
is taken to represent a space of “hidden variables”, in analogy with (the assumption of local hidden variables in) Bell’s theorem
[
27
]
. Often
\chi(\lambda\mid p)
is only required to be a probability distribution over
\Lambda
for every
p\in{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Yet, by Fine’s theorem
[
74
,
174
,
8
]
if
\gamma\in\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
admits a solution to the marginal problem with
\chi
a probability distribution then it also admits a solution to the marginal problem with
\chi
an indicator function.
33
33

33

For unsharp measurements, Eq. (
6
) needs to be modified, see e.g. Ref.
[
172
,
189
,
128
,
129
]
.

Making this simplification lets us rephrase the definition of solutions to the marginal problem in terms of the existence of orthomorphisms
\phi_{\gamma}:{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}(\Lambda)
. (Possibly up to a
\sigma
-ideal of negligible sets)
34
34

34

For finitely generated observable algebras
\Lambda
can be taken to be a finite set. Moreover, in this case an optimal representation in terms of valuations is constructed in Lm.
5
below.

\phi_{\gamma}
satisfies
{\mathrm{supp}}(\chi(\cdot\mid\mathbb{I}))=\Lambda
,
{\mathrm{supp}}(\chi(\cdot\mid 0))=\emptyset
and whenever
p,p^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
for
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
then
{\mathrm{supp}}(\chi(\cdot\mid pp^{\prime}))={\mathrm{supp}}(\chi(\cdot\mid p))\cap{\mathrm{supp}}(\chi(\cdot\mid p^{\prime}))
and
{\mathrm{supp}}(\chi(\cdot\mid p+p^{\prime}))={\mathrm{supp}}(\chi(\cdot\mid p))\cup{\mathrm{supp}}(\chi(\cdot\mid p^{\prime}))
. Since these constraints all pertain to individual contexts, they can be justified operationally, for instance, by demanding that the statistics of repeated measurements of compatible observables be reproduced, as is usually the case in experimental tests of contextuality
[
44
,
92
,
181
]
.

Finally, note that phrasing Def.
15
in terms of orthomorphisms automatically ensures that the indicator functions are independent of contexts, that is,
\chi(\lambda\mid p)=\chi(\lambda\mid p,C)
for all contexts
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
with
p\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
. Eq. (
6
) thus incorporates the assumption that the representation in terms of hidden variables
\Lambda
is independent of contexts.

We emphasise that
\phi_{\gamma}
is not required to be faithful. This will become important in the comparison between different notions of contextuality in the marginal approach below.

3.2
Contextuality in the marginal approach

In this section we compare the marginal approach to contextuality with the algebraic one, as defined in Def.
7
. We will first highlight that the respective notions of contextuality differ, that is, that they capture different aspects of “(non-)classicality”. Specifically, we find that the more commonly adopted notion concerns the existence of non-classical (“contextual”) correlations which is generically stronger than that defined by the classical embedding problem underlying Kochen-Specker noncontextuality. In a second step, we will then provide a reformulation of the latter in terms of a refined marginal problem.

3.2.1
Two distinct notions of classicality

The algebraic approach to Kochen-Specker contextuality is concerned with the existence of a classical (or other type of) embedding for an observable algebra. In contrast, the marginal approach to contextuality studies the subset of states on observable algebras that is compatible with classical (or other types of) embeddings.

Classical correlations.
Def.
15
characterises a subset of
classical states

\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]\subset\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
on
{\mathcal{O}}
, as those states that are compatible with, that is, arise as states under some orthomorphism
\phi:{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}(\Lambda_{\phi})
for some measurable space
\Lambda_{\phi}
with Boolean
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathcal{B}}(\Lambda_{\phi})
. More precisely,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]=\bigcup_{\phi}L^{\geq 0}_{1}(\Lambda_{\phi})|_{\phi({\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})})}
, where
|_{\phi({\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})})}
denotes marginalisation as in Def.
14
. Similarly, we can define
\Gamma_{\mathrm{qm}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]\subset\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
as those states on
{\mathcal{O}}
that are compatible with orthomorphisms into a quantum system. Together, this defines the following hierarchy of states (see also Sec.
4.1
):

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]\subset\Gamma_{\mathrm{qm}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]\subset\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]=:\Gamma_{\mathrm{nd}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]\;,

(7)

where the latter state space contains all non-disturbing states.
35
35

35

In analogy with the definition of classical and quantum states,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{nd}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
can be defined as the set of states compatible with orthomorphisms of
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
into general observable algebras. Since the identity map defines such an orthomorphism we have
\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]=\Gamma_{\mathrm{nd}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
. Similarly, by restricting to observable algebras with additional structure, for instance, within the setting of general probabilistic theories
[
156
,
139
,
55
]
, one may further refine the hierarchy of states in Eq. (
7
).

In general, the inclusions in Eq. (
7
) are strict and give rise to the well-known and much studied problems of characterising the respective state spaces
[
179
,
155
,
123
,
11
,
185
]
. Notably, all sets in Eq. (
7
) are convex, hence, can be described in terms of linear inequalities
[
21
,
123
]
. What is more,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
is a polytope
[
155
]
, hence, it is characterised by its finitely many facet-defining
noncontextuality inequalities
; in the Bell-local case, these are known as
Bell inequalities
. In turn, the violation of such inequalities, can thus be used to rule out a classical state space model for
{\mathcal{O}}
.
36
36

36

Maximal violations of noncontextuality inequalities by quantum mechanics generalise (“Tsirelson”-) bounds on Bell inequalities
[
176
]
from nonlocal to contextual correlations, see e.g. Ref.
[
17
]
.
Bell’s theorem
[
27
]
and the (loophole-free) experimental violation of Bell inequalities
[
20
,
86
,
169
]
irrefutably show that quantum physics is non-local: its state space is not a classical state space that is compatible with composition (in terms of products of measure spaces
[
67
]
). A similar approach has been taken for contextuality
[
125
,
44
,
188
,
123
,
17
,
52
,
185
]
; in analogy with the (Bell) local case, the following notion of classicality is therefore often adopted.

Definition 16
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an observable algebra with partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Then
{\mathcal{O}}
is said to be
(fully) classically correlated
if every state
\gamma\in\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
on
{\mathcal{O}}
admits a solution to the marginal problem defined by
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, that is, if
\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]=\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
.

If an observable algebra is classically correlated, then it cannot violate any noncontextuality inequality, and vice versa. Conversely, if an observable algebra is not fully classically correlated, then there exists some state that cannot be explained in terms a classical model, hence, is contextual in the sense of the marginal approach. If such correlations can be observed, they amount to an “experimental test of contextuality”
[
25
,
121
,
14
,
92
,
138
,
130
,
181
]
. It is with respect to Def.
16
, that the often quoted relation “nonlocality implies contextuality” is to be understood.

Classical correlations vs classical embeddings.
In turn, the above implication is not true when contextuality is understood in the sense of Eq. (
2
). Indeed, the existence of a contextual state does
not
refute the existence of a classical embedding.

Since this goes against the commonly held dictum stated above, we take a moment to provide some intuition by means of a well-known example. It is straightforward to define a classical embedding of the observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{CHSH}}
defined by the CHSH scenario
[
59
]
(for details, see Ex.
1
below). Yet, clearly this does not imply that every state on
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{CHSH}}
can be represented in terms of a classical state, that is, by a probability distribution on the state space
\Lambda
under the embedding. In particular, (most) entangled states are not classical (see Fig.
4
(b)). We remark that in the Bell-local case of composite systems at spacelike separation, the violation of Bell inequalities requires entanglement, hence, is a state-dependent notion; separable states do not violate Bell inequalities. More generally, whenever an observable algebra admits a classical embedding, the violation of a noncontextuality inequality is necessarily state-dependent. In contrast, the violation of noncontextuality inequalities can also be state-independent (see Tab.
4
and Sec.
4.3
).

Proposition 1
.

There exist observable algebras
{\mathcal{O}}
which are Kochen-Specker noncontextual, yet which are not fully classically correlated, hence, which exhibit non-classical (e.g. quantum) correlations, that is, for which
\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]\neq\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
.
37
37

37

Analogously, there exist observable algebras that admit quantum embeddings while also exhibiting super-quantum correlations
[
139
]
. This leads to the fascinating problem of characterising quantum correlations
[
158
,
150
,
23
,
99
,
83
,
47
,
157
,
77
]
and the related research programme on reconstructions of quantum theory, e.g. from information-theoretic principles
[
167
,
98
,
97
,
89
,
58
,
104
,
105
,
140
]
.

Consequently, nonlocality in the sense of violations of Bell inequalities does
not
imply KS contextuality as defined by the embedding problem in Eq. (
2
)!

What about the reverse implication? On a formal level this turns out to be false, too (see Lm.
11
). However, it holds under the mild assumption that states on
{\mathcal{O}}
are separating (for details, see App.
C
). For separating observable algebras, Def.
16
is strictly stronger than Def.
7
. In particular, finitely generated classically correlated observable subalgebras
{\mathcal{O}}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
in quantum theory are KS noncontextual.

In summary, the notions of classicality in Def.
7
and Def.
16
differ, hence, define two different problems with different interpretations and consequences. While KS noncontextuality in Eq. (
2
) clearly implies the existence of
some
classical states, Def.
16
demands that
every
state on
{\mathcal{O}}
is represented by a classical state under the embedding
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
.
Note that both constraints appear in Ref.
[
127
]
,
38
38

38

Eq. (
2
) is Eq. (4) in Ref.
[
127
]
, while Def.
16
generalises Eq. (2) in Ref.
[
127
]
from quantum to general observable algebras.
yet the KS theorem
26
only addresses Eq. (
2
), and proves more than the mere existence of contextual correlations. For a concise comparison between the two approaches, see Tab.
3
.

notion of “classicality”

characterisation

KS noncontextuality (Def.
7
)

\exists
flat context connection
\mathfrak{l}

{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}

\forall g:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}:\ \epsilon(g(O))=g(\epsilon(O))

\mathbf{\neq}

\mathbf{\neq}

)

\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]

NC inequalities
[
155
,

,
21
,

]

Table 3
:
Comparison of approaches to noncontextuality: Kochen-Specker (KS) noncontextuality in Eq. (
2
) is algebraic, that is, it is defined in terms of functional relations between observables
{\mathcal{O}}
(first row), and characterised by the triviality constraints in Eq. (
4
); the marginal approach studies states
(\gamma_{C})_{C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}}\in\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
on
{\mathcal{O}}
, which satisfy no-disturbance by definition and are “classical” if they can be represented as in Eq. (
6
) (second row). The respective notions of classicality generally differ (see Prop.
1
and Lm.
11
); nevertheless, in Thm.
4
we translate KS noncontextuality into the marginal approach.

While KS noncontextuality differs from the notion of classicality in Def.
16
, in the next section we show that it can be given a reformulation as a marginal problem.

3.2.2
Kochen-Specker contextuality in the marginal approach

The notion of classicality in the marginal approach is concerned with characterising the state spaces in Eq. (
7
). In contrast, Kochen-Specker noncontextuality is concerned with the underlying classical embedding problem defined in Def.
7
. In this section, we will obtain a reformulation of the latter within the marginal approach. It is instructive to first consider the notion of contextuality obtained by exchanging the universal quantifier in Def.
16
with an existential one.

Definition 17
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an observable algebra with partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Then
{\mathcal{O}}

exhibits classical correlations
if there exists a state
\gamma\in\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
on
{\mathcal{O}}
that admits a solution to the marginal problem defined by
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, that is, if
\emptyset\neq\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]\subset\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
.

In particular, the converse of Def.
17
implies that every state on
{\mathcal{O}}
violates some noncontextuality inequality.
39
39

39

We will address the more subtle distinction between this notion and the one where all states violate the same noncontextuality inequality in Sec.
4.3
.
Clearly, if
{\mathcal{O}}
is KS noncontextual, then it exhibits classical correlations. Moreover, Def.
17
is weaker than Def.
16
which (under the restriction separating observable algebras, see App.
C
) is stronger than KS noncontextuality. Indeed, Def.
16
proves too weak to imply KS noncontextuality.

Lemma 2
.

There exist observable algebras
{\mathcal{O}}
which are Kochen-Specker contextual, yet which exhibit classical correlations (see Def.
17
), that is, for which
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]\neq\emptyset
,

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{1},{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
be two observable algebras such that
{\mathcal{O}}_{1}
is KS contextual and
{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
is KS noncontextual. Then their product
{\mathcal{O}}={\mathcal{O}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
is also KS contextual.
40
40

40

Similarly, if both
{\mathcal{O}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
are KS noncontextual, their product is KS noncontextual, too. For extensions of the notion of products of observable and partial Boolean algebras, see Ref.
[
67
,
9
]
.
However, since
{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
admits a classical embedding
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\rightarrow L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
by assumption, there exist states
\gamma\in L_{1}(\Lambda)
which define (non-faithful) states on
{\mathcal{O}}
, hence,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]\neq\emptyset
.
∎

Consequently, the notions of contextuality in Def.
7
and Def.
17
also differ. Mathematically, the reason is that the orthomorphism, defined by a state
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
as by Def.
15
, is not required to be faithful, hence, does not induce an embedding by Lm.
1
. Yet, this immediately suggests how to strengthen the existence of classical states in Def.
17
: to the existence of (sets of) classical states that separate events in
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
.

Definition 18
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an observable algebra with partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Then
{\mathcal{O}}
is called
marginally Kochen-Specker (KS) noncontextual
if for all projections
p,p^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
,
p\neq p^{\prime}
, there exists a classical state
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]\subset\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
such that

0\neq\gamma(p)\neq\gamma(p^{\prime})\neq 0
on
{\mathcal{O}}
.

We call the set of states in Def.
18
a
separating set
(see also Def.
31
). For finitely generated observable algebras a separating set
\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
is finite and its existence thus equivalent to the existence of a single state with that property. Indeed, by choosing appropriate mixing parameters
\omega_{i}\in[0,1]
with
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\omega_{i}=1
,
\gamma=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\omega_{i}\gamma_{i}
satisfies
0\neq\gamma(p)\neq\gamma(p^{\prime})\neq 0
for all
0\neq p,p^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
, and clearly
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
.

As the terminology suggests, Def.
18
is equivalent to (the classical embedding problem defined by) Kochen-Specker noncontextuality in Def.
7
, as we now prove.

Theorem 4
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a finitely generated observable algebra. Then
{\mathcal{O}}
is Kochen-Specker noncontextual if and only if it is marginally Kochen-Specker noncontextual.

Proof.

“
\Rightarrow
”: If
{\mathcal{O}}
is KS noncontextual, then there exists a classical embedding
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
for some measurable space
\Lambda
, which restricts to a faithful orthomorphism of event algebras
\varphi=\epsilon|_{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}:{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}(\Lambda)
. Let
\widetilde{\Lambda}=(\Lambda,\sigma(\epsilon))
denote the measurable space with
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\mathrm{im}(\varphi({\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}))
. Every state
\mu\in L^{\geq 0}_{1}(\widetilde{\Lambda})\subset L^{\geq 0}_{1}(\Lambda)
then defines a state
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
on
{\mathcal{O}}
via Eq. (
6
). Finally, since
\epsilon
is faithful by assumption, for every
p,p^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
with
p\neq p^{\prime}
we can also find a state
\mu\in L_{1}(\widetilde{\Lambda})
such that
0\neq\mu(\varphi(p))\neq\mu(\varphi(p^{\prime}))\neq 0
, hence,
{\mathcal{O}}
is marginally KS noncontextual.

“
\Leftarrow
”: We need to show the existence of a classical embedding
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
. Since
{\mathcal{O}}
is marginally KS noncontextual, there exists a classical state
\gamma=(\gamma_{C})_{C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}}\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
, that is,
\gamma
admits a solution
\mu_{\gamma}\in L_{1}(\Lambda)
to the marginal problem defined by
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
(see Def.
15
) for some measurable space
\Lambda
, that is, for all
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
and
p\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{C}(p)=\int_{\Lambda}d\lambda\ \mu_{\gamma}(\lambda)\phi_{\gamma}(p)(\lambda)=\int_{\Lambda}d\lambda\ \mu_{\gamma}(\lambda)\chi(\lambda\mid p)=\int_{\Lambda_{p}}d\lambda\ \mu_{\rho}(\lambda)=\mu(\Lambda_{p})\;,

where
\phi_{\gamma}:{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}(\Lambda)
is an orthomorphism, which represents every event
p\in{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
by (the indicator function with respect to) the measurable subset
\Lambda_{p}\subset\Lambda
. Furthermore, by assumption we may assume that
0\neq\gamma(p)\neq\gamma(p^{\prime})\neq 0
for all
p,p^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
with
p\neq p^{\prime}
. Consequently,
\varphi(p)=\chi(\lambda\mid p)\neq\chi(\lambda\mid p^{\prime})=\varphi(p^{\prime})
, that is,
\varphi:{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}(\Lambda)
is faithful, hence, lifts to an embedding of observable algebras
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
by Lm.
1
.
∎

By Thm.
4
, Def.
18
translates Kochen-Specker noncontextuality into the language of the marginal approach, and thus completes the respective characterisation in Tab.
1
. Together with the preceding lemmata, it clarifies the subtle differences between similar notions in Def.
16
and Def.
17
, in particular, with regards to state-(in)dependence (see Tab.
4
). We will further discuss the notion of state-independent contextuality (SI-C) in Sec.
4.3
, specifically the distinction between SI-C sets and SI-C graphs.

{\mathcal{O}}
(finitely generated, separating)

state-independent

fully classically correlated (Def.
16
)

✓

(Prop.
2
)
\Downarrow

\not\Uparrow
(Prop.
1
)

KSNC (Def.
7
)
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{Thm}.~\ref{thm: KSNC = marginal KSNC}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}
marginal KSNC (Def.
18
)

✗

\Downarrow

\not\Uparrow
(Lm.
2
)

exhibits classical correlations (Def.
17
)

✗

violation of some NC inequality (
\neg
Def.
16
)

✗

(Prop.
2
)
\not\Downarrow

\Uparrow
(Prop.
1
)

KSC (
\neg
Def.
7
)
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{Thm}.~\ref{thm: KSNC = marginal KSNC}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}
marginal KSC (
\neg
Def.
18
)

✗

\not\Downarrow

\Uparrow
(Lm.
2
)

every state violates some of NC inequality (
\neg
Def.
17
)

✓

Table 4
:
Relation between KS noncontextuality (top) and KS contextuality (bottom) with closely related notions of contextuality in the marginal approach
[
40
]
. While both the nonexistence of violations of noncontextuality inequalities (Def.
16
) as well as the state-independent violation of some noncontextuality inequality (converse of Def.
17
) are inherently state-independent notions, the reformulation of both KS contextuality and KS noncontextuality in the marginal approach (see Def.
18
) are state-dependent.

Beyond its conceptual significance, Thm.
4
opens up the possibility to relate the algebraic and marginal approach to contextuality. Marginal noncontextuality is analysed in terms of noncontextuality inequalities (see Tab.
3
). As a consequence of Thm.
4
, these need to encode the triviality constraints in Thm.
1
. A careful analysis using context connections and context cycles thus seems a promising avenue to better characterise the noncontextuality polytope, which in general remains an open problem
[
40
]
.

3.3
Acyclic observable algebras and Vorob’ev theorem

In this section, we further expand on the comparison between the marginal and algebraic approach to Kochen-Specker contextuality. We begin in Sec.
3.3.1
by proving that for
acyclic observable algebras
(see Def.
20
) the triviality constraints in Thm.
1
can always be satisfied. In Sec.
3.3.2
, we further relate this property with Vorob’ev’s theorem
[
179
]
(see also Ref.
[
185
]
), which shows that acyclicity fully characterises classically correlated observable algebras (see Def.
16
). In Sec.
3.3.3
, we then turn to KS contextuality, specifically we make explicit that KS noncontextuality does not also imply acyclicity by proving that a single context cycle is never KS contextual - in stark contrast with the characterisation of the
n
-cycle contextuality scenario in the marginal approach
[
17
]
.

3.3.1
Trivial context cycles and Meyer-Clifton-Kent models

In this section, we will identify a property of (finitely generated) observable algebras and their partial orders of contexts under which the constraints in Thm.
1
are always satisfiable, hence, which implies KS noncontextuality. In doing so, we will obtain a new explanation for the KS noncontextuality of the models constructed in Ref.
[
136
,
117
,
61
]
.

Thm.
1
recasts KS noncontextuality as constraints on context connections along context cycles. It is thus instructive to study individual cycles first.

Definition 19
.

A maximal observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
with
\dim(\mathbb{I})=d
and maximal contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}=\{C_{0},\cdots,C_{n-1}\}
such that
C_{\mathbb{I}}\neq C_{i\cap j}:=C_{i}\cap C_{j}
if and only if
j=i-1,i,i+1
is called an
n
-cycle contextuality scenario in dimension
d
, and denoted by
{\mathcal{O}}={\mathcal{O}}(n,d)
.
41
41

41

Note that this implies
C_{ij}\cap C_{kl}=C_{\mathbb{I}}
unless
i=k
,
j=l
or
i=l
,
j=k
.

The terminology is clear:
{\mathcal{O}}(n,d)
contains a single non-trivial context cycle. Def.
19
has appeared before in Ref.
[
17
,
82
,
185
]
;
42
42

42

Cyclic scenarios of different types have also been studied in various other approaches
[
10
,
54
,
26
,
70
]
.
here, we adapted it to our notation. Note also that we do not assume that subcontexts
C_{i\cap j}
have isomorphic generating sets.

Truncated observable algebras.
We start with the following easy observation.

Lemma 3
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}(n,d)
be a
n
-cycle contextuality scenario in dimension
d
with non-trivial context cycle
(C_{0},\cdots,C_{n-1})
. If
C_{(i+1)i}=C_{i}\cap C_{i+1}=C_{\mathbb{I}}=\mathbb{R}\mathbb{I}
for at least one
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, then
{\mathcal{O}}(n,d)
is Kochen-Specker noncontextual.

Proof.

After possibly relabeling the indices in the context cycle, we may assume that
C_{0(n-1)}=C_{0}\cap C_{n-1}=C_{\mathbb{I}}
. Let
\mathfrak{l}
be any context connection on
{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}}(n,d))
and define
\pi:=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}l_{(i+1)i}\in\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{P}_{1}(C_{0}))\cong S_{d}
. Then
\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}
with
l^{\prime}_{(i+1)i}=l_{(i+1)i}
for all
i\in\{0,\cdots,n-2\}
and
l^{\prime}_{0(n-1)}=\pi^{-1}\circ l_{0(n-1)}
is also a context connection (it satisfies Eq. (
3
) in Def.
8
), for which Eq. (
4
) is clearly satisfied, hence,
{\mathcal{O}}(n,d)
is KS noncontextual by Thm.
1
.
∎

We call a context cycle
(C_{0},\cdots,C_{n-1})

trivial
if
C_{(i+1)i}=C_{\mathbb{I}}
for some
0\leq i\leq n-1
. Lm.
3
suggests to remove trivial context cycles from
{\mathcal{O}}
. We can do so by removing the least element
C_{\mathbb{I}}
from
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, that is, by removing (functions of) the trivial observable
\mathbb{I}
from
{\mathcal{O}}
. We denote by
{{\mathcal{O}}^{\circ}}={\mathcal{O}}/\{\lambda\mathbb{I}\mid\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\}
the resulting
truncated observable algebra
with
truncated partial order of contexts

{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\circ})}={{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}/\{C_{\mathbb{I}}\}
.

While Lm.
3
shows that a single trivial context cycle is always KS noncontextual, it does a priori not apply to several trivial cycles: the reduction from
{\mathcal{O}}
to
{\mathcal{O}}^{\circ}
results in fewer constraints in Eq. (
4
), in other words, the constraints between multiple trivial context cycles may be incompatible with Eq. (
4
). However, the following theorem asserts that for finitely generated observable algebras this is never the case.

Theorem 5
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a countably infinitely generated observable algebra with partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Then
{\mathcal{O}}
is Kochen-Specker noncontextual if and only if
{{\mathcal{O}}^{\circ}}
is.

Proof.

By Lm.
10
, it is sufficient to establish the claim for
{\mathcal{O}}
and
{{\mathcal{O}}^{\circ}}
maximal.

“
\Rightarrow
”: Since
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\circ})}\subset{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, this direction is obvious.

“
\Leftarrow
”: Consider the (finite) graph
G_{1}=(V,E_{1})
, whose set of vertices is given by
V={{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
and whose set of edges
E_{1}\subset V\times V
is given by
E_{1}=\{(C^{\prime},C)\mid C\cap C^{\prime}\neq C_{\mathbb{I}}\}
.
G_{1}
thus describes the context overlaps in
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\circ})}
.
43
43

43

Evidently, this graph differs from and should not be confused with the orthogonality graph associated to
{\mathcal{O}}
as defined in Def.
12
below.
Now, assume that
{{\mathcal{O}}^{\circ}}
is KS noncontextual, that is, by Thm.
1
there exists a flat context connection
\mathfrak{l}_{1}
on
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\circ})}
satisfying the triviality constraints in Eq. (
4
). Next, we successively add edges, forming new graphs
G_{m}=(V,E_{m})\mapsto G_{m+1}=(V,E_{m+1})
, until we obtain the fully-connected graph
G_{N}=(V,E_{N})
with
E_{N}=V\times V
, which corresponds to the partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Note that since vertices in
G_{m}
correspond to maximal contexts in
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
and edges to context overlaps, context cycles are represented by cycles in
G_{m}
.

Let
(C^{\prime},C)\in E_{m+1}/E_{m}
denote the edge added in the
m
-th inductive step. For any paths
44
44

44

A path
p
in
G=(V,E)
is any tuple
p=(v_{1},\cdots,v_{n})
of vertices
v_{i}\in V
,
i=\{1,\cdots,n\}
such that consecutive vertices are connected by an edge, that is,
(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in E
for all
i=\{1,\cdots,n-1\}
.

p_{C_{0}}^{C}=(C_{0},\cdots,C_{k}=C)
and
p_{C^{\prime}}^{C_{0}}=(C_{k+1}=C^{\prime},\cdots,C_{n-1},C_{0})
in
G_{m}
, this adds a new context cycle
\alpha=p_{C^{\prime}}^{C_{0}}\circ(C^{\prime},C)\circ p_{C_{0}}^{C}:=(C_{0},\cdots,C_{k}=C,C_{k+1}=C^{\prime},\cdots,C_{n-1})
to
G_{m+1}
, and thus a new triviality constraint in Eq. (
4
).
45
45

45

Here,
\circ
denotes concatenation of paths with the same end and start point, and such that
(\cdots,C_{i-1},C_{i},C_{i},C_{i+1},\cdots)=(\cdots,C_{i-1},C_{i},C_{i+1},\cdots)
.
Clearly, by setting
\mathfrak{l}({m+1})=\mathfrak{l}(m)\cup\mathfrak{l}_{C^{\prime}C}
with
\mathfrak{l}_{C^{\prime}C}=(\prod_{i=k}^{n-1}\mathfrak{l}_{(i+1)i}(m))^{-1}(\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}\mathfrak{l}_{(i+1)i}(m))^{-1}
we have
\circ_{i=0}^{n-1}l_{(i+1)i}(m+1)=\mathrm{id}
. Now, consider any other paths
q_{C_{0}}^{C}=(C_{0},D_{1},\cdots,D_{k-1},D_{k}=C)
and
q_{C^{\prime}}^{C_{0}}=(D_{k+1}=C^{\prime},D_{k+2}\cdots,D_{n-1},C_{0})
in
G_{m}
with the same end points as
p_{C_{0}}^{C}
and
p_{C^{\prime}}^{C_{0}}
, respectively. Then the context cycle
\beta=q_{C^{\prime}}^{C_{0}}\circ(C^{\prime},C)\circ q_{C_{0}}^{C}
can be written as

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=q_{C^{\prime}}^{C_{0}}\circ(C^{\prime},C)\circ q_{C_{0}}^{C}

\displaystyle=q_{C^{\prime}}^{C_{0}}\circ(p_{C_{0}}^{C^{\prime}}\circ p_{C^{\prime}}^{C_{0}})\circ(C^{\prime},C)\circ(p_{C_{0}}^{C}\circ p_{C}^{C_{0}})\circ q_{C_{0}}^{C}

\displaystyle=(q_{C^{\prime}}^{C_{0}}\circ p_{C_{0}}^{C^{\prime}})\circ\alpha\circ(p_{C}^{C_{0}}\circ q_{C_{0}}^{C})\;,

where e.g.
p_{C}^{C_{0}}=(p_{C_{0}}^{C})^{-1}=(C_{k}=C,C_{k-1},\cdots,C_{0})
denotes the context path
p_{C_{0}}^{C}
in reversed order such that
p_{C}^{C_{0}}\circ p_{C_{0}}^{C}=(C_{0})
, and similarly for the other paths.

Let
\Xi(G_{m})
denote the space of context connections restricted to context overlaps (between maximal contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
) given by
E_{m}
. Then,
\Xi(G_{1})=\Xi({{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\circ})})
and
\Xi(G_{N})=\Xi({{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})})
. By the inductive hypothesis, there exists
\mathfrak{l}(m)\in\Xi(G_{m})
satisfying the triviality constraints in Eq. (
4
), in particular, this applies to the context cycles
(q_{C^{\prime}}^{C_{0}}\circ p_{C_{0}}^{C^{\prime}})
and
(p_{C}^{C_{0}}\circ q_{C_{0}}^{C})
which lie in
G_{m}
. Moreover,
\mathfrak{l}(m+1)
satisfies Eq. (
4
) for
\alpha
(as shown above), and since
\mathfrak{l}(m+1)|_{G_{m}}=\mathfrak{l}(m)
by construction,
\mathfrak{l}(m+1)
thus satisfies the constraint in Eq. (
4
) for
\beta
, and thus for all context cycles
\gamma^{\prime}
in
G_{m+1}
(which contain
(C^{\prime},C)
). Finally, by induction we thus find a context connection
\mathfrak{l}(N)\in\Xi(G_{N})=\Xi({{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})})
such that Eq. (
4
) holds, hence, the result follows by Thm.
1
.
∎

Building on the characterisation of the classical embedding problem (defined in Eq. (
2
)) for observable algebras in terms of context connections on their partial order of contexts in Thm.
1
, Thm.
5
further simplifies the characterisation of Kochen-Specker noncontextuality for finitely generated observable algebras. (In Thm.
12
in App.
D.3
we prove additional reduction arguments of similar type, in particular, we will show that the representation in terms of measurement scenarios, compatibility graphs etc is generally redundant.) In what follows, we discuss two immediate consequences of Thm.
5
.

Acyclic observable algebras.
Thm.
5
motivates the following definition.

Definition 20

(acyclic observable algebras)
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an observable algebra with partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
.
{\mathcal{O}}
is called
acyclic
if
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
contains only trivial context cycles.

Note that acyclicity of
{\mathcal{O}}
is a property of its partial order of contexts, and we thus also call
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}

acyclic
if
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\circ})}={{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}/\{C_{\mathbb{I}}\}
. Thm.
5
immediately implies the following.

Corollary 1
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a countably infinitely generated acyclic observable algebra. Then
{\mathcal{O}}
is Kochen-Specker noncontextual.

Proof.

If
{\mathcal{O}}
is acyclic, then there are no context cycles in
{\mathcal{O}}^{\circ}
, hence, Eq. (
4
) in Thm.
1
poses no constraints and
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\circ})}
is trivially Kochen-Specker noncontextual. But then
{\mathcal{O}}
is also Kochen-Specker noncontextual by Thm.
5
.
∎

The importance of cyclicity in proofs of the Kochen-Specker theorem has been recognised before. Indeed, many proofs explicitly exhibit a “cyclic nature” in terms of their violation of the triviality constraints in Eq. (
4
)
[
134
,
135
,
118
,
119
,
48
]
. By Thm.
1
, this is necessarily the case, and Cor.
1
further distills this cyclic nature of KS contextuality.

Meyer-Clifton-Kent (MCK) models.
It has been suggested that by restricting the set of quantum observables one may avoid the interpretational implications of the Kochen-Specker theorem
[
152
,
153
]
. Motivated by the fact that two quantum observables, whose difference has infinitesimal operator norm, are experimentally indistinguishable, various restrictions to observable algebras
{\mathcal{O}}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
that are dense in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
have been discussed. In Ref.
[
136
,
117
]
, it was first shown that unlike rays in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, rays in
\mathbb{Q}^{d}
admit valuations (“truth functions”), that is, KS-colourings (see Def.
10
). While this is not sufficient to bypass the Kochen-Specker theorem (see Sec.
2.4
), the observable subalgebras
{\mathcal{O}}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
constructed in Ref.
[
61
]
admit a “full collection of truth functions”, that is, are
d
-colourable and thus indeed Kochen-Specker noncontextual by Thm.
2
. With Cor.
1
, we can give a new proof and structural explanation of this fact.

Corollary 2
.

The maximal observable algebras
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{MCK}}
in Ref.
[
61
]
are Kochen-Specker noncontextual.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{MCK}}
denote the maximal observable algebras generated by the projections in Thm. 2.2 in Ref.
[
61
]
. By construction, any two non-identical maximal contexts
C,C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{MCK}})
,
C\neq C^{\prime}
(equivalently, resolutions of the identity) overlap trivially
C\cap C^{\prime}=\emptyset
. Consequently,
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{MCK}}
is acyclic and the result follows from Cor.
1
.
∎

Acyclicity has also been studied in the marginal approach, where it characterises classically correlated observable algebras (see Def.
16
) by Vorob’ev’s theorem
[
179
]
. In the next section, we will review Vorob’ev’s theorem, compare it with Cor.
1
and find that the characterisation of Kochen-Specker noncontextuality is more complex.

3.3.2
Characterisation of classically correlated observable algebras

The problem of characterising all finitely generated observable algebras
{\mathcal{O}}
with
\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]=\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
(see Def.
16
) has a simple answer and was first obtained by Vorob’ev
[
179
]
. Here, we will phrase it in the setting of observable algebras. To this end, we will relate it with the formulation in Ref.
[
185
]
- in terms of the compatibility graphs
\mathcal{G}(X)
of measurement scenarios
M=(X,\mathcal{M})
, which we interpret as generating sets
X=\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}})
of their uniquely associated observable algebras
{\mathcal{O}}
by Lm.
12
. In particular, without loss of generality we will choose
\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}})={\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
, interpreted as elementary “yes-no” observables. For a detailed comparison between the respective notions, see App.
D
.

Lemma 4
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a finitely generated observable algebra with partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, event algebra
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Then
{\mathcal{O}}
is acyclic if and only if
\mathcal{G}({\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})})
is chordal.
46
46

46

A graph
G=(V,E)
is
chordal
if for any cycle
(v_{0},\cdots,v_{n-1},v_{0})
with
v_{i}\in V
for all
0\leq i\leq n-1
and
n\geq 4
there exists an edge connecting non-adjacent vertices in the cycle.

Proof.

“
\Leftarrow
”: Assume that
{\mathcal{O}}
is not acyclic and let
(C_{0},\cdots,C_{n-1})
be a non-trivial context cycle in
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
of minimal length, in particular,
C_{i}\neq C_{j}
for
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
,
i\neq j
. For
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, denote by
p_{(i+1)i}
the maximal projection of the subcontexts
C_{(i+1)i}=C_{(i+1)}\cap C_{i}
, then

\displaystyle C(p_{(i+1)i},\mathbb{I}):=\mathbb{R}p_{(i+1)i}+\mathbb{R}(\mathbb{I}-p_{(i+1)i})\subset C_{(i+1)i}\;.

Since therefore
p_{(i+1)i},p_{i(i-1)}\in\mathcal{P}(C_{i})
, the corresponding dichotomoic observables are connected by an edge in the compatibility graph
\mathcal{G}({\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})})
. It follows that
(p_{(i+1)i})_{i=0}^{n-1}
defines a cycle in
\mathcal{G}({\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})})
which is non-chordal. Indeed, if it was chordal, there would exist
j\notin\{i-1,i,i+1\}
such that
p_{(i+1)i}\in C_{j}
, hence,
C_{i}\cap C_{j}\neq C_{\mathbb{I}}
contradicting that
(C_{0},\cdots,C_{n-1})
is a minimal non-trivial context cycle.

“
\Rightarrow
”: Conversely, assume that
\mathcal{G}({\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})})
is non-chordal and let
(p_{10},\cdots,p_{0(n-1)})
be a minimal non-chordal cycle in
\mathcal{G}({\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})})
, that is, (the dichotomic observables defined by)
p_{ji}
and
p_{kl}
are connected by an edge in
\mathcal{G}({\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})})
if and only if
i=k
or
j=l
. Define
C_{(i+1)i}:=C(p_{(i+1)i},\mathbb{I})=\mathbb{R}p_{(i+1)i}+\mathbb{R}(\mathbb{I}-p_{(i+1)i})
and let
C_{i}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
be maximal contexts such that
C_{(i+1)i}=C_{(i+1)}\cap C_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
. Then we have
C_{ij}=C_{i}\cap C_{j}=C_{\mathbb{I}}=C(\mathbb{I})
for
j\notin\{i-1,i,i+1\}
, since otherwise there would exist a smaller cycle in
\mathcal{G}({\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})})
contradicting minimality of
(p_{01},\cdots,p_{(n-1)0})
. It follows that
(C_{0},\cdots,C_{n-1})
is a non-trivial context cycle in
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, hence,
{\mathcal{O}}
is acyclic.
∎

Theorem 6

(Vorob’ev theorem
[
179
,
185
]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a finitely generated observable algebra with partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Then
\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]=\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
if and only if
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
is acyclic.

Proof.

For any choice of generating set
\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}})
, we obtain a measurement scenario
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}}))
by Def.
34
. Clearly, if
\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}})={\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
is chordal then the same holds for any other choice of generating set, hence,
\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}})
is chordal if and only if
{\mathcal{O}}
is acyclic by Lm.
4
. By the main result in Ref.
[
185
]
it follows that
\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]=\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
.

Conversely, if
\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]=\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
then the compatibility graph
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}}))
of any measurement scenario corresponding to a generating set
\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}})
of
{\mathcal{O}}
(via Lm.
12
) is chordal by the main theorem in Ref.
[
185
]
. Hence,
{\mathcal{O}}
is acyclic by Lm.
4
. (For more details on the relation between measurement scenarios and observable algebras, see App.
D.1
.)
∎

Crucially, the statement in Thm.
6
is a property of the partial order of contexts of an observable algebra only. As such it is similar to the characterisation of Kochen-Specker noncontextuality in terms of context connections in Thm.
1
. However, by Prop.
1
the respective notions of contextuality in Def.
16
and Def.
7
differ. Since an acyclic observable algebra is also Kochen-Specker noncontextual by Cor.
1
, it follows that acyclicity cannot also be sufficient for KS noncontextuality, that is, the converse of Cor.
1
must fail.

3.3.3
Kochen-Specker contextuality in single context cycles

Thm.
1
casts the functional constraints in Eq. (
2
) as triviality constraints for context connections. This motivates the question whether a single (non-trivial) context cycle can already be sufficient to detect KS contextuality.

Recall that Ref.
[
17
]
characterises the
n
-cycle contextuality scenario in dimension
d
in Def.
19
from the perspective of the marginal approach and Def.
16
. There, it is shown that
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{\mathcal{O}}(n,d)]\subsetneq\Gamma_{\mathrm{qm}}[{\mathcal{O}}(n,d)]\subsetneq\Gamma_{\mathrm{nd}}[{\mathcal{O}}(n,d)]
(see Eq. (
7
)), in particular, there exist quantum correlations that violate some of the noncontextuality inequalities which define the noncontextuality polytope
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{\mathcal{O}}(n,d)]
. What is more, a closer look at the main result in Ref.
[
185
]
reveals that the direction “fully classically correlated
\Rightarrow
acyclic” in Thm.
6
follows from the existence of beyond classical correlations in the
n
-cycle contextuality scenario. Since the other direction holds for both notions of contextuality, in particular, “acyclic
\Rightarrow
KS noncontextual” by Cor.
1
, and since both notions are distinct, the converse of Cor.
1
must fail in general. To see this explicitly, we first discuss a well-known example in four-dimensional quantum theory, namely, we construct a classical embedding of the observable algebra corresponding to the CHSH scenario
[
59
]
.

Example 1

(Classical embedding for the CHSH scenario)
.

Let
\Lambda=\Lambda_{A}\times\Lambda_{B}=\{\lambda_{ij}\}_{i,j=-2}^{2}
denote a set consisting of
16
elements, corresponding to the product of two “elementary systems” with
\Lambda_{A}=\{\lambda^{A}_{i}\}_{i=-2}^{2}
and
\Lambda_{B}=\{\lambda^{B}_{j}\}_{j=-2}^{2}
. Measurements are represented by certain sub-partitions of
\Lambda
.
47
47

47

This construction is reminiscent of Spekkens’ toy theory
[
173
]
, where measurements further satisfy the “knowledge-balance principle”, by which a “maximally informative measurement” yields as much knowledge about
\Lambda
as one lacks, together with consistency under repeated measurements and state-update rule (for details, see Ref.
[
173
]
). These additional constraints result in the fact that measurements generally do not commute, and are thus in conflict with a classical embedding of the form in Eq. (
2
). Here, we do not impose the “knowledge-balance principle”, and instead simply consider the usual operations on (measurable) functions in
L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
.
Maximally informative measurements partition the joint system into 4 subsets containing 4 “ontic” states each. Non-maximally informative measurements arise by coarse-graining of maximal ones, where coarse-graining obtains the natural meaning on
\Lambda
(see Fig.
4
).

The observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{CHSH}}
underlying the CHSH scenario
[
59
]
is generated by four product measurements
S_{A}\otimes S_{B}
,
S_{A}\otimes S^{\prime}_{B}
,
S^{\prime}_{A}\otimes S_{B}
and
S^{\prime}_{A}\otimes S^{\prime}_{B}
on a pair of spin-
\frac{1}{2}
systems, corresponding to two choices of measurements for Alice and Bob. Together, these generate four maximal contexts with subcontexts given by the coarse-grained observables
S_{A}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{B},S^{\prime}_{A}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{B}
and
\mathbb{I}_{A}\otimes S_{B},\mathbb{I}_{A}\otimes S^{\prime}_{B}
and trivial context generated by
\mathbb{I}=\mathbb{I}_{A}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{B}
.

We represent Alice’s local spin-
\frac{1}{2}
observables as (measurable) functions on
\Lambda_{A}
via

\displaystyle f_{S_{A}}

\displaystyle:=\{\{\lambda^{A}_{-2},\lambda^{A}_{-1}\}\rightarrow\bar{1},\ \{\lambda^{A}_{1},\lambda^{A}_{2}\}\rightarrow 1\},

\displaystyle f_{S^{\prime}_{A}}

\displaystyle:=\{\{\lambda^{A}_{-2},\lambda^{A}_{2}\}\rightarrow\bar{1},\ \{\lambda^{A}_{-1},\lambda^{A}_{1}\}\rightarrow 1\},

\displaystyle f_{\mathbb{I}_{A}}

\displaystyle:=\{\{\Lambda_{A}\rightarrow 1\}\;,

and similarly for Bob. Product observables are represented via
f_{S_{A}S_{B}}=f_{S_{A}}\times f_{S_{B}}
, e.g.

\displaystyle f_{S_{A}S_{B}}

\displaystyle:=\{\{\lambda^{A}_{-2},\lambda^{A}_{-1}\}\times\{\lambda^{B}_{-2},\lambda^{B}_{-1}\}\rightarrow 1,\ \{\lambda^{A}_{-2},\lambda^{A}_{-1}\}\times\{\lambda^{B}_{1},\lambda^{B}_{2}\}\rightarrow\bar{1},

\displaystyle\quad\ \ \{\lambda^{A}_{1},\lambda^{A}_{2}\}\times\{\lambda^{B}_{-2},\lambda^{B}_{-1}\}\rightarrow\bar{1},\ \{\lambda^{A}_{1},\lambda^{A}_{2}\}\times\{\lambda^{B}_{1},\lambda^{B}_{2}\}\rightarrow 1\}\;.

These functions together with their coarse-graining relations are shown in Fig.
4
(a).

The map
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{CHSH}}\rightarrow L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
sending the generating observables in
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{CHSH}}
to (measurable) functions above thus defines a classical embedding for the CHSH scenario. Clearly, we have
\emptyset\neq\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{CHSH}}]
; moreover,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{CHSH}}]\subsetneq\Gamma[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{CHSH}}]
since the CHSH scenario allows for entanglement. Explicitly, the marginals arising from a Bell state, e.g.
|\psi\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|00\rangle+|11\rangle)
which results in the (maximal) violation of the CHSH inequality in quantum theory
[
59
,
176
]
, is not a valid probability distribution in
L^{\geq 0}_{1}(\Lambda)
.
48
48

48

This should not be confused with the representation of Bell entangled states as epistemic states on
\Lambda
in Spekkens’ toy theory, which rely on a different (to the classical Bayesian) state-update rule defined in line with the “knowledge-balance principle”
[
173
]
.
Indeed, denoting the outcomes of the respective observables by lower case letters, the events defined by
s_{A}s_{B}=1
,
s_{A}s^{\prime}_{B}=1
,
s^{\prime}_{A}s_{B}=1
and
s^{\prime}_{A}s^{\prime}_{B}=\bar{1}
are shown in Fig.

(b), together with the corresponding marginals of
\gamma_{|\psi\rangle}\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{qu}}[{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{CHSH}})]
, which maximises the (quantum-mechanical) expectation value
\mathbb{E}_{|\psi\rangle}(S_{A}S_{B}+S_{A}S^{\prime}_{B}+S^{\prime}_{A}S_{B}-S^{\prime}_{A}S^{\prime}_{B})=2\sqrt{2}

[

,
176
]
.

Note that the maximal violation of the CHSH inequality for general observable algebras (hence, for general probabilistic theories) even exceeds the (quantum) Tsirelson bound
[

,

]
.
Assuming that
\gamma_{|\psi\rangle}\in L_{1}(\Lambda)
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\gamma_{|\psi\rangle}(\lambda)+2\sum_{\lambda\in S\subset\Lambda}\gamma_{|\psi\rangle}(\lambda)=4\cos^{2}\left(\frac{\pi}{8}\right)>3\;.

Yet, since
\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\mu(\lambda)=1
, hence,
\sum_{\lambda\in S\subset\Lambda}\mu(\lambda)<1
for every classical state

\mu>0

\gamma_{|\psi\rangle}\not>0
, hence,
\gamma_{|\psi\rangle}\notin\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{CHSH}})]
.

The marginals

of a (Bell-entangled) quantum state
\gamma_{|\psi\rangle}\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{qu}}[{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{CHSH}})]
which maximally violates the CHSH inequality
[

in quantum mechanics (similarly, for the “Popescu-Rohrlich” box

with

The CHSH scenario
[
