]
admits a classical embedding (a), hence, is Kochen-Specker noncontextual, while also exhibiting nonclassical correlations e.g. in the form of entangled states and more general non-signalling correlations (b).

Ex.
1
shows that a proof of Kochen-Specker contextuality must generally involve constraints encoded in several non-trivial context cycles. Our next theorem asserts that this is always the case in quantum-mechanical systems of dimension three.

Theorem 7
.

Let
(C_{0},\cdots,C_{n-1})
be a context cycle in
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\mathcal{H})
with
\dim(\mathcal{H})=3
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there exist unitaries
(U_{(i+1)i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
,
U_{(i+1)i}\in{\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H})}
such that
C_{i+1}=U_{(i+1)i}.C_{i}:=U_{(i+1)i}C_{i}U^{*}_{(i+1)i}
and
C_{(i+1)i}=U_{(i+1)i}.C_{(i+1)i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, and such that

\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}(U_{(i+1)i})={\mathrm{id}}\;.

(8)

In particular, there exists a flat context connection on
{\mathcal{C}}(n,d)
, defined by
(C_{0},\cdots,C_{n-1})
.

Proof.

We first proceed by induction in the cycle length
n
to show that we can reduce to minimal non-trivial context cycles. To this end, we consider the following two cases.

(i)

First, let
C_{i}=C_{j}
for some
i\neq j
in the context cycle
(C_{0},\cdots,C_{n-1})
, then by the inductive hypothesis there exist unitaries
(U_{(i+1)i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
for the context cycles
(C_{i},\cdots,C_{j-1})
and
(C_{j},\cdots,C_{i-1})
such that
\prod_{k=i}^{j-1}\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}(U_{(k+1)k})={\mathrm{id}}=\prod_{k=j}^{i-1}\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}(U_{(k+1)k})
. Consequently, we also have
\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}(U_{(k+1)k})={\mathrm{id}}
.

We may thus assume that
C_{i}\neq C_{j}
for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{n},i\neq j
.

(ii)

Second, let
C_{(i+1)i}=C_{i(i-1)}
for some
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
. Since
d=3
, this implies
C_{(i+1)(i-1)}=C_{(i+1)i}=C_{i(i-1)}
. Indeed, there exists
p\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(C_{(i+1)i}),\mathcal{P}_{1}(C_{i(i-1)})
from which
p\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(C_{i+1}),\mathcal{P}_{1}(C_{i-1})
, and, since
C_{i+1}\neq C_{i-1}
by (i),
C_{(i+1)(i-1)}\subsetneq C_{(i+1)},C_{(i-1)}
.

W.l.o.g., let
i=n-1
and consider the (nontrivial) context cycle
(C_{0},\cdots,C_{n-2})
. Since this cycle has length
n-1
, by the inductive hypothesis, there exist unitaries
U_{(i+1)i}\in{\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H})}
,
C_{(i+1)}=U_{(i+1)i}.C_{i}
,
C_{(i+1)i}=U_{(i+1)i}.C_{(i+1)i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n-1}
and such that
\prod_{i=0}^{n-2}\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}(U_{(i+1)i})={\mathrm{id}}
. Since
U_{0(n-2)}
stabilises
C_{0(n-2)}=C_{0(n-1)}=C_{(n-1)(n-2)}
we can decompose (the non-trivial action on the two-dimensional subspace corresponding to
1-p
as)
U_{0(n-2)}=U_{0(n-1)}U_{(n-1)(n-2)}
into unitaries
U_{0(n-1)}
and
U_{(n-1)(n-2)}
such that both
C_{0(n-1)}=U_{0(n-1)}.C_{0(n-1)}
and
C_{(n-1)(n-2)}=U_{(n-1)(n-2)}.C_{(n-1)(n-2)}
, as well as
C_{0}=U_{0(n-1)}.C_{n-1}
and
C_{n-1}=U_{(n-1)(n-2)}.C_{n-2}
hold. Clearly, we then have

\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}(U_{(i+1)i})=\left(\prod_{i=0}^{n-2}\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}(U_{(i+1)i})\right)={\mathrm{id}}\;.

Now, let
({\tilde{U}}_{(i+1)i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
,
{\tilde{U}}_{(i+1)i}\in{\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H})}
be unitaries such that
\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}({\tilde{U}}_{(i+1)i})=\pi\in\mathrm{Sym}(C_{0})\cong S_{3}
, while also satisfying the constraints
C_{i+1}={\tilde{U}}_{(i+1)i}.C_{i}
and
C_{(i+1)i}={\tilde{U}}_{(i+1)i}.C_{(i+1)i}
. Note that the latter two constraints define
{\tilde{U}}_{(i+1)i}
only up to permutations
\pi_{(i+1)i}\in{\mathrm{Stab}}_{\mathrm{Sym}(C_{i})}(C_{(i+1)i})
and
\pi_{i(i+1)}\in{\mathrm{Stab}}_{\mathrm{Sym}(C_{i+1})}(C_{(i+1)i})
, that is,

\displaystyle U_{(i+1)i}=\pi_{i(i+1)}{\tilde{U}}_{(i+1)i}\pi_{(i+1)i}

(9)

satisfies the same constraints as
{\tilde{U}}_{(i+1)i}
.
50
50

50

Note also that the action of
U_{(i+1)i}
between contexts
C_{i}
and
C_{(i+1)}
is left invariant under a transformation
U_{(i+1)i}\rightarrow O_{{(i+1)}}U_{(i+1)i}O_{i}
for any operator
O_{i}\in C_{i}
and
O_{(i+1)}\in C_{(i+1)}
.
We will prove the existence of a set of unitaries
(U_{(i+1)i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
satisfying Eq. (
8
) by choosing appropriate permutations as above.

By (i) and (ii), we may assume that
C_{(i+1)i}\neq C_{i(i-1)}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
. By Thm.
5
, we may further assume that
C_{(i+1)i},C_{i(i-1)}\neq\mathbb{C}\mathbbm{1}
. In other words,
(C_{0},\cdots,C_{n-1})
defines a
n
-cycle contextuality scenario in dimension
d=3
(see Def.
19
), hence,
C_{(i+1)i}
and
C_{i(i-1)}
are generated by different projections in
\mathcal{P}_{1}(C_{i})
(together with the identity
\mathbbm{1}
) for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
. It follows that the permutations
\pi_{(i+1)i}
and
\pi_{(i-1)i}
in Eq. (
9
) (which stabilise
C_{(i+1)i}
and
C_{i(i-1)}
, respectively) correspond to distinct transpositions in
\mathrm{Sym}(C_{i})
.

Fix a maximal context
C_{0}
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\pi_{10}={\mathrm{id}},\tau_{12}\in\mathrm{Sym}(C_{0})
and
\pi_{(n-1)0}={\mathrm{id}},\tau_{23}\in\mathrm{Sym}(C_{0})
.
51
51

51

In our notation we distinguish between generic permutations
\pi\in\mathrm{Sym}(C_{i})
and explicit elements
\sigma_{\pi(1,\cdots,n)}
; we also write
\tau_{ij}
for the permutation corresponding to a transposition of elements
i,j\in[n]
.
If
\pi\in\Pi_{1}=\{{\mathrm{id}},\tau_{12},\tau_{23},\tau_{12}\tau_{23}=\sigma_{312}\}
, Eq. (
8
) is thus satisfied for appropriate choices of
\pi_{10}
in
U_{10}={\tilde{U}}_{10}\pi_{10}
and
\pi_{(n-1)0}
in
U_{0(n-1)}=\pi_{(n-1)0}{\tilde{U}}_{0(n-1)}
. For
\pi\notin\Pi_{1}
, we further consider a change of unitary
U_{21}={\tilde{U}}_{21}\pi_{21}
, where
\pi_{21}\in{\mathrm{Stab}}_{\mathrm{Sym}(C_{1})}(C_{21})
, which corresponds to a permutation
{\widetilde{\pi}}_{21}=U^{-1}_{10}\pi_{21}U_{10}\in\mathrm{Sym}(C_{0})
. By the same argument as above,
C_{21}
and
C_{10}
are generated by different projections in
\mathcal{P}_{1}(C_{1})
, hence,
\pi_{21}
and
\pi_{01}
induce different transpositions in
\mathrm{Sym}(C_{1})
, and thus also in
\mathrm{Sym}(C_{0})
under conjugation by
U^{-1}_{10}
. For this induced symmetry in
\mathrm{Sym}(C_{0})
, we therefore either have
{\widetilde{\pi}}_{21}={\mathrm{id}},\tau_{23}
or
{\widetilde{\pi}}_{21}={\mathrm{id}},\tau_{13}
. The unitaries in the first case generate any permutation in

\Pi_{1}\cup\{\tau_{23}\tau_{12}=\sigma_{231}=\sigma^{2}_{312},\tau_{23}\tau_{12}\tau_{23}=\tau_{13}\}=S_{3}\;.

Similarly, the unitaries in the second case generate any permutation in

\Pi_{1}\cup\{\tau_{13},\tau_{13}\tau_{23}=\sigma_{231}=\sigma^{2}_{312}\}=S_{3}\;.

Hence, for any
\pi=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}({\tilde{U}}_{(i+1)i})\in\mathrm{Sym}(C_{0})\cong S_{3}
, we can find permutations
{\widetilde{\pi}}_{21},\pi_{10},\pi_{0(n-1)}\in\mathrm{Sym}(C_{0})
(together with
\pi_{(k+1)k}={\mathrm{id}}
for all
k\neq 1,0,n-1
) such that the unitaries
(U_{(i+1)i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
,
U_{(i+1)i}={\tilde{U}}_{(i+1)i}\pi_{(i+1)i}
satisfy Eq. (
8
). Finally,
(U_{(i+1)i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
defines a context connection
\mathfrak{l}
by setting
l_{(i+1)i}(p)=U_{(i+1)i}.p
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
(and
l_{ij}
arbitrary for
j\neq i\pm 1
), which satisfies Eq. (
4
) as a consequence of Eq. (
8
).
∎

Thm.
7
should be contrasted with the study of the
n
-cycle scenario in Ref.
[
17
,
185
]
, which instead of Kochen-Specker noncontextual observable algebras is concerned with fully classically correlated observable algebras (see Def.
16
). As we have seen, the latter are characterised in terms of acyclic context categories by Vorob’ev’s theorem (Thm.
6
). Moreover, acyclicity implies KS noncontextuality by Cor.
1
. Yet, the converse does not hold, and thus allows for “hybrid” scenarios such as the CHSH scenario in Ex.
1
which exhibits both classical and quantum features (cf. Ref.
[
173
]
). This shows that the characterisation of Kochen-Specker contextuality is more complex than that of Def.
16
:
it requires not just a single context cycle, but constraints arranged along several context cycles
, e.g. as explicated by the topological arrangements in Ref.
[
146
,
147
,
144
,
18
]
.

It would be interesting to see whether Thm.
7
generalises to arbitrary context cycles in higher dimensions. A more ambitious objective is to characterise quantum within more general observable algebras in terms of the constraints on context connections it gives rise to. Such would amount to a geometric characterisation and explanation of Kochen-Specker contextuality in quantum theory.

To (efficiently) characterise and identify Kochen-Specker noncontextual observable algebras remains an open problem - one that is not only of foundational, but also of practical interest. Its foundational importance mainly stems from clarifying the classical-quantum divide, specifically with respect to the emergence of classicality. From a practical perspective, contextuality has been identified as the main resource in quantum computation
[
162
,
107
,
80
,
81
,
37
]
. Characterising observable subalgebras
{\mathcal{O}}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
in quantum theory in terms of KS contextuality thus presents an important task towards provable quantum advantage. We believe that the formalism presented here and the new tools it introduces will prove useful towards both of these goals.

In the next section, we collect some further evidence in support of this claim. More precisely, by comparing our formalism with the graph-theoretic approach to contextuality
[
52
]
, we will sharpen the (formal) characterisation of KS contextuality in terms of a colouring problem in Thm.
2
and thereby generalise various known results.

4
Algebraic vs graph-theoretic approach

In this section, we compare the notion of Kochen-Specker contextuality in Def.
7
and its characterisation in terms of the triviality constraints in Thm.
1
, specifically, in terms of the colouring problem in Thm.
2
and Thm.
3
, with various notions of contextuality for orthogonality graphs in the graph-theoretic approach
[
52
,
160
,
51
,
12
]
.

We start by reviewing the relevant properties of orthogonality graphs in Sec.
4.1
. In Sec.
4.2
, we translate the notion of Kochen-Specker contextuality for observable algebras to an equivalent notion applicable to orthogonality graphs, which is similar the notion of marginal Kochen-Specker contextuality in Def.
18
, and restricted to cases where both notions are directly comparable. However, as we show in App.
D.2
this is generally not the case. Yet, under appropriate assumptions on the realisation of an orthogonality graph, in Sec.
4.3
, we relate our results with known criteria for SI-C graphs and SI-C sets, which are expressed in terms of their chromatic number
[
46
,
160
,
51
]
. In doing so, we will further answer an open question in Ref.
[
160
]

4.1
Basic elements of the graph-theoretic approach

The graph-theoretic approach was initiated in Ref.
[
57
,
82
,
52
]
, and has proven a successful tool in the study of contextuality. We refer to Ref.
[
52
,
11
,
12
]
for a detailed exposition, and restrict ourselves to the very basics needed for our purposes.

To begin with, recall that an (undirected) graph
G=(V,E)
is specified by the set of its vertices
V
and edges
E\subset V\times V
. Two vertices that are joined by an edge are called
adjacent
. We will restrict to finite graphs for which
V=\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{n}\}
(and thus also
E
) are finite sets. A subset
I\subset V
is called an
independent set
if
(v,v^{\prime})\notin E
for all
v,v^{\prime}\in I
. In contrast, a
clique
\mathrm{C}
of
G
is a fully connected subgraph of
G
.
52
52

52

We will distinguish cliques in the graph-theoretic approach from contexts (commutative algebras in observable algebras) by using the non-italic font for the former.
We denote by
\omega(G)=\max_{\mathrm{C}\subset G}|\mathrm{C}|
the cardinality of the largest clique in
G
. Finally, the
chromatic number
\chi(G)
of
G
is the minimal number of colours needed to colour the vertices of
G
such that no two adjacent vertices are assigned the same colour.

Orthogonality graphs and their realisations.
The essential idea behind the graph-theoretic approach is to reduce the study of contextuality to the bare essentials needed to describe the constraints on correlations.

Definition 21

(orthogonality graph)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}\subset{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
be a finite set of minimal projections on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
,
\dim(\mathcal{H})<\infty
. The
orthogonality graph
G(\mathcal{P})=(V,E)
of
\mathcal{P}
has vertices
V=\mathcal{P}
, and edges
(p,p^{\prime})\in E\subset\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}
if and only if
pp^{\prime}=0
.

Note first that since every non-minimal projection
p\in{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
can be decomposed into finitely many orthogonal projections (and since
p
inherits its orthogonality relations from the elements in this decomposition), for observable subalgebras of quantum theory
{\mathcal{O}}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
it is sufficient to restrict to the orthogonality graph of its minimal projections.

The properties of projections impose some conditions on the types of graphs that can arise as orthogonality graphs. For instance, since
p^{2}=p\neq 0
(unless
p
is the zero projection), an orthogonality graph contains no self-loops. Moreover, there exists at most one edge between two nodes, that is, an orthogonality graph contains no multi-edges. Graphs without loops and multi-edges are called
simple
. One may ask whether every finite, simple graph
G
can be realised in terms of projections on some Hilbert space. Without further constraints, e.g. on the dimension of the Hilbert space, this is indeed the case
[
101
]
.
53
53

53

We will come across various more restrictive notions of (quantum) realisations throughout this section. We list these in App.
E
; to the best of our knowledge their solutions remain by and large open.
It follows that the restriction in Def.
21
can be relaxed to the above general constraints on graphs. In particular, every finite, simple
exclusivity graph
, whose vertices represent events and whose edges relate events if they are exclusive
[
83
,
52
]
, can be interpreted as an orthogonality graph.
54
54

54

Another closely related concept is that of a compatibility graph (see also App.
D.1
), which instead relates events if they are compatible. In the case of ideal observables (specifically, for elementary events), as discussed here, the respective notions are easily seen to encode the same information.

Yet, reducing the study of contextuality to orthogonality graphs raises a new question: which observable algebras are (faithfully) represented by orthogonality graphs? Recall that by Lm.
1
, event algebras capture the full structure of observable algebras. Yet only for partial algebras, that is, under the assumption of Specker’s principle (or local orthogonality, see Ref.
[
57
,
82
,
11
]
) can the necessary structure be reduced to that of minimal projections. Throughout this section, we will therefore assume observable algebras to be (finite-dimensional and finitely generated) partial algebras.

Definition 22

(realisation)
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be an orthogonality graph and
{\mathcal{O}}
a partial algebra. A
realisation of
G
in
{\mathcal{O}}
is a map
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
such that
\pi(v)\pi(v^{\prime})=0
if and only if
(v,v^{\prime})\in E
. A
quantum realisation of
G
is a realisation with
{\mathcal{O}}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
.

Often quantum realisations are restricted to rank-
1
projections
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{H})}
, which can equivalently be thought of as maps
\pi:G\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{d}
for
\dim(\mathcal{H})=d
. We will generally not make this restriction, yet unlike some authors we will mostly restrict realisations to be
constraint-preserving
, for which
\pi(v)\pi(v^{\prime})=0
implies
(v,v^{\prime})\in E
(see Tab.
5
).
55
55

55

Other authors define realisations to be neither faithful nor constraint-preserving. As our main interest in orthogonality graphs stems from the characterisation of SI-C sets and SI-C graphs in Sec.
4.3
, here we follow the convention in Ref.
[
160
]
.

unital

faithful

constraint-preserving

realisation

✗

✓

✓

orthomorphism

✓

✗

✗

embedding

✓

✓

✗

Table 5
:
Comparison between morphisms of orthogonality graphs, event and observable algebras. Realisations are both faithful and constraint-preserving, yet are generally not unital. In contrast, classical embeddings, equivalently faithful orthomorphisms (in terms of functors
\eta
in Lm.
1
, this means faithful on objects) are not necessarily constraint-preserving, that is,
\epsilon(p)\epsilon(p^{\prime})=0
only if
pp^{\prime}=0
(in terms of functors
\eta
in Lm.
1
, this means faithful on morphisms).

Observe that orthogonality graphs
G=G({\mathcal{O}})=G({\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}})})
associated with a partial algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
, by definition, admit a realisation where maximal cliques correspond with resolutions of the identity
\mathbb{I}
in
{\mathcal{O}}
. In other words, maximal cliques in such orthogonality graphs are non-trivially related. It is thus natural to constrain the notion of realisation for orthogonality graphs
G=G({\mathcal{O}})
such that this additional structure is preserved.

Definition 23

(unital realisation)
.

Let
G
be an orthogonality graph. A realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
is called
unital
if for every maximal clique
\mathrm{C}\subset G
it holds
\sum_{v\in\mathrm{C}}\pi(v)=\mathbb{I}
.

Clearly, not every realisation is unital. While every orthogonality graph has a quantum realisation, it remains an open problem which orthogonality graphs admit a unital quantum realisation
[
101
]
(see also App.
E
). Consequently, upon restriction of orthogonality graphs to (those associated with) partial algebras, we need to add additional information in the form of the identity element (see also App.
D.2
).
56
56

56

In Sec.
4.3
below, we discuss this in more detail by introducing a notion of faithful completion (see Def.
29
) with respect to a realisation of an orthogonality graph.
In turn, given a unital realisation we may identify the vertices in an orthogonality graph with the (minimal) generating projections of some partial algebra, that is, we may assume that
G
is associated with a partial algebra. Indeed, it is easily seen that unital realisations correspond with faithful (and constraint-preserving) orthomorphisms which induce (constraint-preserving) embeddings of partial algebras by Lm.
1
(see Tab.
5
). In Sec.
4.2
below, we will therefore first study Kochen-Specker contextuality for orthogonality graphs under the restricting to unital realisations in Def.
23
.

Orthogonality graphs as constraints on correlations.
By interpreting orthogonality graphs
G=(V,E)
as encoding exclusivity relations - representing events that are related if they correspond to different measurement outcomes - a correlation on
G
is simply defined as a map
{\widetilde{\gamma}}:V\rightarrow[0,1]
that is additive on exclusive events
[
52
,
11
,
12
]
. General correlations thus constitute the
clique constrained stable set polytope
,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{QStab}}(G)=\{{\widetilde{\gamma}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|V|}_{+}\mid\sum_{v\in\mathrm{C}}{\widetilde{\gamma}}(v)\leq 1\quad\forall\mathrm{\ cliques\ }\mathrm{C}\mathrm{\ in\ G}\}\;.

(10)

The inequality in Eq. (
10
) is a consequence of the fact that
G\subset{G({\mathcal{O}})}
is generally only a subgraph for
{\mathcal{O}}
a partial algebra, in particular,
\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]\subsetneq{\mathrm{QStab}}(G)
in this case.
57
57

57

Since every orthogonality graph
G=(V,E)
has a quantum realisation on a Hilbert space of dimension
\dim(\mathcal{H})\leq|V|

[
101
]
, a partial algebra
{\mathcal{O}}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
with
G\subset{G({\mathcal{O}})}
always exists (cf. App.
E
).
In turn, it is natural to demand equality for associated orthogonality graphs
G={G({\mathcal{O}})}
, that is, under the restriction to unital realisations. We will at times distinguish unnormalised “correlations”
{\widetilde{\gamma}}\in{\mathrm{QStab}}(G)
for
G\subset{G({\mathcal{O}})}
from normalised “states”
\gamma\in\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
for
G={G({\mathcal{O}})}
, by marking the former with a tilde.

Classical correlations are modelled as probabilistic mixtures of all events that can occur together, that is, that are non-exclusive. These define the
stable set polytope
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathrm{Stab}}(G)&=\mathrm{conv}\{{\widetilde{\gamma}}\in\{0,1\}^{|V|}\mid{\widetilde{\gamma}}(v){\widetilde{\gamma}}(v^{\prime})=0\quad\forall\ (v,v^{\prime})\in E\}\\
&=\mathrm{conv}\{I(G)\mid I(G)\mathrm{\ independent\ set\ of\ }G\}\;.\end{split}

(11)

For
G={G({\mathcal{O}})}
, maximal independent sets with support in every maximal clique correspond with valuations, which we denote by
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{Val}}({G({\mathcal{O}})})\subset\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{max}}({G({\mathcal{O}})})
; in particular,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]\subsetneq{\mathrm{Stab}}({G({\mathcal{O}})})
(for more details on the distinction between states on partial algebras
{\mathcal{O}}
and correlations on
{G({\mathcal{O}})}
, see App.
D.2
).
58
58

58

The converse is clearly false: an independent set does generally not define a valuation, since an independent set is not required to assign one element in every maximal clique the value
1
. Consequently, the correlations
{\mathrm{Stab}}(G)
are more general than classical correlations for fixed dimension.

Finally, we consider quantum correlations, that is, those arising from quantum states under the Born rule. More precisely, given an orthogonality graph
G=(V,E)
with realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
and quantum state
\rho\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H})
we obtain a correlation of the form
{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{\rho}(v)=\mathrm{tr}[\pi(v)\rho]
for all
v\in V
. This gives rise to the so-called
theta body
,
59
59

59

Here, realisations are not required to be faithful or constraint-preserving
[
52
]
.

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Th}}(G)=\{{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{\rho}\in\mathbb{R}^{|V|}_{+}\mid{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{\rho}(v)=\mathrm{tr}[\pi(v)\rho]\mathrm{\ for\ }\rho\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H})\mathrm{\ and\ }\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}\mathrm{\ a\ realisation}\}\;.

(12)

For general realisations,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{qu}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]\subset{\mathrm{Th}}(G)
, while equality holds under the restriction to unital realisations (since normalisation in this case follows from the normalisation of quantum states). Finally, for every orthogonality graph
G
one finds the hierarchy

\displaystyle\mathrm{Stab}(G)\subset{\mathrm{Th}}(G)\subset{\mathrm{QStab}}(G)\;.

(13)

which for orthogonality graphs associated with a partial algebra
G={G({\mathcal{O}})}
closely resembles Eq. (
7
). Note that the (non-trivial) inclusion
{\mathrm{Stab}}(G)\subset{\mathrm{Th}}(G)
holds since
{\mathrm{Th}}(G)
contains realisations of any Hilbert space dimension. Moreover, one can show that the inclusions are strict if and only if
G
contains an
n
-cycle of length
n\geq 5
as an induced subgraph
[
52
]
.

To summarise, representing exclusivity relations between (a subset of the) events in a partial algebra via an orthogonality graph encodes the constraints on states of partial algebras in an economic way. Moreover, it allows to bound correlations via graph-theoretic quantities
[
52
,
160
,
51
]
, and to use these bounds in order to construct noncontextuality inequalities
[
154
,
21
,
17
]
. Below, we will be concerned with one such quantity: the chromatic number. In order to compare the respective results on contextuality for orthogonality graphs in Ref.
[
52
,
160
,
51
]
with Thm.
2
, we first translate the notion of Kochen-Specker contextuality to orthogonality graphs with unital realisation.

4.2
Kochen-Specker contextuality for orthogonality graphs
G={G({\mathcal{O}})}

Our aim in this section is to translate the notion of Kochen-Specker (KS) contextuality to the graph-theoretic language. To this end, we cast Def.
18
to the present setting.

Definition 24
.

An orthogonality graph
G={G({\mathcal{O}})}
is called
noncontextual
if there exists a normalised
60
60

60

To be clear, we require that
\gamma
is normalised in every maximal clique
\mathrm{C}\subset G
, that is,
\sum_{v\in\mathrm{C}}\gamma(v)=1
.
correlation
\gamma\in{\mathrm{Stab}}(G)
such that
0\neq\gamma(v)\neq\gamma(v^{\prime})\neq 0
for all
v,v^{\prime}\in V
,
v\neq v^{\prime}
.

As we point out in detail in App.
D.2
, the notions of classical correlations
{\mathrm{Stab}}(G)
on
G
and classical states
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
on
{\mathcal{O}}
are not related for general orthogonality graphs
G\subsetneq{G({\mathcal{O}})}
. We thus restrict (first) to orthogonality graphs
G={G({\mathcal{O}})}
associated to a finitely generated, not necessarily maximal partial algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
. Yet, even in this case classical correlations on
{G({\mathcal{O}})}
are more general than classical states on
{\mathcal{O}}
, that is,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]\subsetneq{\mathrm{Stab}}({G({\mathcal{O}})})
. Consequently, the precise relationship between Def.
24
for
{G({\mathcal{O}})}
and (marginal) KS noncontextuality for
{\mathcal{O}}
is not immediately clear.

In order to compare the notion of (marginal) KS noncontextuality with that in Def.
24
, we first show that a KS noncontextual partial algebra admits an optimal classical representation with respect to the subset
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{Val}}({G({\mathcal{O}})})\subset\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{max}}({G({\mathcal{O}})})
of all maximal independent sets on
{G({\mathcal{O}})}
, that is, those that contain a single element in every maximal clique of
{G({\mathcal{O}})}
, hence, which correspond to valuations on
{\mathcal{O}}
.

Lemma 5
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a finitely generated partial algebra with partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
and associated orthogonality graph
{G({\mathcal{O}})}
. If
{\mathcal{O}}
is Kochen-Specker noncontextual, then there exists a classical embedding
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow L_{\infty}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{Val}}({G({\mathcal{O}})}))
.

Proof.

If
{\mathcal{O}}
is KS noncontextual, then by definition there exists an embedding
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
for some measurable space
\Lambda
, and since
{\mathcal{O}}
is finitely generated, we may take
\Lambda
to be a finite set. Consequently, every element
\lambda\in\Lambda
defines a subset
I(\lambda)=\{p\in{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}})}\mid\chi(\lambda\mid p)=\epsilon(p)(\lambda)=1\}
, and we have
I(\lambda)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{Val}}({G({\mathcal{O}})})
as
\lambda
singles out a unique projection in every maximal context
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
: indeed, in every partition
\Lambda=\dot{\cup}_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}(C)}\Lambda_{p}
with
\Lambda_{p}=\mathrm{supp}(\epsilon(p))
we have
p\in I(\lambda)
if and only if
\lambda\in\Lambda_{p}
.

In general, different elements
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda
,
\lambda\neq\lambda^{\prime}
(under the classical embedding
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
) will give rise to the same independent set
I(\lambda)=I(\lambda^{\prime})
. We define a more economical map
\epsilon^{\prime}
by composing
\epsilon
with the “coarse-graining” map
\Lambda\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{Val}}({G({\mathcal{O}})})
, that is, by linear extension of
\epsilon^{\prime}(p)(I(\lambda))=\epsilon(p)(\lambda)=1
if and only if
p\in I(\lambda)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{Val}}({G({\mathcal{O}})})
. In other words,
\mathrm{supp}(\epsilon^{\prime}(p))=\mathcal{I}(p):=\{I(\lambda)\mid\exists\lambda\in\Lambda:\ p\in I(\lambda)\}
. Denote the image under this coarse-graining map by
\mathcal{I}(\epsilon)\subset\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{val}}({G({\mathcal{O}})})
. Clearly, we have
\epsilon^{\prime}(\mathbb{I})=\dot{\cup}_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}(C)}\mathcal{I}(p)=\mathcal{I}(\epsilon)
(since every independent set
I\in\mathcal{I}(p)\subset\mathcal{I}(\epsilon)
contains one element in every context
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
). Moreover,
\mathcal{I}(p)\cap\mathcal{I}(p^{\prime})=\emptyset
whenever
p,p^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}})}
,
pp^{\prime}=0
since no independent set
I\in\mathcal{I}({G({\mathcal{O}})})
contains both
p
and
p^{\prime}
.
\epsilon^{\prime}|_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}})}
thus defines a faithful orthomorphism which induces a classical embedding by Lm.
1
.
∎

Note that Lm.
5
is closely related with Lm.
1
which shows that the existence of a classical embedding for
{\mathcal{O}}
is equivalent to the existence of a faithful orthomorphism of
{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, and hence, that KS contextuality is a property of the latter.

Under the embedding in Lm.
5
, every classical state
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
thus defines a (normalised) classical correlation on
{G({\mathcal{O}})}
, that is,
\gamma\in{\mathrm{Stab}}({G({\mathcal{O}})})
. This brings us one step closer to comparing (marginal) KS noncontextuality of
{\mathcal{O}}
with the notion of noncontextuality of its associated orthogonality graph
{G({\mathcal{O}})}
.
Yet, the converse does not hold, that is,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]\subsetneq{\mathrm{Stab}}({G({\mathcal{O}})})
since
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{Val}}({G({\mathcal{O}})})\subsetneq\mathcal{I}({G({\mathcal{O}})})
, in particular, maximal independent sets of
{G({\mathcal{O}})}
are not necessarily valuations. Still, the following theorem shows that the respective notions of contextuality coincide if
G=G({\mathcal{O}})
.

Theorem 8
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a finitely generated partial algebra with associated orthogonality graph
G={G({\mathcal{O}})}
. Then
{\mathcal{O}}
is Kochen-Specker contextual if and only if
G
is contextual.

Proof.

If
{\mathcal{O}}
is KS noncontextual, then there exists a classical embedding
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
, which by Lm.
5
reduces to a classical embedding
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow L_{\infty}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{Val}}({G({\mathcal{O}})}))
under the coarse-graining
\Lambda\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{Val}}({G({\mathcal{O}})})\subset\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{max}}({G({\mathcal{O}})})
. Now, since
\epsilon
is faithful, there exists a separating state
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]\subset{\mathrm{Stab}}({G({\mathcal{O}})})
with full support on
{G({\mathcal{O}})}
, that is, such that
0\neq\gamma(v)\neq\gamma(v^{\prime})\neq 0
for all
v,v^{\prime}\in V
,
v\neq v^{\prime}
. Hence,
G
is noncontextual by Def.
24
.

Conversely, if
G={G({\mathcal{O}})}
is noncontextual by Def.
24
, then there exists a state (a normalised correlation)
\gamma\in{\mathrm{Stab}}(G)
, that is, for all
v\in V={\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}})}

\displaystyle\gamma(v)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(G)}\mathrm{w}_{\gamma}(I)I(v)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(v)}\mathrm{w}_{\gamma}(I)\;,

(14)

where
\mathrm{w}_{\gamma}(I)\geq 0
for all
I\in\mathcal{I}(G)
with
\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(G)}\mathrm{w}_{\gamma}(I)=1
, and where we defined
\mathcal{I}(v)=\{I\in\mathcal{I}(G)\mid I(v)=1\}
. Clearly, we have
\mathcal{I}(v)\cap\mathcal{I}(v^{\prime})=\emptyset
whenever
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathrm{C}
with
v\neq v^{\prime}
for any clique
\mathrm{C}\subset{G({\mathcal{O}})}
. Setting
\mathcal{I}(\emptyset)=\emptyset
we thus obtain orthomorphisms
\varphi_{C}:{\mathcal{P}(C)}\rightarrow 2^{\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{C})}
defined additively on minimal projections by
\varphi_{C}(v)=\mathcal{I}(v)
(recall that
V={\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}})}
), and where
\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{C}):=\dot{\bigcup}_{v\in\mathrm{C}}\mathcal{I}(v)=\{I\in\mathcal{I}(G)\mid\exists v\in\mathrm{C}:\ I(v)=1\}
for every context
C\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})
(equivalently, for every clique
\mathrm{C}\subset G
). Now, we would like to define an orthomorphism from the collection of orthomorphisms
\{\varphi_{C}\}_{C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}}
. For this to be the case we must have
\varphi_{C}(p)=\varphi_{C^{\prime}}(p)
for all
C,C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
with
p\in{\mathcal{P}(C)},\mathcal{P}(C^{\prime})
, yet since
\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{C})\neq\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{C}^{\prime})
for different (maximal) cliques, this is generally not the case.

Nevertheless, since
\gamma
is normalised (by assumption) and using Eq. (
14
) for every maximal clique
\mathrm{C}\in G
, we find

\displaystyle 1=\sum_{v\in\mathrm{C}}\gamma(v)=\sum_{v\in\mathrm{C}}\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(v)}\mathrm{w}_{\gamma}(I)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{C})}\mathrm{w}_{\gamma}(I)\;.

Since also
\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(G)}\mathrm{w}_{\gamma}(I)=1
this implies that
\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}(\mathrm{C})=\mathcal{I}(G)/\mathcal{I}_{\gamma,0}(G)=:\Lambda_{\gamma}
, where
\mathcal{I}_{\gamma,0}(G)=\{I\in\mathcal{I}(G)\mid\mathrm{w}_{\gamma}(I)=0\}
depends on the correlation
\gamma
, but is independent of the clique
\mathrm{C}\subset G
. We may thus define a new collection of orthomorphisms
\{\varphi_{\gamma,C}\}_{C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}}
,
\varphi_{\gamma,C}:{\mathcal{P}(C)}\rightarrow 2^{\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}(\mathrm{C})}
by
\varphi_{\gamma,C}(v)=\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}(v):=\mathcal{I}(v)/\mathcal{I}_{\gamma,0}(G)
. Clearly, we still have
\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}(v)\cap\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}(v^{\prime})=\emptyset
whenever
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathrm{C}
. Moreover,
\varphi_{\gamma,C}(\mathbb{I})=\varphi_{\gamma,C^{\prime}}(\mathbb{I})
for all
C,C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, which implies that
\varphi_{\gamma}:{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}(\Lambda_{\gamma})=2^{\Lambda_{\gamma}}
,
\varphi_{\gamma}(p)=\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}(p)
defines an orthomorphism of event algebras. From
\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}(\mathrm{C})=\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}(\mathrm{C}^{\prime})
for all maximal cliques
\mathrm{C},\mathrm{C}^{\prime}\subset G
, it follows that
\gamma(I)\neq 0
only if
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{val}}(G)
, and since by Def.
24
we may assume
0\neq\gamma(v)\neq\gamma(v^{\prime})\neq 0
whenever
v,v^{\prime}\in V
,
v\neq v^{\prime}
, we conclude that
\varphi_{\gamma}:{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}(\Lambda_{\gamma})
is a faithful orthomorphism of event algebras, which induces a classical embedding by Lm.
1
.
∎

Thm.
8
proves that Def.
24
is indeed a reformulation of KS noncontextuality for orthogonality graphs associated with observable algebras, that is, in cases where both notions are comparable. We highlight two important steps in the proof of Thm.
8
.

First, the converse direction necessarily requires that equality holds in Eq. (
10
), which is natural if
G={G({\mathcal{O}})}
is associated with (equivalently, has a unital realisation in) a partial algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
. In turn, if we consider general realisations
\pi:G\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
, we have to add further vertices in order to obtain equality in Eq. (
10
), that is, we need to consider (correlations on) larger graphs
G\subset G^{\prime}=G({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
. Yet, by Prop.
3
in App.
D.2
,
{\mathrm{Stab}}(G^{\prime})|_{G}\neq{\mathrm{Stab}}(G)
for
G\subset G^{\prime}
, that is, the respective correlations in
G
and
G^{\prime}
are not naturally related, in particular, they generally depend on the realisation
\pi
.

Second, note that Thm.
8
is very similar to Thm.
4
. In particular, the second assertion in Thm.
8
relies on the existence of a separating classical state with full support on an orthogonality graph. This assumption cannot be omitted without spoiling the equivalence in general: while we can construct the orthomorphism
\varphi_{\gamma}
in the proof of Thm.
8
for any (normalised) state
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]\subset{\mathrm{Stab}}({G({\mathcal{O}})})
, by a similar argument to the one in the proof of Lm.
2
in Sec.
3.2.2
, it will generally not be faithful.

Next, we show that contextual orthogonality graphs are characterised by their chromatic number. For ease of notation, we introduce the following definition.

Definition 25
.

Let
G
be an orthogonality graph with unital realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, and let
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
be a maximal extension of the partial algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
generated by
\pi(G)
. Then the
maximal extension
G^{*}
of
G
with respect to
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
(and thus
\pi
)
is defined as
G^{*}=\mathcal{P}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}^{*})
.

Corollary 3
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a partial algebra with orthogonality graph
G={G({\mathcal{O}})}
and
\dim(\mathbb{I})=d
. Then
G
is contextual if and only if
\chi(G^{*})>d
.

Proof.

This follows from Thm.
8
together with Thm.
3
.
∎

The restriction to orthogonality graphs
G={G({\mathcal{O}})}
associated with partial algebras simplifies the characterisation of KS contextuality, yet it leaves open the problem of finding a (quantum) realisation for
G
, equivalently of characterising quantum among more general partial algebras. While this is a fascinating and likely hard problem in general, in many applications one is given a subset of quantum observables
{\mathcal{O}}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
with
\dim(\mathcal{H})=d
to begin with, and the problem thus reduces to determining whether
{\mathcal{O}}
is contextual or not. The study of state-independent contextuality (SI-C) sets which we turn to next is concerned with this more restricted problem.

4.3
State-independent contextuality (SI-C) sets and graphs

The notion of (non)contextuality in Def.
24
is not commonly used in the graph-theoretic approach. Instead, one finds a notion similar to the one in Def.
16
, which is motivated by the study of noncontextuality inequalities
[
155
,
21
,
123
]
. Similar to Bell inequalities
[
27
,
39
,
106
,
115
]
, the violation of a noncontextuality inequality is generally state-dependent
[
125
,
133
]
. Yet, while the violation of a Bell inequality is necessarily state-dependent there exist noncontextuality inequalities that are violated in every state
[
44
,
188
,
30
,
187
,
132
]
. This has motivated the following definition.

Definition 26

(SI-C set)
.

A set of projectors
\{\pi(v)\in{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}\}_{v\in V}
, given in terms of a quantum realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
of an orthogonality graph in dimension
\dim(\mathcal{H})=d
, is called a
state-independent contextuality (SI-C) set
if
{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{\rho}\notin{\mathrm{Stab}}(G)
for all
\rho\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H})
, where
{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{\rho}(v)=\mathrm{tr}[\pi(v)\rho]
for all
v\in V
.
61
61

61

We follow the notation in Ref.
[
51
]
, which avoids confusion with the commonly used abbreviation ‘SIC POVM’ for symmetric, informationally complete positive operator-valued measure
[
84
]
.
62
62

62

While
{\widetilde{\gamma}}
is generally not a state, that is, it is not normalised in every clique,
{\widetilde{\gamma}}
necessarily extends to a state on the completion
\overline{\pi(V)}
with respect to
\pi
(see Eq. (
15
) below).

SI-C sets play an important role in experimental tests of contextuality
[
170
,
137
,
49
,
44
,
25
,
121
,
14
,
92
,
130
]
. However, characterising SI-C sets is a hard problem in general
[
51
]
. A simplification to orthogonality graphs was proposed in Ref.
[
160
]
.

Definition 27

(SI-C graph)
.

An orthogonality graph
G
is called
(state-dependently) contextual in dimension
d
if there exists a quantum state
\rho\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H})
with
\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{H})=d
such that
{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{\rho}(v)=\mathrm{tr}[\pi(v)\rho]\notin\mathrm{STAB}(G)
for some realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{H})}
of
G
.

Moreover,
G
is
state-independently contextual (SI-C) in dimension
d
if it is (state-dependently) contextual in dimension
d
for every quantum state
\rho\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H})
.

Our present concern with SI-C sets (and graphs) is that the discovery of SI-C sets has raised some concern over the true essence of Kochen-Specker (KS) contextuality. We recall that many proofs of the KS theorem construct so-called KS sets
[
127
,
134
,
151
,
135
,
192
,
118
]
, that is, sets of projectors for which it is impossible to single out one in every set of mutually orthogonal ones (see Sec.
2.4
). Yet, while every KS set is a SI-C set
[
21
,
191
,
190
]
, not every SI-C set is also a KS set
[
188
,
30
,
46
]
. In Ref.
[
76
]
, we argue that proofs of contextuality based on (the violation of noncontextuality inequalities corresponding to) SI-C sets are nevertheless compatible with the notion of Kochen-Specker contextuality. In the following, we will provide a more detailed comparison.

To begin with, observe that existing (partial) characterisations of SI-C sets and SI-C graphs somewhat resemble Thm.
3
which implies Cor.
3
. Specifically, Ref.
[
46
,
51
]
argue that
\chi(G)>d
is a necessary condition for the realisation of an orthogonality graph
G
to be SI-C set (note the necessary restriction on realisations discussed below and in App.
F
), and Ref.
[
160
]
further proves that for an orthogonality graph
G
to be SI-C in dimension
d
is equivalent to
\chi_{f}(G)>d
, where
\chi_{f}(G)
denotes the fractional chromatic number of
G
. Yet, despite the similarity of these results with Thm.
3
and Cor.
3
, we point out various subtleties involved with the underlying definitions. First, unlike Def.
24
, Def.
26
and Def.
27
are concerned with the reduced marginal problem of quantum states with respect quantum realisations in a given dimension. Second, unlike the realisations in Cor.
3
, the realisations of SI-C graphs (in particular, SI-C sets) are generally not unital. And, third, SI-C graphs are not simply the orthogonality graphs of SI-C sets: there are SI-C graphs which do not have a realisation that is a SI-C set (see Thm. 1 in Ref.
[
51
]
).
63
63

63

Since the two notions coincide whenever the quantifiers over realisations and states in Def.
26
and Def.
27
commute, this also shows that this is generally not the case (see Sec.
4.3
).
The following subsections (together with App.
F
) will address these issues in turn, beginning with the case of SI-C graphs associated with partial algebras.

4.3.1
Unital realisations

We consider Def.
27
under the restriction to orthogonality graphs
G={G({\mathcal{O}})}
associated to a partial algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
. As discussed in Sec.
4.1
, in order to preserve the additional structure imposed by the identity
\mathbb{I}
in
{\mathcal{O}}
one needs to require that quantum realisations map maximal cliques in
G
to resolutions of
\mathbb{I}
. Consequently, for orthogonality graphs
G={G({\mathcal{O}})}
, we modify Def.
27
by restricting to
unital
quantum realisations.

Definition 28
.

An orthogonality graph
G
is
unital (state-dependently) contextual in dimension
d
if there exists a state
\rho\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H})
with
d=\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{H})
such that
\gamma_{\rho}(v)=\mathrm{tr}[\pi(v)\rho]\notin\mathrm{STAB}(G)
for some unital quantum realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
of
G
.

Moreover,
G
is
unital state-independently contextual (USI-C) in dimension
d
if it is unitarily (state-dependently) contextual in dimension
d
for every state
\rho\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H})
.

By Lm.
1
, a unital quantum realisation of
{G({\mathcal{O}})}
is equivalent to a constraint-preserving quantum embedding of
{\mathcal{O}}
(see also Tab.
5
). Under this stronger assumption, we can employ the characterisation of KS contextuality in Thm.
3
.

Theorem 9
.

Let
G
be an orthogonality graph with unital quantum realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
in
\dim(\mathcal{H})=d
. Then
G
is unital state-independently contextual (USI-C) in dimension
d
only if
\chi(G^{*})>d
.

Proof.

By assumption,
G={G({\mathcal{O}})}
with
{\mathcal{O}}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
the partial algebra induced by the unital quantum realisation, that is,
{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}})}=\pi(G)
. Therefore, if
\chi(G^{*})=d
then
{\mathcal{O}}
is KS noncontextual by Thm.
3
, that is, there exists a classical embedding
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
. Moreover, by Lm.
9
, there exists a classical embedding
\epsilon^{*}:{\mathcal{O}}^{*}\rightarrow L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
, and
G^{*}=G({\mathcal{O}}^{*})
has a unital realisation
\pi^{*}:G^{*}\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{H})}
such that
\pi^{*}|_{G}=\pi
. (Indeed, since
G
is finite, we can always decompose projections
\pi(v)=\sum_{i=1}^{\dim(\pi(v))}\pi^{*}(v^{*}_{i})
such that no new orthogonality relations arise, that is,
\pi^{*}(v^{*}_{i})\pi^{*}(v^{*})=0
if and only if
v^{*}_{i},v^{*}\in\mathrm{C}^{*}
and
v_{i}^{*}\neq v^{*}
.) By Lm.
5
, we may further assume that
(\epsilon^{*})^{\prime}:{\mathcal{O}}^{*}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{val}}(G^{*})
, and since
\chi(G^{*})=d
, there exists a
d
-colouring
\zeta:V^{*}\rightarrow[d]
. In particular, each colour picks out a unique valuation in
\mathcal{I}(\zeta)=\{I_{1}=\zeta^{-1}(v_{1}),\cdots,I_{d}=\zeta^{-1}(v_{d})\}\subset\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{val}}(G^{*})
for the set of vertices
\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{d}\}=\mathrm{C}^{*}
in any maximal clique
\mathrm{C}^{*}\subset G^{*}
. The valuations in
\mathcal{I}(\zeta)
satisfy
I_{i}\cap I_{j}=\emptyset
whenever
i\neq j
and
\dot{\bigcup}_{i=1}^{d}I_{i}=V^{*}
. Now, define the classical state
\gamma^{*}\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}}^{*})]
by
\gamma^{*}(I)=\frac{1}{d}
if
I\in\mathcal{I}(\zeta)
and
\gamma^{*}(I)=0
otherwise. Clearly, we have
\gamma^{*}=\mathbbm{1}/d|_{\pi^{*}(G^{*})}
, hence, by setting
\gamma(v)=\sum_{i}\gamma(v^{*}_{i})
for all
v\in V
we also have
\gamma(v)=\mathrm{tr}[\pi(v)\mathbbm{1}/d]\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
. Hence,
G
is not a USI-C graph in dimension
d
.
∎

Corollary 4
.

A necessary condition for
\pi(V)
for
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
a unital quantum realisation of an orthogonality graph
G
in dimension
\dim(\mathcal{H})=d
to be a state-independent contextuality (SI-C) set is that
\chi(G^{*})>d
.

Proof.

The orthogonality graph of a SI-C set is always a SI-C graph.
∎

The converse implications of Thm.
9
and Cor.
4
are generally false, since a quantum state
\rho\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H})
may have a classical representation of the form
\gamma(v)=\mathrm{tr}[\pi(v)\rho]\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
, yet one for which
\gamma
is not separating and of full support (cf. Lm.
2
in Sec.
3.2.2
). The notions of USI-C graph
G={G({\mathcal{O}})}
, SI-C sets with unital realisation and KS contextuality of
{\mathcal{O}}
therefore generally differ. What about more general realisations.

Thm.
9
and Cor.
4
generalise the results in Ref.
[
46
,
160
,
51
]
in the case of a unital realisations. Yet, what about more general realisations? In the next section, we further extend our results by lifting a non-unital realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
of an orthogonality graph
G
to the unital realisation of its completion in
{\mathcal{O}}
.

4.3.2
Realisations with faithful completion

We seek to generalise Thm.
9
to orthogonality graphs with not necessarily unital realisation. Note that the realisations
\pi
in Def.
26
and Def.
27
are not assumed to be unital. Still, the correlations one is interested in are those generated by
\pi
in
{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
:

\displaystyle\overline{\pi(V)}=\langle\pi(V),\mathbbm{1}\rangle\;,

(15)

where the
completion
\overline{\pi(V)}
contains all projections that can be written as sums of elements in
\{\pi(V),\mathbbm{1}\}
, and recursively from newly generated elements; in particular,
\{{\overline{p}}(\mathrm{C})=\mathbbm{1}-\sum_{v\in\mathrm{C}}\pi(v)\mid\mathrm{C}\subset G\mathrm{\ a\ maximal\ clique}\}\subset\overline{\pi(V)}
, moreover
\overline{\pi(V)}
may contain new contexts generated by such elements. Indeed, the observables violating a noncontextuality inequality corresponding to a SI-C set necessarily include the identity element
[
44
,
188
,
21
]
. This is most evident in noncontextuality inequalities involving dichotomic observables, whose quantum violation stems from measurements of the form
O_{v}=\mathbbm{1}-2\pi(v)
for all
v\in V
. In this sense, SI-C sets correspond with the full partial algebra
{\mathcal{O}}(\pi(V))={\mathcal{O}}(\overline{\pi(V)})
induced by the event algebra
\overline{\pi(V)}=\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}}(\pi(V)))
, generated by
\pi(V)
in
{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
. Define
{\overline{G(\pi)}}:=G({\mathcal{O}}(\pi(V)))
, then
\pi
has an extension to a unital quantum realisation
\overline{\pi}:{\overline{G(\pi)}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
such that
\overline{\pi}|_{G}=\pi
.
64
64

64

Note that
G
is always a vertex-restricted subgraph of its completion
{\overline{G(\pi)}}
(see App.
D.2
).
Clearly, the orthogonality graph
{\overline{G(\pi)}}
depends on the realisation
\pi
of
G
. We therefore first establish some relevant facts about completions induced by general realisations of orthogonality graphs.

Faithful completions.
What is the difference between Thm.
9
and the results in Ref.
[
46
,
160
,
51
]
? The latter consider the chromatic number of the subgraph
G\subset{\overline{G(\pi)}}
, where
{\overline{G(\pi)}}
denotes the orthogonality graph of its completion
\overline{\pi(V)}
with respect to the realisation
\pi
in Eq. (
15
). Now, it is generally not true that
\chi(G)<d
implies
\chi({\overline{G(\pi)}})<d
. Indeed, the completion
{\overline{G(\pi)}}
with respect to a realisation
\pi
will generally introduce new constraints between the vertices in
G
. Explicitly, write
{\overline{v}}_{\pi}(\mathrm{C})
for the vertex in
{\overline{G(\pi)}}
corresponding to the projection
{\overline{p}}(\mathrm{C})=\mathbb{I}-\sum_{v\in\mathrm{C}}\pi(v)
in the context realisation of the maximal clique
\mathrm{C}\subset G
. Then we may have
{\overline{v}}_{\pi}(\mathrm{C})={\overline{v}}_{\pi}(\mathrm{C}^{\prime})
for two distinct maximal cliques
\mathrm{C},\mathrm{C}^{\prime}\subset G
,
\mathrm{C}\neq\mathrm{C}^{\prime}
. Moreover, two vertices
{\overline{v}}_{\pi}(\mathrm{C})
and
{\overline{v}}_{\pi}(\mathrm{C}^{\prime})
may be orthogonal and thus pose additional constraints on a colouring of
{\overline{G(\pi)}}
. Consequently,

\chi({\overline{G(\pi)}})>\chi(G)
in general (even if
\omega({\overline{G(\pi)}})=\omega(G)
). For example, for a uniquely colourable graph with two non-maximal cliques
\mathrm{C},\mathrm{C}^{\prime}\subset G
which jointly contain all colours in
\chi(G)
, together with a realisation such that
{\overline{v}}_{\pi}(\mathrm{C})={\overline{v}}_{\pi}(\mathrm{C}^{\prime})
, one finds
\chi({\overline{G(\pi)}})>\chi(G)
. The following restriction to faithful completions avoids this issue.

Definition 29
.

A realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
of an orthogonality graph
G
is called
freely completable
and the completion
\overline{\pi(V)}

faithful
if
{\overline{p}}(\mathrm{C})\neq{\overline{p}}(\mathrm{C}^{\prime})
for any two distinct maximal cliques
\mathrm{C},\mathrm{C}^{\prime}\subset G
,
\mathrm{C}\neq\mathrm{C}^{\prime}
and
{\overline{p}}(\mathrm{C})\pi(v)=0
only if
v\in\mathrm{C}
.

For instance, the completion of the realisation in Fig. 2 in Ref.
[
188
]
of the Yu-Oh orthogonality graph (see also Fig.
6
in App.
F
) is faithful. Faithful completions of an orthogonality graph
G
depend on the realisation only via the dimension
\dim(\mathbb{I})=d
of the target partial algebra; we therefore write
\overline{G(d)}
in this case. As an aside, we mention that the existence of
any
realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
of
G
with
\dim(\mathbb{I})=d
implies the existence of a realisation with faithful completion
\overline{G(d)}
in the same dimension.

Lemma 6
.

Let
G
be an orthogonality graph with realisation
\pi^{\prime}:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
in dimension
\dim(\mathbb{I}^{\prime})=d
. Then
G
has a realisation with
\dim(\mathbb{I})=d
and faithful completion
\overline{G(d)}
.

Proof.

Since
G
has a realisation
\pi^{\prime}:G\rightarrow\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
with
\dim(\mathbb{I}^{\prime})=d
, there exists a dimension function
\dim^{\prime}:\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
on
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
(see Def.
5
) with
\dim(\mathbb{I}^{\prime})=\dim^{\prime}({\overline{v}}_{\pi^{\prime}}(\mathrm{C}))+\sum_{v\in\mathrm{C}}\dim^{\prime}(v)
for all cliques
\mathrm{C}\subset G
and
{\overline{v}}_{\pi^{\prime}}(\mathrm{C})
the unique vertex corresponding to
{\overline{p}}(\mathrm{C})
in its completion in Eq. (
15
). Now, let
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
be a realisation with faithful completion of
G
as by Def.
29
. Then
\dim^{\prime}
induces a dimension function
\dim:{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
on
{\mathcal{O}}
by setting
\dim|_{V}=\dim^{\prime}|_{V}
and
\dim({\overline{v}}_{\pi}(\mathrm{C}))=\dim^{\prime}({\overline{v}}_{\pi^{\prime}}(\mathrm{C}))
. In particular, we have

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07182pt\dim(\mathbb{I})=\dim({\overline{v}}_{\pi}(\mathrm{C}))+\sum_{v\in\mathrm{C}}\dim(v)=\dim^{\prime}({\overline{v}}_{\pi^{\prime}}(\mathrm{C}))+\sum_{v\in\mathrm{C}}\dim^{\prime}(v)=\dim(\mathbb{I}^{\prime})=d\;.\hskip 34.14322pt\qed

Next, we relate the chromatic number
\chi(G)
of an orthogonality graph
G\subset{\overline{G(\pi)}}
with that of the minimal extension of its completion
\chi({\overline{G(\pi)}}^{*})
with respect to some realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Clearly, a
d
-colouring of
{\overline{G(\pi)}}^{*}
induces a
d
-colouring of
G
by restriction. In other words,
\chi(G)\leq\chi({\overline{G(\pi)}}^{*})
. However, the converse is generally not true. However, under the restriction to freely completable realisations with one-dimensional projections the following relation holds.

Lemma 7
.

Let
G
be an orthogonality graph with freely completable realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{1}({\mathcal{O}})}
in dimension
\dim(\mathbb{I})=d
. Then
\chi(G)\leq d
if and only if
\chi(G^{*})\leq d
.

Proof.

Clearly,
\chi(G)\leq\chi(G^{*})
since a colouring
\zeta^{*}:V^{*}\rightarrow[d]
yields a colouring
\zeta^{*}|_{V}:V\rightarrow[d]
for
G
by restriction, hence,
\chi(G^{*})\leq d
implies
\chi(G)\leq d
.

Conversely,
G
arises from
G^{*}=G({\mathcal{O}}^{*})
by removing in (some) maximal cliques
\mathrm{C}\subset\mathrm{C}^{*}\subset G^{*}
the vertices
{\overline{v}}_{\pi^{*},i}(\mathrm{C})
corresponding to the one-dimensional projections
p^{*}_{i}(\mathrm{C})
in the maximal extension
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
of
{\mathcal{O}}={\mathcal{O}}(\pi(V))
, which (by Def.
30
) satisfy
p^{*}_{i}(\mathrm{C})p^{*}_{j}(\mathrm{C}^{\prime})=0
if and only if
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}^{\prime}
and
i\neq j
as well as
{\overline{p}}(\mathrm{C})=\mathbbm{1}-\sum_{v\in\mathrm{C}}\pi(v)=\sum_{i=1}^{d-|\mathrm{C}|}p^{*}_{i}(\mathrm{C})
, where
{\overline{p}}(\mathrm{C})
is the projection with corresponding vertex
{\overline{v}}_{\pi}(\mathrm{C})
in the faithful completion of
G
. Moreover, by Def.
29
,
{\overline{v}}_{\pi}(\mathrm{C})\neq{\overline{v}}_{\pi}(\mathrm{C}^{\prime})
whenever
\mathrm{C}\neq\mathrm{C}^{\prime}
and
({\overline{v}}_{\pi}(\mathrm{C}),v)\in E^{*}
if and only if
v\in\mathrm{C}
. Consequently, given a
d
-colouring
\zeta:V\rightarrow[d]
for
G
, we obtain a valid
d
-colouring
\zeta^{*}:V^{*}\rightarrow[d]
for
G^{*}
by assigning to
\{{\overline{v}}_{\pi^{*},i}(\mathrm{C})\}_{i}
the colours not yet assigned to any other vertex in
\mathrm{C}
, that is, such that
\zeta^{*}({\overline{v}}_{\pi^{*},i}(\mathrm{C}))\neq\zeta(v)
for all
v\in\mathrm{C}
and
i\in\{1,\cdots,d-|\mathrm{C}|\}
. It follows that
\chi(G)\leq d
implies
\chi(G^{*})\leq d
.
∎

With the notion of freely completable realisations at hand, we obtain the following partial characterisation of SI-C graphs in terms of their chromatic number.

A necessary condition for SI-C graphs and SI-C sets.

Theorem 10
.

Let
G
be an orthogonality graph with freely completable realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
in
\dim(\mathcal{H})=d
and let
{\overline{G(\pi)}}
be the orthogonality graph of its faithful completion with respect to
\pi
. Then
{\overline{G(\pi)}}
is a SI-C graph only if
\chi(\overline{G(d)}^{*})>d
.

Moreover, if
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{H})}
then
{\overline{G(\pi)}}
is a SI-C graph only if
\chi(G)>d
.

Proof.

The first part follows from Thm.
9
since
\overline{\pi}:{\overline{G(\pi)}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
is a unital realisation and since
{\overline{G(\pi)}}=\overline{G(d)}
, by assumption. The second assertion follows from Lm.
7
.
∎

Clearly, this also implies a partial characterisation of SI-C sets.

Corollary 5
.

A necessary condition for the faithful completion
\overline{\pi(V)}
of a (freely completable) quantum realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
of an orthogonality graph
G
in dimension
\dim(\mathcal{H})=d
to be a state-independent contextuality (SI-C) set is that
\chi(\overline{G(d)}^{*})>d
.

If moreover
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{H})}
, then
\overline{\pi(V)}
is a SI-C set only if
\chi(G)>d
.

Proof.

Since the orthogonality graph of a SI-C set is a SI-C graph, the result follows immediately from Thm.
10
.
∎

Importantly, the statements in Thm.
10
and Cor.
5
do not hold without assuming (faithful) completions. Since this creates a conflict with the results in Ref.
[
160
,
51
]
, we elaborate on it in more detail in App.
F
. In turn, under the restriction to freely completable realisations, Thm.
10
and Cor.
5
generalise Thm. 4 in Ref.
[
51
]
which is also implied by Thm. 2 in Ref.
[
160
]
. Finally, we remark that our proofs of Thm.
10
and Cor.
5
are independent from the ones given in Ref.
[
51
]
and Ref.
[
160
]
, as we derived the characterisation of state-independent contextuality (in terms of the chromatic number of an orthogonality graph) from the algebraic characterisation of KS noncontextuality in Thm.
1
, in particular, its reformulation in Thm.
2
and Thm.
3
.

We finish this section by briefly commenting on the distinction between SI-C graphs and SI-C sets. Note that SI-C sets and SI-C graphs are distinguished by the order of quantifiers over realisations and states in Def.
26
and Def.
27
, which in general do not commute due to the existence of classical states: clearly, if
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H})|_{{\mathrm{Stab}}({\overline{G(\pi)}})}=\Gamma_{\mathrm{qu}}[{\mathcal{O}}(\pi(V))]\cap{\mathrm{Stab}}({\overline{G(\pi)}})\neq\emptyset
then
\overline{\pi(V)}
is not a SI-C set. Nevertheless, there exist SI-C graphs with
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H})|_{{\mathrm{Stab}}({\overline{G(\pi)}})}\neq\emptyset
, for which therefore no realisation is a SI-C set
[
51
]
. Such examples arise since Def.
27
allows to choose different realisations (hence, different noncontextuality inequalities) for different quantum states, which renders “SI-C” graphs
“state-independent on an operational level”

[
51
]
. We can see explicitly how the notion of SI-C graph applies to KS contextual partial algebras with
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H})|_{{\mathrm{Stab}}({\overline{G(\pi)}})}\neq\emptyset
. Namely, Ref.
[
160
]
introduce a measure of contextuality in terms of the distance of a quantum state
\rho\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H})
from the set
{\mathrm{Stab}}({\overline{G(\pi)}})
over
all
realisations.
88
In particular, given a realisation
\pi
of
G
this includes the unitarily rotated realisations
\pi_{U}=U\pi U^{*}
for every unitary
U\in{\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H})}
. Consequently, Def.
27
implicitly assumes that the embedding is with respect to a simple quantum algebra
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
. Yet,
G
may have a (minimal) realisation with respect to a non-simple algebra, that is, with respect to an algebra with superselection rules (for more details, see App.
E
). In this case, allowing for arbitrary, specifically unitarily rotated realisations is indeed not justified and, consequently, the argument in Thm. 1 in Ref.
[
160
]
, which reduces the problem to analysing the violation of noncontextuality inequalities by the maximally mixed state, no longer applies.

4.3.3
A positive resolution of the conjecture in Ref.
[
160
]

In this final section, we address a question raised in Ref.
[
160
]
: whether
\chi(G)>d
is a necessary and sufficient condition for state-independent contextuality (in Def.
28
) for the completion
{\overline{G(\pi)}}
of the orthogonality graph
G
with respect to a realisation
\pi
as in Eq. (
15
). The failure of the converse of Thm.
9
shows that this is not true even in the case of orthogonality graphs with unital realisation, for which the completion in Eq. (
15
) is trivial, while Thm.
10
refutes it for orthogonality graphs with freely completable realisation. However, by comparing with Thm.
8
, we find that for the notion of contextual orthogonality graph in Def.
24
the conjecture holds true.

Theorem 11
.

Let
G
be an orthogonality graph with freely completable realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
in
\dim(\mathcal{H})=d
and let
{\overline{G(\pi)}}
be the orthogonality graph of its faithful completion with respect to
\pi
. Then
{\overline{G(\pi)}}
is contextual if and only if
\chi(\overline{G(d)}^{*})>d
.

Moreover, if
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{H})}
then
{\overline{G(\pi)}}
is contextual if and only if
\chi(G)>d
.

Proof.

Since a contextual graph is also a SI-C graph, the “only if”-directions both follow from Thm.
10
(equivalently, from Cor.
3
and Lm.
7
). The first part of the converse assertion holds by Thm.
8
and Thm.
3
, and the second additionally by Lm.
7
.
∎

In particular, Thm.
11
shows that under the restriction to realisations with faithful completion and one-dimensional projections the colouring problem in Thm.
3
can be reduced to the chromatic number of subgraphs
G\subset{\overline{G(\pi)}}
.

We re-emphasise that the assumptions in Thm.
11
cannot be omitted: (i) the restriction of SI-C to contextual graphs is necessary for the converse of Thm.
10
, while (ii) the restriction to (faithful) completions is required already for Thm.
10
(see App.
F
).

Thm.
11
completes our comparison between SI-C sets, SI-C graphs and Kochen-Specker contextuality. To summarise, recall that the existence of SI-C sets that are not KS sets such as in Ref.
[
188
]
indicated that the traditional notion of KS contextuality had to be superceded. In Ref.
[
76
]
we show that this conclusion is misguided, since the existence of KS sets is only a necessary but not sufficient criterion to prove KS contextuality (see also Sec.
2.4
). Yet, Ref.
[
76
]
does not fully resolve the relationship between the respective notions. Here, we provided a full comparison: first, we showed that the notions of SI-C set in Def.
26
and SI-C graph in Def.
27
differ from that of Kochen-Specker contextuality for observable algebras in Def.
7
- even under the restriction to unital realisations (by Thm.
9
and Cor.
4
); second, we independently proved that the chromatic number of an orthogonality graph still remains a necessary criterion for state-independent contextuality - provided it has a unital quantum realisation (Thm.
9
and Cor.
4
) or one with a faithful completion (Thm.
10
and Cor.
5
, see also App.
F
); third, we found that for contextual orthogonality graphs (Def.
24
) the chromatic number provides a complete invariant, thus leading to a positive version of the conjecture in Ref.
[
160
]
. Together, this fully resolves the subtle differences between the respective notions of contextuality. Tab.
6
summarises these various relationships.

Table 6
:
The graph-theoretic approach in Ref.
[
52
]
aims to characterise contextuality purely in terms of a (finite) orthogonality graph
G
. At a minimum, this requires
G
to admit a realisation with respect to some
d
-dimensional partial algebra, which is equivalent to the existence of a dimension function with
\dim(\mathbb{I})=d
(see App.
B
). Already at this general level, the condition
\chi(G^{*})>d
of orthogonality graphs
G
with unital realisation is a complete invariant of KS contextuality by Cor.
3
(top row), and restricts to a necessary criterion for SI-C graphs with unital quantum realisation (second row). More generally,
\chi(\overline{G(d)}^{*})>d
remains a necessary criterion for SI-C graphs
G
with freely completable (f.c.) realisation (third row), which further carries over to SI-C sets (bottom row). Finally, for realisations restricted to one-dimensional projections, the respective conditions further simplify since
\chi(G)\leq d
if and only if
\chi(\overline{G(d)}^{*})\leq d
by Lm.
7
.

5
Conclusion

Summary (see also Tab.
1
).
We proved a new and complete characterisation of Kochen-Specker (KS) contextuality
[
127
]
(Thm.
1
), based on the concept of context connections (Def.
8
) and the constraints encoded by them along context cycles (Def.
9
), which were first introduced in Ref.
[
76
]
. Our result applies at the level of generality (and beyond that) of the original definition, which applied to partial algebras.

Given this generality, the comparison with various other frameworks becomes possible, and we have outlined in detail the subtle differences with various notions of “contextuality” used in the marginal and (hyper)graph-theoretic approaches
[
40
]
. In particular, we highlighted the difference between KS contextuality and the violation of noncontextuality inequalities: while the latter is characterised in terms of acyclic partial orders of contexts by Vorob’ev’s theorem
[
179
,
185
]
(Thm.
6
), that is, by the existence of a single non-trivial context cycle
[
17
]
, the former generally requires constraints across several contexts cycles (Thm.
7
). Nevertheless, KS contextuality can be phrased as a colouring problem (Thm.
3
), which explains why traditional proofs of the Kochen-Specker theorem in terms of KS sets and colourings do not always capture KS contextuality:
d
-colourability (Def.
11
) implies KS colourability (Def.
10
), but the converse is generally false
[
188
,
76
]
. Moreover, we have fully explored the relation between
d
-colourability and existing partial characterisations of contextuality in the graph-theoretic approach, based on the chromatic number of orthogonality graphs. Specifically, we gave new and refined proofs of the results in Ref.
[
46
,
51
]
, and resolved the conjecture raised in Ref.
[
160
]
.

Outlook.
The unified perspective offered by observable algebras, paired with the effectiveness of the concept of context connections - introduced first in Ref.
[
76
]
, and explored in more detail here - strongly suggests that this novel approach is a fruitful one also when considering applications of KS contextuality. We finish by mentioning a few.

For one thing, analysing constraints on context connections offers a complementary approach to the common study of noncontextuality inequalities, which is mainly concerned with methods from convex geometry. It may thus lead to new insights in characterising noncontextuality polytopes of quantum measurement scenarios, e.g. with respect to optimal noncontextuality inequalities for experimental tests
[
25
,
121
,
14
,
92
,
138
,
130
,
181
]
.

Yet, our formalism is not tied to quantum theory; and one motivation to study general observable algebras is the question of how to characterise quantum observable algebras among more general ones - explicitly, when expressed in terms of the constraints these pose on context connections. Since our formalism is amenable to various related structures in quantum logic
[
159
,
126
,
9
]
and general probabilistic theories
[
140
,
156
,
139
]
, it may thus offer a new perspective towards identifying properties that are essentially quantum, in line with the reconstruction programme of quantum theory
[
98
,
97
,
140
]
.

On a more formal level, the algebraic characterisation of contextuality in Thm.
1
may shed new light on the nature of KS contextuality as a geometric obstruction to a classical state space picture
[
19
,
120
,
175
]
. In particular, it is tempting to interpret context connections as (generalisations of) connections in differentiable geometry.

Of important practical interest is to apply our new tools towards a fine-grained characterisation of contextuality as a resource in quantum computation
[
15
,
162
,
107
,
37
,
80
,
81
]
, quantum cryptography
[
72
,
31
,
56
,
13
,
168
]
, quantum machine learning
[
124
,
73
,
50
,
85
,
16
,
36
]
, quantum metrology
[
109
,
110
]
etc. In future work, we will compare constraints on context connections with tools from algebraic topology that have been successfully applied to characterise contextuality in measurement-based quantum computation
[
163
,
146
,
147
,
144
]
, as well as sheaf-cohomological methods
[
10
,
54
,
26
,
6
]
and the study of (quantum solutions to) linear constraint systems in
[
18
,
60
,
144
,
79
]
. To this end, we wish to promote the present techniques to quantitative tools for the analysis of contextuality necessary, e.g. as need for specific computational tasks. We believe this is a key effort towards provable quantum advantage in quantum computing and beyond.

Appendix A
Proof of Thm.
1

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we choose non-degenerate observables
O
for every maximal context
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, that is,
C=C(O)
with identical spectra
\mathrm{sp}(O)=[d]=\{0,\cdots,d-1\}
for
d=\dim(\mathbb{I})
.
65
65

65

Here,

is any
d

d
-dimensional quantum system, we may take the observables to correspond to (Stern-Gerlach) measurements on a spin-
\frac{d-1}{2}
particle with
\mathrm{sp}(O)=\{-\frac{d-1}{2},\cdots,\frac{d-1}{2}\}
.
Importantly, note that this still does not fix
O
uniquely, but only up to a permutation on its spectral decomposition (equivalently, pre-composition of
O:\Sigma_{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by a permutation on
\Sigma_{C}
).

\epsilon
maps the projections
p\in{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
to measurable subsets
\Lambda_{p}\subset\Lambda
such that (possibly up to negligible sets)
\Lambda_{0}=\emptyset
,
\Lambda_{\mathbb{I}}=\Lambda
and
\Lambda_{p+p^{\prime}}=\Lambda_{p}\dot{\cup}\Lambda_{p^{\prime}}
whenever
pp^{\prime}=0
. For every observable
O\in{\mathcal{O}}
with spectral decomposition
O=\sum_{\sigma\in[d]}\sigma p_{\sigma}
,
p_{\sigma}\in{\mathcal{P}_{1}({\mathcal{O}})}
,
\epsilon
therefore induces a partition
\Lambda=\dot{\cup}_{\sigma\in[d]}\Lambda^{O}_{\sigma}
into measurable subsets
\Lambda^{O}_{\sigma}
, which yield the outcome
\sigma\in\mathrm{sp}(O)
under evaluation of
f_{O}:=\epsilon(O):\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, where
{\mathrm{im}}(f_{O})=\mathrm{sp}(O)=[d]
.
66
66

66

Formally,
\epsilon|_{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}:{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}(\Lambda)
defines an orthomorphism into the complete Boolean
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathcal{B}}(\Lambda)
.

Consider two non-degenerate observables
O,O^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}}
, and let
\phi_{O^{\prime}O}:\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
be a measurable function such that
\phi_{O^{\prime}O}(\Lambda^{O}_{\sigma})\subset\Lambda^{O^{\prime}}_{\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma}
for all
\sigma\in[d]
. Clearly, this definition depends on the choice of generating observables for the contexts
C=C(O),C^{\prime}=C(O^{\prime})
. In other words, it depends on a choice of bijection
l_{O^{\prime}O}:\mathcal{P}_{1}(C)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{1}(C^{\prime})
. We may thus write
\phi_{O^{\prime}O}=\phi^{l_{C^{\prime}C}}_{C^{\prime}C}
, and
\phi^{\mathfrak{l}}=(\phi^{l_{C^{\prime}C}}_{C^{\prime}C})_{C,C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}}
for a collection of maps between maximal contexts, which depends on the context connection
\mathfrak{l}=(l_{C^{\prime}C})_{C,C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}}
. We will derive constraints on context connections
\mathfrak{l}
by concatenating the maps in
\phi^{\mathfrak{l}}
along context cycles
(C_{0},\cdots,C_{n-1})
, in which case we will further abbreviate our notation to
\phi^{l_{(i+1)i}}_{(i+1)i}:=\phi^{l_{C_{(i+1)}C_{i}}}_{C_{(i+1)}C_{i}}=\phi_{O_{(i+1)}O_{i}}
for
C_{i}=C(O_{i})
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.

To this end, note first that
\phi_{O_{(i+1)}O_{i}}
can generally not be chosen one-to-one, since
\Lambda^{O_{i}}_{\sigma_{i}=\sigma}
and
\Lambda^{O_{(i+1)}}_{\sigma_{i+1}=\sigma}
will generally have different cardinalities. Nevertheless, by restricting to measurable subsets
\tilde{\Lambda}^{O_{i}}_{\sigma_{i}}\subset\Lambda^{O_{i}}_{\sigma_{i}}
such that there exist injections
\tilde{\Lambda}^{O_{i}}_{\sigma_{i}}\hookrightarrow\Lambda^{O_{j}}_{\sigma_{j}=\sigma_{i}}
for all
\sigma_{i}\in[d]
and
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, we can always find invertible (measurable) maps
\phi_{O_{(i+1)}O_{i}}:\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
such that
\phi_{O_{(i+1)}O_{i}}(\tilde{\Lambda}^{O_{i}}_{\sigma_{i}})=\tilde{\Lambda}^{O_{(i+1)}}_{\sigma_{(i+1)}=\sigma_{i}}
for all
\sigma_{i}\in[d]
and such that
\phi_{O_{(i+1)}O_{i}}|_{\Lambda/\tilde{\Lambda}^{O_{i}}}=\mathrm{id}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, where
\tilde{\Lambda}^{O_{i}}=\dot{\cup}_{\sigma_{i}\in[d]}\tilde{\Lambda}^{O_{i}}_{\sigma_{i}}
.
67
67

67

Indeed, we may choose
\tilde{\Lambda}^{O}_{\sigma}\subset\Lambda^{O}_{\sigma}
to be of cardinality
\min_{O^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}}}|\Lambda^{O^{\prime}}_{\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma}|
for all
\sigma\in[d]
and
O\in{\mathcal{O}}
.

Denote by
f_{i}=\epsilon(O_{i})
the measurable function on
\Lambda
representing the observable
O_{i}
under the classical embedding
\epsilon
. Then
f_{(i+1)}|_{\tilde{\Lambda}^{O_{(i+1)}}}=f_{i}|_{\tilde{\Lambda}^{O_{i}}}\circ(\phi^{l_{(i+1)i}}_{(i+1)i})^{-1}
, hence,
68
68

68

What is more, since
\phi^{l_{(i+1)i}}_{(i+1)i}|_{\Lambda/\tilde{\Lambda}^{O_{i}}}=\mathrm{id}
we also have
f_{0}\circ\left(\circ_{i=0}^{n-1}\phi^{l_{(i+1)i}}_{(i+1)i}\right)^{-1}=f_{0}
.

f_{0}|_{\tilde{\Lambda}^{O_{0}}}\circ\left(\circ_{i=0}^{n-1}\phi^{l_{(i+1)i}}_{(i+1)i}\right)^{-1}=f_{0}|_{\tilde{\Lambda}^{O_{0}}}\ \ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ \circ_{i=0}^{n-1}\phi^{l_{(i+1)i}}_{(i+1)i}={\mathrm{id}}\ \ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ \circ_{i=0}^{n-1}l_{(i+1)i}={\mathrm{id}}\;,

where we used that
f_{0}=\epsilon(O_{0})
is non-degenerate with respect to the
\{\Lambda^{O_{0}}_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[d]}
-partition (
O_{0}
is non-degenerate and
{\mathrm{im}}(f_{0})=\mathrm{sp}(O_{0})=[d])
. Since we constructed
\phi^{\mathfrak{l}}
simply by assuming the existence of a classical embedding
\epsilon
, the result follows.

Conversely, let
\mathfrak{l}
be a flat context connection on
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, that is,
\mathfrak{l}
satisfies the triviality constraints in Eq. (
4
) for every context cycle in
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}

\Sigma_{C_{0}}:=\{\hat{\sigma}_{p}:\mathcal{P}_{1}(C_{0})\rightarrow\{0,1\}\mid\forall p,q\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(C_{0}):\ \hat{\sigma}_{p}(q)=\delta_{pq}\}
be the “state space” of
C_{0}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
.
69
69

69

\hat{\sigma}_{p}\in\Sigma_{C_{0}}
defines a state on
C_{0}
by linear extension,

for all

For any

by setting

, (where the action of

on

) and, second, by
\lambda_{{\widetilde{C}}|C}:=\lambda_{C}|_{{\widetilde{C}}}
for all

whenever

.
70
70

70

In other words,

[

]

\Lambda:=\{\lambda^{\mathfrak{l},\lambda_{C_{0}}}\mid\lambda_{C_{0}}\in\Sigma_{C_{0}},\mathfrak{l}\in\Xi_{\mathrm{cl}}({{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})})\}\;.

(16)

. Clearly, this defines a measurable function: since the spectrum of
