Appendix B
Finite-dimensional observable algebras

In Ref.
[
76
]
, context connections on the partial order of contexts of a (spin-
1
) quantum system were defined as bijective maps between rank-
1
projections. App.
A
generalises Thm. 2 in Ref.
[
76
]
to maximal observable algebras (see Def.
6
), where - similar to the quantum case - minimal projections all have the “same size”. However, not every observable algebra is maximal, in particular, minimal events may be of “different size”. In this section, we will extend the result (and thus the proof of Thm.
1
) to finite-dimensional observable algebras, for which a notion of size (see Def.
5
) still exists.

We first characterise maximal observable algebras in terms of maximally mixed states. Let
\dim
be a dimension function on
{\mathcal{O}}
and define the map
\gamma_{\dim}:{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}
by
\gamma_{\dim}(p)=\frac{\dim(p)}{\dim(\mathbb{I})}
for all
p\in{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Then
\gamma_{\dim}\in\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
since the marginalisation constraints in Eq. (
5
) hold by additivity of
\dim
, and
\gamma_{\dim}(\mathbb{I})=\frac{\dim(\mathbb{I})}{\dim(\mathbb{I})}=1
is normalised.
71
71

71

For finitely generated observable algebras the converse also holds: if
\gamma\in\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
with
\gamma(p)\in\mathbb{Q}
for all
p\in{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
, then we obtain a dimension function by setting
\dim(p)=L\gamma(p)
, where
L
is the least common multiple of all denominators in
\gamma(p)
.

Lemma 8
.

An observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
is maximal if and only if it contains the maximally mixed state
\gamma_{\mathbb{I}/d}\in\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
with
\gamma_{\mathbb{I}/d}(p)=\frac{1}{d}
for all
p\in{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}})}
and
d=\dim(\mathbb{I})
.

Proof.

If
{\mathcal{O}}
is maximal and of dimension
\dim(\mathbb{I})=d
, then the state
\gamma_{\mathbb{I}/d}(p)=\frac{\dim(p)}{d}
is maximally mixed since
\dim(p)=1
for all
p\in{\mathcal{P}_{1}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Conversely, if
{\mathcal{O}}
contains the maximally mixed state
\gamma_{\mathbb{I}/d}\in\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
, defined by
\gamma_{\mathbb{I}/d}(p)=\frac{1}{d}
for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}})
, then the canonical dimension function on
{\mathcal{O}}
is given by
\dim(p)=d\gamma_{\mathbb{I}/d}(p)
.
∎

While the existence of a maximally mixed state may seem a trivial assumption, there exist finitely generated observable algebras that do not admit such a state, and indeed ones that admit no states at all
[
88
]
. By Lm.
8
, such observable algebras do not have a dimension function.
72
72

72

For finitely generated observable algebras, the additive constraints of a dimension function define a linear set of Diophantine equations (see Eq. (
18
) in App.
E
). The existence of a dimension function thus coincides with the existence of a solution to this set of equations which can be checked efficiently.

Maximal extensions of observable algebras.
Next, we show that observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
may have a dimension function yet not be maximal, and may even support different dimension functions. For instance, given a set of minimal events
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
with
\dim(p_{i})=1
for
i=\{1,2,3\}
for some context
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
of some maximal observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
such that
p_{i}p=0
only if
p\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
. Then the “coarse-grained” observable algebra
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}
, which instead of
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}
only contains the events
{\widetilde{p}}_{1},{\widetilde{p}}_{2}
and is otherwise identical, admits at least two dimension functions: (i)
\widetilde{\dim}({\widetilde{p}}_{1})=1
,
\widetilde{\dim}({\widetilde{p}}_{2})=2
and
\widetilde{\dim}(p)=\dim(p)
for all
p\in{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}/\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\}
and (ii)
\widetilde{\dim}^{\prime}({\widetilde{p}}_{1})=2
,
\widetilde{\dim}^{\prime}({\widetilde{p}}_{2})=1
and
\widetilde{\dim}^{\prime}(p)=\dim(p)
for all
p\in{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}/\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\}
.

Observable algebras of this type arise, for instance, from measurement scenarios (see Def.
32
in App.
D.1
), where the (discrete) outcomes sets of measurement labels generally do not have the same cardinality. In order to deal with such cases, we need to generalise Thm.
1
from maximal to general (finite-dimensional) observable algebras. The basic idea is to embed finite-dimensional observable algebras within maximal ones.

Definition 30
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an observable algebra with dimension function
\dim
. Then the
maximal extension
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
of
{\mathcal{O}}
with respect to
\dim
is a maximal observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
of dimension
\dim(\mathbb{I}^{*})=\dim(\mathbb{I})
, together with an embedding
\iota:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
such that:

(i)

for all
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
there exists a unique maximal context
C^{*}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}}^{*})}
such that
C\subset C^{*}
(and
C=C^{*}
if
C
is minimally generated by
\dim(\mathbb{I})
elements),

(ii)

for all
C^{\prime*}\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}}^{*})}
either
C^{\prime*}\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
or
C^{\prime*}\subset C^{*}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}}^{*})}
, where
C^{*}
is the unique maximal context of some maximal context
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
by (i),

(iii)

for all
C,C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
with
C\neq C^{\prime}
it holds
C\cap C^{\prime}=C^{*}\cap C^{\prime*}
.

Conditions (i) and (ii) in Def.
30
define maximal extensions to be minimal under all embeddings of
{\mathcal{O}}
into maximal observable algebras. Furthermore, if (iii) was not true, then there would exist maximal contexts
C,C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
with
C\cap C^{\prime}\subsetneq C^{*}\cap C^{\prime*}
, that is, there would exist
\mathcal{P}(C^{*}),\mathcal{P}(C^{\prime*})\ni p^{*}\notin{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
such that
p^{*}p=p^{*}p^{\prime}=0
for all
p\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
and
p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(C^{\prime})
. Consequently,
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
would therefore pose additional constraints (not present in
{\mathcal{O}}
) between elements in
different
maximal contexts in
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
since
p^{*}\leq(\mathbb{I}-p),(\mathbb{I}-p^{\prime})
for all
p\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
and
p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(C^{\prime})
. (iii) thus ensures that
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
already contains all relevant (noncontextuality) constraints between elements in
{\mathcal{O}}
.

The following lemma shows that a maximal extension always exists and is unique.

Lemma 9
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an observable algebra with partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
and dimension function
\mathrm{dim}:{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
. Then
{\mathcal{O}}
has a unique maximal extension
\iota:{\mathcal{O}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
with maximal observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
, corresponding partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}}^{*})}
and dimension function
\dim^{*}:\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}}^{*})\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that
\dim^{*}|_{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}=\dim
.

Proof.

Write
|C|:=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}(C)
for the cardinality of a minimal generating set of
C
. For every maximal context
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, define
\iota_{C^{*}C}:C\hookrightarrow C^{*}
to be an embedding of
C
into a commutative algebra
C^{*}
with
|C^{*}|=\dim(\mathbb{I})
and such that
\dim^{*}(\iota_{C^{*}C}(p))=\dim(p)
for all
p\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
. Then
\iota_{C^{*}C}
is the unique (up to isomorphism) extension of
C
such that for every
p\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
with
\dim(p)=k
there exist projections
p^{*}_{1},\cdots,p^{*}_{k}\in\mathcal{P}(C^{*})
with
\dim^{*}(p^{*}_{i})=1
,
\sum_{i=1}^{k}p^{*}_{i}=\iota_{C^{*}C}(p)
. Clearly, (i) and (ii) in Def.
30
are satisfied under this definition. Moreover,
p^{*}_{i}p=0
for
p\in{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
if and only if
p\in{\mathcal{P}(C)}
by construction such that
C\cap C^{\prime}=C^{*}\cap C^{\prime*}
for any two distinct maximal contexts
C,C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Hence, (iii) in Def.
30
also holds and
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
is a maximal observable, is clearly unique and
\dim^{*}:\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}}^{*})\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
defines a dimension function on
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
such that
\dim^{*}|_{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}=\dim
.
∎

Using Lm.
9
, we can reduce the study of KS contextuality of (non-maximal) finite-dimensional observable algebras to that of their maximal extensions.

Lemma 10
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a finite-dimensional observable algebra. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

{\mathcal{O}}
is Kochen-Specker noncontextual,

(ii)

there exists a maximal extension
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
as by Lm.
9
with respect to some choice of dimension function
\dim
on
{\mathcal{O}}
that is Kochen-Specker noncontextual,

(iii)

the maximal extension
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
as by Lm.
9
with respect to any choice of dimension function
\dim
on
{\mathcal{O}}
is Kochen-Specker noncontextual.

Proof.

The implications (iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i) are obvious. Indeed, let
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
be the unique maximal observable algebra of
{\mathcal{O}}
in Lm.
9
with respect to any dimension function. If
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
is KS noncontextual then there exists a classical embedding
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}^{*}\rightarrow L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
for some measurable space
\Lambda
which induces an embedding
\epsilon|_{\mathcal{O}}=\epsilon\circ\iota:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
for
{\mathcal{O}}
by restriction. Hence,
{\mathcal{O}}
is KS noncontextual.

To prove (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii), assume that
{\mathcal{O}}
is KS noncontextual, that is, there exists an embedding
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
for some measurable space
\Lambda
. Now, let
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
be the unique maximal observable algebra in Lm.
9
with respect to some dimension function
\dim
on
{\mathcal{O}}
. We need to show that every observable
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}\ni O^{*}\notin{\mathcal{O}}
can also be represented as a measurable function on
\Lambda
. To do so, it is sufficient to find a decomposition of the measurable subset
\Lambda_{p}={\mathrm{supp}}(\chi(\lambda\mid p))\subset{\mathcal{B}}(\Lambda)
, corresponding to the indicator function
\chi(\lambda\mid p)=\epsilon(p)(\lambda)
for every projection
p\in{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
with
\mathrm{dim}(p)=k>1
, into measurable subsets
\Lambda_{p^{*}_{i}}=:{\mathrm{supp}}(\chi(\lambda\mid p^{*}_{i}))
,
\Lambda_{p}=\dot{\cup}_{i=1}^{k}\Lambda_{p^{*}_{i}}
for
p^{*}_{1},\cdots,p^{*}_{k}
the unique one-dimensional projections in
\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}}^{*})
with
\sum_{i=1}^{k}p^{*}_{i}=\iota_{C^{*}C}(p)
(where
\iota_{C^{*}C}:C\hookrightarrow C^{*}
is the embedding defined by the maximal extension
\iota
in Lm.
9
). The only constraints on the subsets
\Lambda_{p^{*}_{i}}
are that they are measurable and that
\Lambda_{p^{*}_{i}}\subset\Lambda_{p^{\prime}}
only if
p^{*}_{i}\leq p^{\prime}
for
p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}}^{*})
.

Let
\sigma|_{\Lambda_{p}}=\{\widetilde{\Lambda}\cap\Lambda_{p}\mid\widetilde{\Lambda}\in\sigma_{\Lambda}\}
be the induced
\sigma
-algebra under the restriction to
\Lambda_{p}
. Moreover, define
\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}=\dot{\cap}_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}}\Lambda_{p^{\prime}}
as the intersection with respect to any subset of events
\mathcal{P}\subset{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Note that
\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\in\sigma_{\Lambda}
, in particular,
\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\in\sigma|_{\Lambda_{p}}
whenever
\mathcal{P}
is a countable set. If
{\mathcal{O}}
is finitely generated,
\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
is a finite intersection, hence,
\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
is itself a measurable subset for all
\mathcal{P}\subset{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
. We can therefore find a (finite) minimal generating set
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda_{p}}
for
\sigma|_{\Lambda_{p}}
such that
\Lambda_{p}=\dot{\cup}_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda_{p}}}\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
and
\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\cap\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}=\emptyset
for all
\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}},\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda_{p}}
.
73
73

73

Indeed, for finitely generated observable algebras we may assume that
\Lambda
is a finite set (see Lm.
5
).
After possibly enlarging each generating set
\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda_{p}}
such that it contains at least
k
elements, let
\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}=\dot{\cup}_{i=1}^{k}\Lambda_{\mathcal{P},i}
be a disjoint decomposition into (measurable) subsets for every
\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda_{p}}
, and define
\Lambda_{p^{*}_{i}}:=\dot{\cup}_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda_{p}}}\Lambda_{\mathcal{P},i}
. It follows that
\Lambda_{p}=\dot{\cup}_{i=1}^{k}\Lambda_{p^{*}_{i}}
and
\Lambda_{p^{*}_{i}}\subset\Lambda_{p^{\prime}}
only if
p^{*}_{i}\leq p^{\prime}
for
p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}}^{*})
by construction. Finally, setting
\epsilon^{*}(p^{*}_{i})=\Lambda_{p^{*}_{i}}
and
\epsilon^{*}|_{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}=\epsilon
we thus obtain a classical embedding for the maximal extension
{\mathcal{O}}^{*}
.

If
{\mathcal{O}}
is not finitely generated, the
\sigma
-algebra
\sigma_{\Lambda}
on
\Lambda
is not finitely generated either and the above construction does not work. In this case, we construct a new space
\Lambda^{\prime}
by replacing
\Lambda_{p}
in
\Lambda
by the product space
\Lambda_{p}\times[k]
for
\dim(p)=k
where
[k]
denotes a set of
k
elements with
\sigma
-algebra
\sigma_{p}(k)=2^{[k]}
. We then recover
\sigma|_{\Lambda_{p}}
as the projection onto the first factor in the product
\sigma
-algebra
\sigma|_{\Lambda_{p}}\times\sigma_{p}(k)
on
\Lambda_{p}\times[k]
, and since every measurable set
\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\in\sigma
decomposes as
\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}=(\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\cap\Lambda_{p})\cup(\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\cap\overline{\Lambda_{p}})
, we also recover
\sigma_{\Lambda}
by defining the
\sigma
-algebra
\sigma_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
on
\Lambda^{\prime}
as the smallest
\sigma
-algebra generated by the sets
\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}=((\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\cap\Lambda_{p})\times 1_{k})\cup(\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\cap\overline{\Lambda_{p}})
for all
\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\in\sigma
. We thus obtain a new classical embedding
\widetilde{\epsilon}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\Lambda^{\prime}
of
{\mathcal{O}}
. Moreover,
\widetilde{\epsilon}^{\prime}
is readily extended to the elements
p^{*}_{i}<p
in
\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}}^{*})
by setting
\epsilon^{\prime}(p^{*}_{i})=\Lambda_{p}\times\{i\}=:\Lambda_{p^{*}_{i}}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
. Clearly, we have
\Lambda_{p^{*}_{i}}\neq\Lambda_{q}
for any
q\in\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}}^{*})
by construction.

To prove (i)
\Rightarrow
(iii) it is enough to note that the construction in the previous paragraph is independent of, hence, applies to any choice of dimension function
\dim
on
{\mathcal{O}}
.
∎

Appendix C
Observable algebras with separating sets of states

Prop.
1
says that a KS noncontextual observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
is not necessarily fully classically correlated: the existence of a classical embedding implies
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]\neq\emptyset
, yet generally not
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]=\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
. On the other hand, this suggests that KS noncontextuality is a weaker notion than being fully classically correlated. In this section, we prove under which condition this is the case. We first show that it is not true in general.

Lemma 11
.

There exist observable algebras
{\mathcal{O}}
which are Kochen-Specker contextual, yet which are fully classically correlated, that is, for which
\emptyset\neq\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]=\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
.

Proof.

We will use the construction of state spaces for orthomodular lattices in Ref.
[
141
,
142
]
(and references therein). To this end, recall first that every orthomodular lattice defines a transitive partial Boolean algebra
[
91
]
, which in turn generates a unique partial algebra (see Lm.
1
). It follows that there exist orthomodular lattices
L
with exactly one state, which assigns every atom (and thus every minimal projection in
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
in the finitely generated, maximal observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}(L)
associated with
L
) the same value, hence, which is a maximally mixed state.

Let
L_{1}
be such an orthomodular lattice and denote by
\gamma_{\mathbb{I}}\in\Gamma[L_{1}]
the unique (maximally mixed) state on
L_{1}
. For instance, we may take
L_{1}
to be the orthomodular lattice in Ref.
[
141
]
. Next, consider its product
L=L_{1}\times L_{2}
with the Boolean algebra
L_{2}\cong{\mathcal{B}}(2^{\Lambda})
of measurable sets on some finite set
\Lambda
. In this case we have
\Gamma[L]\cong\Gamma[L_{2}]

[
143
]
.
L
defines a finitely generated, maximal observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}(L)
, in particular, note that
\gamma_{\mathbb{I}}
gives rise to a dimension function on
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
by Lm.
8
. Hence, we can meaningfully talk about Kochen-Specker contextuality,
74
74

74

In turn, for this reason we cannot use orthomodular lattices with no states
[
88
,
116
,
141
]
.
and clearly,
{\mathcal{O}}(L)
is Kochen-Specker contextual, since a classical embedding would define more than a single state on
L_{1}
, and thus
\Gamma[L_{2}]\subsetneq\Gamma[L_{1}\times L_{2}]
contradicting
\Gamma[L_{2}]\cong\Gamma[L_{1}\times L_{2}]
.

Still, every state
\gamma
on
L
has a solution to the marginal problem. Indeed, the respective (non-faithful) orthomorphisms in Def.
15
are simply given by the projection onto the second factor, that is,
\phi_{\gamma}:L_{1}\times L_{2}\rightarrow\mathbf{1}\times L_{2}\cong L_{2}
, where
\mathbf{1}
denotes the unique single-element Boolean algebra. Consequently,
\emptyset\neq\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{\mathcal{O}}(L)]=\Gamma[{\mathcal{O}}(L)]
, hence,
{\mathcal{O}}(L)
is fully classically correlated.
∎

Admittedly, the example in the proof of Lm.
11
is rather artificial, and relies on the vast generality of observable algebras (specifically, orthomodular lattices), as compared to observable subalgebras
{\mathcal{O}}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}

in quantum theory. Physically, observable algebras as in Lm.
11
violate the idea that contextuality, that is, the compatibility relations between observables in
{\mathcal{O}}
are
learnt
: if states cannot distinguish between projections in different contexts, then the structure of
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
cannot be inferred operationally alone.

In the same spirit, one may argue that the assumption of an embedding in Def.
7
should be restricted to observable algebras admitting sufficiently many states in order to distinguish the events in
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
in
{\mathcal{O}}
. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 31
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an observable algebra with event algebra
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
. A set of states
\Gamma\subset\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
is called
separating
if for any
p,p^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
there exists
\gamma\in\Gamma
with
\gamma(p)\neq\gamma(p^{\prime})
.
{\mathcal{O}}
is called
separating
if it admits a separating set of states.

We observe that if
{\mathcal{O}}
is finitely generated and separating, then it is also finite-dimensional. Note also that observable subalgebras
{\mathcal{O}}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
in quantum theory are separating. Moreover, if
{\mathcal{O}}
admits a classical embedding
\epsilon:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
every measure on
\Lambda
induces a state on
{\mathcal{O}}
. It follows that the existence of a separating set of
classical
states is a necessary condition for
{\mathcal{O}}
to be KS noncontextual.

In turn, under the assumptions that
{\mathcal{O}}
is finitely generated and separating, the implication “fully classically correlated
\Rightarrow
Kochen-Specker noncontextual” does indeed hold.

Proposition 2
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a finitely generated, separating and fully classically correlated observable algebra. Then
{\mathcal{O}}
is Kochen-Specker noncontextual.

Proof.

By assumption,
{\mathcal{O}}
contains a separating set of states, which is classical since
{\mathcal{O}}
is also fully classically correlated. Hence,
{\mathcal{O}}
is marginally KS noncontextual by Def.
18
, which further implies KS noncontextuality by Thm.
4
.
∎

Together with Prop.
1
, we thus find that for finitely generated, separating observable algebras, KS noncontextuality is a weaker notion of classicality than Def.
16
(see Tab.
3
).

Appendix D
Comparison of approaches and unified perspective

Unfortunately, the various approaches to (the marginal approach to) contextuality use slightly different mathematical structures, notations and conventions. The purpose of this section is threefold: first, to give a minimal account of the necessary terminology in order to collect all these approaches under one umbrella,
75
75

75

A similar effort was made in Ref.
[
40
]
, where the relevant notion was identified as a “marginal scenario”
[
57
,
82
]
. Here, we substantially expand this view: we add the complementary algebraic perspective on KS contextuality, provide a detailed comparison between the respective approaches and prove various new results including the minimal mathematical structure necessary to study KS contextuality.
second, to expose unnecessary redundancies inherent in various descriptions, and third, to prove that the formalism of observable algebras and their underlying partial orders of contexts subsumes the minimal account in the most economical fashion. The latter may thus be seen as a unified framework for Kochen-Specker contextuality, and we endorse it as such.

D.1
Measurement scenarios vs observable algebras

We begin by reviewing the notion of a measurement scenario in the sheaf-theoretic approach
[
8
]
. For a brief review of the sheaf approach, see Ref.
[
8
,
10
,
7
]
.

Definition 32
.

Let
X
be a finite set of
measurement labels
. Each label
x\in X
is assigned a discrete
outcome space
o_{x}\subset\mathbb{R}
.
76
76

76

For a generalisation to random variables with continuous spectra, see Ref.
[
22
]
.
A
measurement cover
\mathcal{M}\subset 2^{X}
of
X
is a set of subsets of
X
such that (i)
\mathcal{M}
covers
X
, that is,
\bigcup\mathcal{M}=X
, and (ii)
\mathcal{M}
is an anti-chain, that is, whenever
{\mathrm{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathrm{C}}
for
{\mathrm{C}},{\mathrm{C}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
then
{\mathrm{C}}^{\prime}={\mathrm{C}}
. A pair
M=(X,\mathcal{M})
is called a
measurement scenario
.

Any subset
\tilde{{\mathrm{C}}}\subset X
that can be written as
\tilde{{\mathrm{C}}}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}{\mathrm{C}}_{i}
for
{\mathrm{C}}_{i}\in\mathcal{M}
is called a
measurement context
. In particular, the elements
{\mathrm{C}}\in\mathcal{M}
are called
maximal measurement contexts
. The set of all measurement contexts is denoted
{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{M})
with
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\mathcal{M})=\mathcal{M}
.

We will distinguish measurement contexts in the sheaf-theoretic approach, that is, subsets of
X
from contexts (commutative algebras in observable algebras) by using the non-italic font for the former.

It is usually not explicitly assumed that
X
contains a measurement label
I\in X
corresponding to the trivial observable which has outcome set
o_{I}=\{1\}
77
77

77

Measuring
I
always results in the outcome
1
(“true”). Its interpretation lies in defining the physical system with respect to which all other measurements
x\in X
are performed.
and is contained in every context, hence, defines a least element
{\mathrm{C}}_{I}=\{I\}
of
{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{M})
. Nevertheless, this assumption is made implicitly when considering states (“empirical models”) (see Def.
14
), where it appears in the form of the normalisation condition on probability distributions across contexts. Indeed, normalisation follows from
\mu_{\mathrm{C}}(1)=\mu_{\mathrm{C}}|_{{\mathrm{C}}_{I}}(1)=1
if
{\mathrm{C}}_{I}\subset{\mathrm{C}}
for all
{\mathrm{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{M})
. For this reason, we will henceforth explicitly include the measurement label
I\in X
for the trivial observable, equivalently, a least element
{\mathrm{C}}_{I}
in
{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{M})
.

The collection of all (not necessarily maximal) contexts is sometimes also called a
marginal scenario
{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{M})\subset 2^{X}

[
57
,
82
,
40
]
which satisfies (i) in Def.
32
and instead of (ii) is closed under subsets, that is, if
{\mathrm{C}},{\mathrm{C}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}
with
{\mathrm{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathrm{C}}
then
{\mathrm{C}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}
. A measurement cover encodes which measurement labels in
X
are compatible. This information can further equivalently be encoded in terms of the
compatibility graph
\mathcal{G}(X)=(X,\circ\subset X\times X)
of
X
where
x\circ x^{\prime}
if and only if
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathrm{C}\in\mathcal{M}
. Indeed, given
\mathcal{G}(X)
we recover
\mathcal{M}
from
X\supset\mathrm{C}\in\mathcal{M}
if and only if
x\circ x^{\prime}
for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathrm{C}
and
\mathrm{C}
is maximal.
78
78

78

For two self-adjoint operators
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
in quantum theory, we have
x\circ x^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow[x,x^{\prime}]=0
.
In essence, measurement covers, marginal scenarios and compatibility graphs all define a partial order, represented in terms of subsets of a set (measurement labels
X
of
M
or vertices
V
of
\mathcal{G}(X)
). Clearly, this partial order is the analogue of the partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
for observable algebras
{\mathcal{O}}
. However, unlike observable algebras, measurement scenarios and compatibility graphs are concerned with a particular choice of measurements. In general, this adds a level of redundancy to the presentation, which as we now show relates to a choice of (generally non-minimal) generating set
\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}})
of
{\mathcal{O}}
.

Measurement scenarios as generating sets of observable algebras.
At first sight, by removing the full algebraic structure in commutative algebras
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
of an observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
, the sheaf-theoretic approach seems to achieve a significant reduction and thus a more economical representation of a “contextuality scenario”. However, this intuition turns out to be misleading as we will show in this section.

To see this, we first show that a (finitely generated) observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
specifies a measurement scenario through (subsets of observables that constitute) a generating set.

Definition 33
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a finitely generated observable algebra with partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Then
\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}})=\{O_{1},\cdots,O_{N}\}
is called a
generating set for
{\mathcal{O}}
if
{\mathcal{O}}\ni O=\sum_{k=1}^{|S_{O}|}c_{k}h_{k}(O_{k})
for some
c_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
,
h_{k}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
O_{k}\in\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}})
. A generating set
\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}})
is called
minimal
if
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}})\subset\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}})
is a generating set for
{\mathcal{O}}
only if
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}})=\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}})
.

Definition 34
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a finitely generated observable algebra with maximal set of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
and generating set
\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}})
. Then
M(\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}}))=(X,\mathcal{M})
with
X=\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}})
and
\mathcal{M}={{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
is called the
measurement scenario associated with
\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}})
.

Clearly, the measurement scenario associated with an observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
depends on the choice of generating set
\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}})
. At the same time, by the definition of a generating set, each choice defines the same partial order of contexts. Since the latter is the only relevant structure (from the perspective of KS contextuality), we see that specifying a partial order of contexts in terms of measurement scenarios is generally redundant.

Nevertheless, there are certain canonical choices of generating sets, which we have encountered before: (i) for every maximal context
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, let
O\in C
be a non-degenerate observable such that
C=C(O)=\{h(O)\mid h:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\}
. We have been using such generating sets in the arguments leading up to Thm.
1
. Recall that KS noncontextuality may be seen as the impossibility to choose a generating observable in every maximal context. (ii) for every minimal context
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\circ})}
, let
O\in C
be a non-degenerate observable such that
C=C(O)=\{h(O)\mid h:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\}
. Both
\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}})=\{O_{C}\}_{C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\circ})}}
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}})=\{O_{C}\}_{C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}}
are minimal generating sets for
{\mathcal{O}}
. In particular, (the set of elementary observables corresponding with)
{\mathcal{P}_{1}({\mathcal{O}})}
forms a minimal generating set for maximal observable algebras. More generally,
{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}})}
is a minimal generating set for coarse-grainged observable algebras (see Lm.
21
below). Generating sets of this form (for a canonical choice of outcome sets
\{0,1\}
) justify the (hyper)graph-theoretic approach
[
52
,
57
,
82
,
11
,
12
]
(see App.
D.2
below).

However, the above are not the only minimal generating sets. Indeed, there are even infinitely many generating sets already of the form (i) and (ii), whose observables merely differ by a choice of invertible function
\{h_{C}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\}_{C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}}
, that is, by a choice of re-labelling of their outcome sets. Such re-labellings play no role in the sheaf-theoretic formalism to contextuality, and may thus be considered as “gauge”. This again emphasises that functional relations between observables may be seen as part of the side-processing of an experiment. Removing this ambiguity therefore naturally leads back to the perspective of observable algebras and their partial order of contexts defined in terms of commutative subalgebras as presented here.

Observable algebras associated with measurement scenarios.
We complete our comparison between our formalism and the sheaf-theoretic one by showing that observable algebras subsume measurement scenarios, that is, given a measurement scenario
M=(X,\mathcal{M})
, there always exists an observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
and a generating set
\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}})
such that
M=M(\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}}))
. One way to see this is by noting that any partial order with least element corresponds to the partial order of contexts of some observable algebra. An explicit construction is given in the following lemma.

Lemma 12
.

Let
M=(X,\mathcal{M})
be a measurement scenario. Then there exists a unique observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
with generating set
\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}})
such that
M=M(\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{O}}))
.

Proof.

We need to represent the measurement labels
x\in X
by discrete random variables with event spaces ordered according to the partial order of measurement contexts, defined by the marginal scenario
{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{M})
of
M
. More precisely, for every measurement context
{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{M})\ni{\mathrm{C}}\subset X
, we need to define a commutative algebra
C
, whose generating projections define a Boolean algebra (of events)
{\mathcal{P}(C)}
such that the measurement label
x\in X
is represented as a random variable
O:{\mathcal{P}(C)}\rightarrow o_{x}\subset\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, the inclusion relations between these commutative algebras must reflect the partial order of measurement contexts, that is,
{\widetilde{C}}\subset C
if and only if
\tilde{{\mathrm{C}}}\subset{\mathrm{C}}
for all
\tilde{{\mathrm{C}}},{\mathrm{C}}\in\mathcal{M}
.

To do so, we proceed by induction in the cardinality of measurement contexts
{\mathrm{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{M})
in the measurement cover
\mathcal{M}
. Since we assume that
I\in{\mathrm{C}}
for all
{\mathrm{C}}\in\mathcal{M}
, the initial step is to represent the trivial measurement context
{\mathrm{C}}_{I}=\{I\}
(with
|{\mathrm{C}}|=1
) by the Boolean algebra
\mathcal{P}(C_{I})\cong\mathbf{2}
, and to define the commutative algebra
C_{\mathbb{I}}=\{\lambda\mathbb{I}\mid\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\}
which will be included in every other commutative algebra containing the identity
\mathbb{I}\in{\mathcal{O}}
.

In the inductive step, we may assume to have represented all measurement contexts
{\mathrm{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{M})
of cardinality smaller or equal to
n\in\mathbb{N}
by commutative algebras
C
such that
\widetilde{C}\subset C
if and only if
\widetilde{{\mathrm{C}}}\subset{\mathrm{C}}
. Let
{\mathrm{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{M})
be a measurement context of cardinality
|{\mathrm{C}}|=n+1
and let
\{\widetilde{{\mathrm{C}}}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{S}
with
|\widetilde{{\mathrm{C}}}_{k}|=m_{k}\leq n
for all
1\leq k\leq S
be the set of measurement subcontexts
\widetilde{{\mathrm{C}}}_{k}\subset{\mathrm{C}}
in
{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{M})
. We represent
{\mathrm{C}}
by the minimal commutative algebra
C
such that there exist inclusion relations
i_{C{\widetilde{C}}_{k}}:{\widetilde{C}}_{k}\hookrightarrow C
, subject to the conditions
i_{C{\widetilde{C}}_{k}}({\widetilde{C}}_{k})\neq i_{C{\widetilde{C}}_{l}}({\widetilde{C}}_{l})
whenever
k\neq l
for
1\leq k,l\leq S
and

\displaystyle i_{C{\widetilde{C}}_{k}}|_{{\widetilde{C}}_{l}}({\widetilde{C}}_{k}\cap{\widetilde{C}}_{l})=i_{C{\widetilde{C}}_{l}}|_{{\widetilde{C}}_{k}}({\widetilde{C}}_{k}\cap{\widetilde{C}}_{l})=i_{C{\widetilde{C}}_{k}}({\widetilde{C}}_{k})\cap i_{C{\widetilde{C}}_{l}}({\widetilde{C}}_{l})\;.

Clearly, in this way we obtain commutative algebras
C
for every measurement context
{\mathrm{C}}\subset\mathcal{M}
whose inclusion relations define a partial order
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
which faithfully represents the measurement scenario generated by
\mathcal{M}
, that is,
{\widetilde{C}}\subset C
if and only if
\widetilde{{\mathrm{C}}}\subset{\mathrm{C}}
. Since we assume the commutative algebras
C
to be minimal, we thus obtain a unique observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}=\bigcup_{C\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}}C
, where every measurement label
x\in X
is represented by a random variable
O(x):{\mathcal{P}(C)}\rightarrow o_{x}\subset\mathbb{R}
; and it is straightforward to see that
\{O(x)\}_{x\in X}
forms a generating set for
{\mathcal{O}}
with
M=M(\{O(x)\}_{x\in X})
.
∎

Note that the generating set
\{O(x)\}_{x\in X}
for
{\mathcal{O}}
in the proof of Lm.
12
is generally not minimal, as it may contain observables
O(x)
that can be written as linear combinations of functions of other observables in the set. In this case, we may remove the respective measurement labels from
X
without changing the construction in Lm.
12
. Consequently, such measurement labels are redundant for the purposes of studying contextuality. Moreover, we reiterate from above that even specifying a minimal generating set is generally ambiguous given re-labellings of outcome sets.

In summary, up to a choice of generating set, every measurement scenario is represented by an observable algebra such that the respectively defined partial orders of contexts coincide. Hence, our formalism subsumes, and generally provides a more economical representation than, the sheaf-theoretic approach
[
8
,
10
,
7
]
(see also Sec.
D.3
).

D.2
Orthogonality graphs vs partial algebras

The generating sets of type (ii) in App.
D.1
, for fixed choice of outcomes sets
o=o_{x}=\{0,1\}
, are given by
{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, that is, they correspond with the event algebra
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
of the observable algebra
{\mathcal{O}}
defined in Lm
12
. Since, by Lm.
1
,
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
encodes the same context structure as
{\mathcal{O}}
, it constitutes an economical representation of the contextuality scenario described by
{\mathcal{O}}
. For general observable algebras, the information contained in
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
is readily encoded as a hypergraph
[
57
,
82
,
11
]
.
14
What is more, under the restriction to partial algebras (which also satisfy Specker’s principle), we can further restrict to simple (“orthogonality”) graphs (see Def.
21
), that is, to the orthogonal relations between minimal elements in
{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. For the ensuing comparison between our formalism and that of orthogonality graphs, we will therefore (mostly) restrict to partial algebras.

Now, every partial algebra defines a unique orthogonality graph (see Def.
12
). However, the converse is generally false, since orthogonality graphs lack a notion of identity element. In the following, we first show that this implies that (classical) correlations in Eq. (
13
) are generally incompatible with “coarse-graining”, and then provide various ways to encode this additional structure in order to uniquely identify an orthogonality graph with an event algebra. The issue of sub-normalisation has been pointed out in Ref.
[
11
]
, and further distinguishes the graph- from the hyper-graph approach. Here, we highlight those aspects necessary for our treatment of SI-C sets and graphs in Sec.
4.3
.

Embedding and coarse-graining of states and correlations.
We compare states on partial algebras in Eq. (
7
) with correlations defined on orthogonality graphs in Eq. (
13
), with respect to their compatibility under embedding and coarse-graining. In particular, it is natural to require that the state spaces
\Gamma_{X}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}})}]
for
X=\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{qu},\mathrm{nd}
do not depend on whether we consider
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}
as describing a closed system or whether it arises as part of a larger system which is described by a partial algebra
{\mathcal{O}}\supset{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}
, that is, whether we first embed
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}
within a larger partial algebra, followed by coarse-graining. (Similarly, for correlations on orthogonality graphs
{\widetilde{G}}\subset G
.)

To see whether this is the case, note first that for a general embeddings
\epsilon:{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}
, we have
\Gamma_{X}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}})}]\not\subset\Gamma_{X}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
for
X=\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{qu},\mathrm{nd}
, since
{\mathcal{O}}
will generally impose more constraints on states than
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}
does, that is, the marginal problem posed by
{\mathcal{O}}
is generally more restrictive than that posed by any observable subalgebra
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}
.
79
79

79

Of course, we also have
\Gamma_{X}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]\not\subset\Gamma_{X}[{\mathcal{C}}({\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}})]
for
X=\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{qu},\mathrm{nd}
since states on
{\mathcal{O}}
generally contain more information than states
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}
.
For instance, consider a classical observable algebra
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}=L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
with
\Lambda
a finite set of cardinality
|\Lambda|=d
. Clearly, in this case
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]=\Gamma[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
. Now, consider the embedding
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}
with
{\mathcal{O}}=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
for a Hilbert space of dimension
\dim(\mathcal{H})=d
, which maps the elements in
\Lambda
to an orthogonal basis of
\mathcal{H}
. By the Kochen-Specker theorem,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]=\emptyset
whenever
d\geq 3
. Consequently, we find
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}})}]\not\subset\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})})]
in this case. A similar argument will show that
\Gamma_{\mathrm{qu}}[{\mathcal{C}}({\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}})]\not\subset\Gamma_{\mathrm{qu}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
.

We call an embedding
\epsilon:{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}

constraint-preserving
if
\epsilon(p)\epsilon(p^{\prime})=0
only if
pp^{\prime}=0
for all
p,p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}({\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}})
,
80
80

80

Equivalently, an embedding
\epsilon
is constraint-preserving if
\eta
in Lm.
1
is a fully faithful functor.
and write
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}\subset_{\mathrm{c-p}}{\mathcal{O}}
in this case.

Lemma 13
.

Let
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}\subset_{\mathrm{c-p}}{\mathcal{O}}
be two observable algebras. Then
\Gamma_{X}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}})}]\subset\Gamma_{X}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
, where
X=\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{qu},\mathrm{nd}
denotes classical, quantum and non-disturbing states, respectively.

Proof.

By definition, two contexts
{\widetilde{C}},{\widetilde{C}}^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}})}
are related
{\widetilde{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\widetilde{C}}
in
{{\mathcal{C}}({\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}})}
if and only if
\epsilon({\widetilde{C}}^{\prime})\subset\epsilon({\widetilde{C}})
are related in
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. It follows that contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}\ni C\notin{{\mathcal{C}}({\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}})}
pose no additional marginalisation constraints on states in Eq. (
5
).
∎

Clearly, we also have
\Gamma_{X}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]|_{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}\subset\Gamma_{X}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
for
X\in\{\mathrm{cl,qu,nd}\}
, where
|_{{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}}
denotes marginalisation. In other words, (classical, quantum) states are compatible with marginalisation. Consequently,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{X}}[{\mathcal{C}}({\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}})]=\Gamma_{\mathrm{X}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]|_{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}
whenever
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}\subset_{\mathrm{c-p}}{\mathcal{O}}
.

Finally, we observe that compatibility with marginalisation already implies a partial consistency condition with respect to general subsystem embeddings.

Lemma 14
.

Let
\epsilon:{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}
be an embedding. Then
\Gamma_{\mathrm{X}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]|_{{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}}\subset\Gamma_{\mathrm{X}}[{\mathcal{C}}({\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}})]
, where
X=\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{qu},\mathrm{nd}
denotes classical, quantum and non-disturbing states, respectively.

Proof.

Clearly,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{nd}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]|_{{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}}\subset\Gamma_{\mathrm{nd}}[{\mathcal{C}}({\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}})]
. Moreover, every classical, respectively quantum embedding
\epsilon^{\prime}:{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
induces a classical, respectively quantum embedding
\epsilon^{\prime}|_{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}
of
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}
by restriction. It therefore follows from the definition of the state spaces
\Gamma_{X}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
for
X=\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{qu}
via such embeddings in Eq. (
7
) that marginalisation of classical, respectively quantum states
\gamma\in\Gamma_{X}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]
defines classical, respectively quantum states on
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}
, that is,
\gamma|_{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}\in\Gamma_{X}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}})}]
.
∎

Differences between
\Gamma_{\mathrm{X}}[{\mathcal{C}}({\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}})]
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{X}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]|_{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}
in a physical theory reveal a mismatch with respect to subsystem embeddings. In turn, by demanding full consistency with subsystem embeddings, that is,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{X}}[{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}]|_{{\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}}}=\Gamma_{\mathrm{X}}[{\mathcal{C}}({\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}})]
for
X=\mathrm{cl},\mathrm{qu},\mathrm{nd}
, one may sometimes rule out states on certain subsystems, for instance, one may rule out non-quantum states
[
158
]
on two-dimensional systems under consistency with quantum embeddings.

Next, we turn to correlations on orthogonality graphs in Eq. (
13
). Specifically, given a subgraph
{\widetilde{G}}\subset G
we seek a notion of “coarse-graining” for correlations that corresponds with the restriction to subgraphs. Note first that in analogy with the case of states on observable algebras the marginal problems defined by
G
and
{\widetilde{G}}
also differ in two opposing ways. On the one hand,
G
generally admits more correlations than
{\widetilde{G}}
since it contains more vertices and thus correlations on
G
encode more information than those on
{\widetilde{G}}
. On the other hand,
G
generally poses more constraints on the respective marginal problem than
{\widetilde{G}}
does. Consequently, in general
{\mathrm{QStab}}(G)\not\subset{\mathrm{QStab}}({\widetilde{G}})
and
{\mathrm{QStab}}({\widetilde{G}})\not\subset{\mathrm{QStab}}(G)
, and similarly for the restriction to quantum and classical correlations.

Nevertheless, the following relations hold for vertex-restricted subgraphs
{\widetilde{G}}=({\widetilde{V}},{\widetilde{E}})\subset_{V}G=(V,E)
defined by
{\widetilde{V}}\subset V
and
{\widetilde{E}}=E|_{\widetilde{V}}=\{({\widetilde{v}},{\widetilde{v}}^{\prime})\in E\mid({\widetilde{v}},{\widetilde{v}}^{\prime})\in{\widetilde{V}}\}
, analogous to constraint-preserving embeddings between observable algebras in Lm.
13
.

Lemma 15
.

Let
{\widetilde{G}},G
be two orthogonality graphs with
{\widetilde{G}}\subset_{V}G
. Then

(i)

{\mathrm{QStab}}({\widetilde{G}})\subset{\mathrm{QStab}}(G)
,

(ii)

{\mathrm{Stab}}({\widetilde{G}})\subset{\mathrm{Stab}}(G)
,

(iii)

{\mathrm{Th}}({\widetilde{G}})\subset{\mathrm{Th}}(G)
.
81
81

81

Here, we allow for realisations
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
of
G=(V,E)
with
\pi(v)\pi(v^{\prime})=0
even if
(v,v^{\prime})\notin E
.

Proof.

(i) Since cliques in
{\widetilde{G}}
are cliques in
G
, we find
{\mathrm{QStab}}({\widetilde{G}})\subset{\mathrm{QStab}}(G)
.

(ii) Let
{\widetilde{\gamma}}\in{\mathrm{Stab}}({\widetilde{G}})
,
82
82

82

Unlike before, here we write
\gamma\in{\mathrm{QStab}}(G)
,
{\widetilde{\gamma}}\in{\mathrm{QStab}}({\widetilde{G}})
for correlations on
G
,
{\widetilde{G}}\subset G
, respectively.
that is, there exist weights
\mathrm{w}_{\widetilde{\gamma}}({\widetilde{I}})\in[0,1]
over independent sets
{\widetilde{I}}\in\mathcal{I}({\widetilde{G}})
with
\sum_{{\widetilde{I}}\in\mathcal{I}({\widetilde{G}})}\mathrm{w}_{\widetilde{\gamma}}({\widetilde{I}})=1
,
{\widetilde{\gamma}}({\widetilde{v}})=\sum_{{\widetilde{I}}\in\mathcal{I}({\widetilde{G}})}\mathrm{w}_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(I){\widetilde{I}}({\widetilde{v}})
for all
{\widetilde{v}}\in{\widetilde{V}}
and such that
\sum_{{\widetilde{v}}\in\widetilde{{\mathrm{C}}}}\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}({\widetilde{v}})}\mathrm{w}_{\widetilde{\gamma}}({\widetilde{I}})\leq 1
for all (maximal) cliques
\widetilde{{\mathrm{C}}}\subset{\widetilde{G}}
. But since
\mathcal{I}({\widetilde{G}})\subset\mathcal{I}(G)
, we also have
{\widetilde{\gamma}}\in{\mathrm{Stab}}(G)
, hence,
{\mathrm{Stab}}({\widetilde{G}})\subset{\mathrm{Stab}}(G)
.

(ii) Let
{\widetilde{\gamma}}\in{\mathrm{Th}}({\widetilde{G}})
, that is, there exists a realisation
\pi:{\widetilde{G}}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}({\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}})
and a quantum state
{\widetilde{\rho}}\in{\mathcal{S}({\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}})}
such that
{\widetilde{\gamma}}({\widetilde{v}})=\mathrm{tr}[{\widetilde{\rho}}{\widetilde{\pi}}({\widetilde{v}})]
for all
{\widetilde{v}}\in{\widetilde{V}}
. Since
{\widetilde{G}}\subset_{V}G
with
{\widetilde{E}}=E|_{\widetilde{V}}
we can extend
{\widetilde{\pi}}
to a realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
with
\dim(\mathcal{H})=\dim({\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}})+|V|/|{\widetilde{V}}|
, by mapping the vertices in
V/{\widetilde{V}}
onto mutually orthogonal rank-
1
projections in the subspace orthogonal to
{\widetilde{\pi}}({\widetilde{V}})
. Clearly, in this case
{\widetilde{\pi}}=\pi|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}}
as well as
{\widetilde{\rho}}=\rho|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}}
under the canonical embedding
{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
, hence,
{\widetilde{\gamma}}\in{\mathrm{Th}}(G)
.
∎

What about the (partial) consistency condition with respect to subsystem embeddings in Lm.
14
? In particular, do correlations on
G
marginalise to correlations on
{\widetilde{G}}
for subgraphs
{\widetilde{G}}\subset G
, specifically for vertex-restricted subgraphs
{\widetilde{G}}\subset_{V}G
?

Note first that there is no natural notion of marginalisation given merely the structure of an orthogonality graph.
83
83

83

Indeed, this requires a notion of coarse-graining of events, such as given by the event algebra of an observable algebra or by hyperedges in a hypergraph
[
11
]
.
Nevertheless, in the absence of a canonical marginalisation map, we may call any map
|_{\widetilde{G}}:{\mathrm{QStab}}(G)\rightarrow{\mathrm{QStab}}({\widetilde{G}})
a
valid marginalisation map
if (i) it preserves convex decompositions, that is,
\gamma|_{\widetilde{G}}=p\gamma_{1}|_{\widetilde{G}}+(1-p)\gamma_{2}|_{\widetilde{G}}
for every
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma=p\gamma_{1}+(1-p)\gamma_{2}\in{\mathrm{QStab}}(G)
and
p\in[0,1]
, and (ii) it acts trivially on
{\mathrm{QStab}}({\widetilde{G}})
, that is,
(|_{\widetilde{G}})|_{{\mathrm{Stab}}({\widetilde{G}})}=\mathrm{id}
. For non-disturbing and quantum states, such maps generally exist: let
{\widetilde{G}}\subset_{V}G
be a vertex-restricted subgraph of
G
, e.g. with
V={\widetilde{V}}\cup\{v\}
. Given a unital (quantum) realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{1}({\mathcal{O}})}
, we obtain a unital (quantum) realisation
{\widetilde{\pi}}:{\widetilde{G}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
by setting
{\widetilde{\pi}}(v_{\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}})=\pi(v)+\pi(v_{\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}})
for some choice of vertex
v_{\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}}\in{\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}}
in every maximal clique
C={\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}}\cup\{v\}\subset G
(such that if
v_{\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}}\in\mathrm{C}^{\prime}={\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}}\cup\{v\}
, then
v_{{\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}}^{\prime}}=v_{\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}}
) and
{\widetilde{\pi}}=\pi
otherwise. This is easily seen to define a valid marginalisation map.
84
84

84

Here, we assume that correlations are normalised, that is, we assume equality in Eq. (
10
).
In particular, this construction applies to graph completions
G\subset_{V}{\overline{G(\pi)}}
.

However, there is generally no marginalisation map for classical correlations. The reason is that upon deleting a vertex
v\in V
from
G
there is generally no way to “re-distribute” the weight
w_{\gamma}(I_{v})
with
I_{v}(v^{\prime})=\delta_{vv^{\prime}}
of a correlation
\gamma\in{\mathrm{QStab}}(G)
to a coarse-grained correlation in
{\mathrm{QStab}}({\widetilde{G}})
. More precisely, note that
I\in{\mathrm{Stab}}(G)
for every
I\in\mathcal{I}(G)
. Hence, for independent sets
I\in\mathcal{I}(G)
with
I({\widetilde{v}})=1
for some
{\widetilde{v}}\in{\widetilde{V}}
, a natural way to define marginalisation is by restriction, that is,
I|_{\widetilde{G}}\in{\mathrm{Stab}}({\widetilde{G}})
and thus
w_{\gamma|_{\widetilde{G}}}(I|_{\widetilde{G}})=w_{\gamma}(I)
, where
I|_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{v}})=I({\widetilde{v}})
for all
{\widetilde{v}}\in{\widetilde{G}}
. However, for
I_{v}
with
V\ni v\notin{\widetilde{V}}
there is no natural way to assign a probability weight and we may thus assign it any element
I_{v}|_{\widetilde{G}}\in{\mathrm{Stab}}({\widetilde{G}})
. Yet, if
\gamma\in{\mathrm{Stab}}(G)
is a classical correlation such that
w_{\gamma}(I_{v})>0
, then
\sum_{I|_{\widetilde{G}}\in\mathcal{I}({\widetilde{G}})}w_{\gamma}|_{\widetilde{G}}(I|_{\widetilde{G}})=\sum_{I_{v}\neq I|_{\widetilde{G}}\in\mathcal{I}({\widetilde{G}})}w_{\gamma}|_{\widetilde{G}}(I|_{\widetilde{G}})+w_{\gamma}(I_{v})=1+w_{\gamma}(I_{v})>1
.

Proposition 3
.

There exists no marginalisation map
|_{\widetilde{G}}:{\mathrm{Stab}}(G)\rightarrow{\mathrm{Stab}}({\widetilde{G}})
for
{\widetilde{G}}\subset G
. Consequently,
{\mathrm{Stab}}(G)
is incompatible with subsystem embeddings, that is,
{\mathrm{Stab}}(G)|_{\widetilde{G}}\neq{\mathrm{Stab}}({\widetilde{G}})
, in particular,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}[{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}}({\overline{G(\pi)}})]|_{G}\neq{\mathrm{Stab}}(G)
for
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
.

states

\bullet({\widetilde{O}})\subset\bullet({\mathcal{O}})

\bullet({\mathcal{O}})|_{\widetilde{O}}\subset\bullet({\mathcal{O}})

\bullet({\mathcal{O}})|_{\widetilde{O}}\subset\bullet({\widetilde{O}})

\bullet({\widetilde{O}})\subset\bullet({\mathcal{O}})|_{\widetilde{O}}

\bullet=\Gamma[{\mathcal{C}}(\cdot)]
