Table 7
:
Compatibility of (top) states on observable (specifically, partial) algebras as opposed to (bottom) correlations on orthogonality graphs, with respect to constraint-preserving embeddings
{\widetilde{O}}\subset_{\mathrm{cp}}{\mathcal{O}}
, respectively vertex-restricted subgraphs
{\widetilde{G}}\subset_{V}G
, as well as coarse-graining and subsystem embeddings. Contrary to classical states in Lm.
14
, by Prop.
3
there is no notion of marginalisation and thus of subsystem embedding for classical correlations.

Prop.
3
is a consequence of the different normalisation condition imposed on classical correlations (in Eq. (
11
)) as compared to the normalisation condition imposed on states on observable algebras. This shows that the respective notions are generally incomparable (see also App.
F
). If we nevertheless want to compare correlations on orthogonality graphs
G
with states on partial algebras
G\subset{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, we thus need to add additional information to the graph
G
in the form of an identity. The obvious way to enforce normalisation is by restricting realisations of
G
to be unital (see Def.
23
). Yet, while every orthogonality graph admits a realisation in some (quantum) partial algebra
[
101
]
, to determine whether it admits a unital realisation remains an open problem (see App.
E
).

In summary, orthogonality graphs correspond with the event algebras of partial algebras if and only if they admit a unital realisation (see Tab.
5
). While the existence problem of such realisations is notably harder than that of generic realisations (see App.
E
), in Thm.
9
we find that under the restriction to unital realisations the chromatic number of (the maximal extensions of) an orthogonality graph (see Def.
25
) is a complete invariant of KS contextuality. What is more, in Thm.
11
we prove that the chromatic number of (the extension of) an orthogonality graph with freely completable realisation (see Def.
29
) is a complete invariant of state-independent contextuality.

D.3
Reduced context categories

We finish this section by pointing out further redundancies in the presentation of observable algebras. More precisely, we will prove a number of reduction arguments that allow us to reduce a given observable algebra to a simpler one, which is Kochen-Specker contextual if and only if the original one is. By Thm.
1
, this is the case as long as we do not change the constraints in Eq. (
4
). By comparison with measurement scenarios and (hyper)graphs, and the context categories (the partial order of contexts) they generate as shown in App.
D.1
and App.
D.2
, we will find that these frameworks generally contain redundant information from the perspective of KS contextuality.

Downward generated context categories.

Definition 35
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an observable algebra with context category
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. We call
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}\supset{{\mathcal{C}}_{\cap}({\mathcal{O}})}:=\{{\widetilde{C}}\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}\mid\exists C,C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}:{\widetilde{C}}=C\cap C^{\prime}\}
the
downward generated context category
, and
{{\mathcal{O}}_{\cap}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}
the
downward generated observable algebra of
{\mathcal{O}}
.

Note that
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}\subset{{\mathcal{C}}_{\cap}({\mathcal{O}})}
(pick
C^{\prime}=C
in Def.
35
). Since a classical observable algebra with
{\mathcal{O}}=L_{\infty}(\Lambda)
for a measurable space
\Lambda
has a single maximal context, this immediately implies that
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\cap}({\mathcal{O}})}={{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
for classical systems.

Lemma 16
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
with
\dim(\mathbb{I})=\dim(\mathcal{H})=d
. Then
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\cap}({\mathcal{O}})}={{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
.

Proof.

Let
{\widetilde{C}}\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
be a non-maximal context in
{{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{H})}
with generating projections
\{{\widetilde{p}}_{1},\cdots,{\widetilde{p}}_{m}\}
, that is,
\mathbbm{1}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}{\widetilde{p}}_{k}
,
\sum_{k=1}^{m}d_{k}=d
and
d_{k}>1
for some
k
. Consequently, there exist maximal contexts
C,C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
generated by one-dimensional projections

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{1}(C)

\displaystyle=\{p_{11},\cdots,p_{1d_{1}},p_{21},\cdots,p_{2d_{2}},p_{31},\cdots,p_{md_{m}}\}\;,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{1}(C^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\{p^{\prime}_{11},\cdots,p^{\prime}_{1d_{1}},p^{\prime}_{21},\cdots,p^{\prime}_{2d_{2}},p^{\prime}_{31},\cdots,p^{\prime}_{md_{m}}\}\;,

with
{\widetilde{p}}_{k}=\sum_{j=1}^{d_{k}}p_{kj}=\sum_{j=1}^{d_{k}}p^{\prime}_{kj}

p_{ik}\neq p^{\prime}_{jl}
for all
k,l\in\{1,\cdots,m\}
and such that the contexts
C_{k}=C(p_{k1},\cdots,p_{kd_{k}},\mathbbm{1})
,
C^{\prime}_{k}=C(p^{\prime}_{k1},\cdots,p^{\prime}_{kd_{k}},\mathbbm{1})
generated by the respective resolutions of
{\widetilde{p}}_{k}
satisfy
C_{k}\cap C^{\prime}_{k}=C({\widetilde{p}}_{k},\mathbbm{1})
for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,m\}
. It follows that
C\cap C^{\prime}={\widetilde{C}}
, hence,
{\widetilde{C}}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\cap}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Since this holds for all
{\widetilde{C}}\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, the result follows.
∎

Since every finite-dimensional quantum system is a direct sum of matrix algebras, the result holds for all finite-dimensional quantum systems. However, for general observable algebras, including more general subalgebras
{\mathcal{O}}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
we have
{{\mathcal{O}}_{\cap}}\subsetneq{\mathcal{O}}
. Namely, if
{\widetilde{C}}\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
is such that
{\widetilde{C}}\subsetneq C\cap C^{\prime}
for all
C,C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
then
{\widetilde{C}}\notin{{\mathcal{C}}_{\cap}({\mathcal{O}})}
.

Lemma 17
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an observable algebra with context category
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Then
{\mathcal{O}}
is Kochen-Specker contextual if and only if
{{\mathcal{O}}_{\cap}}
is Kochen-Specker contextual.

Proof.

By Lm.
10
, it is sufficient to establish the claim for
{\mathcal{O}}
maximal.
85
85

85

This follows from condition (iii) on maximal extensions in Def.
30
(see App.
B
).

“
\Rightarrow
”: Since
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\cap}({\mathcal{O}})}\subset{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, this direction is obvious.

“
\Leftarrow
”: Let
\gamma=(C_{0},\cdots,C_{n-1})
be a context cycle in
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Since,
C_{i+1i}=C_{i+1}\cap C_{i}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\cap}({\mathcal{O}})}
for all
C_{i},C_{i+1}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}({\mathcal{O}})}
by definition,
\gamma
is also a context cycle in
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\cap}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Hence,
{\mathcal{O}}
poses no additional constraints and so inherits KS noncontextuality from
{{\mathcal{O}}_{\cap}}
.
∎

Lm.
17
highlights redundant structure (from the perspective of KS contextuality) in general observable algebras, e.g. those generated from measurement scenarios (see App.
D.1
). A similar result holds for the other direction.

Upward generated observable algebras.

Definition 36
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an observable algebra with context category
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. We call
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}\supset{{\mathcal{C}}_{\cup}({\mathcal{O}})}:=\{{\widetilde{C}}\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}\mid\exists C,C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\circ})}:{\widetilde{C}}=C\cup C^{\prime}\}
the
upward generated context category
, and
{{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}
the
upward generated observable algebra of
{\mathcal{O}}
.

Minimal contexts are defined in the same way as maximal ones. However, for Def.
36
not to be trivial, we need to exclude the least element in
C_{\mathbb{I}}
in
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
.

In the extreme case, minimal contexts are generated by a single projection. Finitely generated classical and quantum observable algebras are of this form.

Lemma 18
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}={{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sa}}}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
with
\dim(\mathcal{H})=d
. Then
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\cup}({\mathcal{O}})}={{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
.

Proof.

Clearly, every maximal context
C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\mathcal{H})}
is generated by one-dimensional projections
\mathcal{P}_{1}(C)=\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{d}\}
for
p_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
,
\sum_{i=1}^{d}p_{i}=\mathbbm{1}
and
p_{i}p_{j}=\delta_{ij}
.
∎

Since (finitely generated) classical observable algebras arise as a special case with
{{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sa}}}=\oplus_{k=1}^{N}M_{1}(\mathbb{C})
, Lm.
18
applies to both the classical and quantum case.

For general observable algebras, minimal contexts are not generated by a single projection, and we have
{{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}}\subsetneq{\mathcal{O}}
. Namely, if
{\widetilde{C}}\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
is such that
C\cup C^{\prime}\subsetneq{\widetilde{C}}
for all
C,C^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\circ})}
then
{\widetilde{C}}\notin{{\mathcal{C}}_{\cup}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Nevertheless, for what regards Kochen-Specker contextuality, we may always reduce to upward generated observable algebras.

Lemma 19
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an observable algebra with context category
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Then
{\mathcal{O}}
is Kochen-Specker contextual if and only if
{{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}}
is Kochen-Specker contextual.

Proof.

By Lm.
10
, it is again sufficient to establish the claim for
{\mathcal{O}}
maximal.
85

“
\Rightarrow
”: Since
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\cup}({\mathcal{O}})}\subset{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
, this direction is obvious.

“
\Leftarrow
”: Let
\gamma=(C_{0},\cdots,C_{n-1})
be a context cycle in
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
with subcontexts
C_{i+1i}=C_{i+1}\cap C_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
. By Def.
36
, there exist contexts
C_{i+1i}\supset{\widetilde{C}}_{i+1i}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\cup}({\mathcal{O}})}
and
C_{i}\supset{\widetilde{C}}_{i}={\widetilde{C}}_{i+1i}\cup{\widetilde{C}}_{ii-1}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\cup}({\mathcal{O}})}
such that

{\widetilde{\gamma}}=({\widetilde{C}}_{0},\cdots,{\widetilde{C}}_{n-1})
is a context cycle in
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\cup}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Moreover, Def.
36
implies that the contexts
C_{i}\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
and
{\widetilde{C}}_{i}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\cup}({\mathcal{O}})}
(respectively,
C_{i+1i}\in{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
and
{\widetilde{C}}_{i+1i}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\cup}({\mathcal{O}})}
) are distinguished by additional projections which are not related to projections other than in
{\widetilde{C}}_{i}
(respectively,
{\widetilde{C}}_{i+1i}
). Consequently,
{\mathcal{O}}
is a (non-maximal) extension of
{{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}}
(similar to Def.
30
), and the result thus follows by analogous arguments to those in the proof of Lm.
10
.
∎

Of course, we can combine the reductions in Def.
35
and Def.
36
.

Lemma 20
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a finitely generated observable algebra with context category
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Then
(({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}})_{\cap})_{\cup}=({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}})_{\cap}=({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cap}})_{\cup}=(({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cap}})_{\cup})_{\cap}
.

Proof.

Clearly,
(({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}})_{\cap})_{\cup}\subset({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}})_{\cap}
. Conversely, let
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}})_{\cap})
, then there exist two maximal contexts
{\widetilde{C}},{\widetilde{C}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\max}(({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}})_{\cap})
such that
C={\widetilde{C}}\cap{\widetilde{C}}^{\prime}
. We will show that there also exist
{\widetilde{\widetilde{C}}},{\widetilde{\widetilde{C}}}^{\prime}\in(({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}})_{\cap})_{\cup}
such that
C={\widetilde{\widetilde{C}}}\cap{\widetilde{\widetilde{C}}}^{\prime}
. Let
{\widetilde{C}}\supset{\widetilde{\widetilde{C}}}\in(({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}})_{\cap})_{\cup}
and
{\widetilde{C}}^{\prime}\supset{\widetilde{\widetilde{C}}}^{\prime}\in(({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}})_{\cap})_{\cup}
be maximal. Since
{\widetilde{\widetilde{C}}}\cap C={\widetilde{C}}\cap C
(similarly,
{\widetilde{\widetilde{C}}}^{\prime}\cap C={\widetilde{C}}^{\prime}\cap C
) for all
C\in(({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}})_{\cap})_{\cup}
(unless
{\widetilde{C}}\supset C\supset{\widetilde{\widetilde{C}}}
, but then
C\notin(({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}})_{\cap})_{\cup}
) this implies
{\widetilde{\widetilde{C}}}\cap{\widetilde{\widetilde{C}}}^{\prime}={\widetilde{\widetilde{C}}}\cap{\widetilde{C}}^{\prime}(={\widetilde{C}}\cap{\widetilde{\widetilde{C}}}^{\prime})={\widetilde{C}}\cap{\widetilde{C}}^{\prime}=C
. Consequently,
C\in(({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}})_{\cap})_{\cup}
which implies
({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}})_{\cap}\subset(({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}})_{\cap})_{\cup}
and thus
({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}})_{\cap}=(({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}})_{\cap})_{\cup}
. Similarly, we find
(({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cap}})_{\cup})_{\cap}=({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cap}})_{\cup}
.

Finally, for
{\widetilde{C}}\in{\mathcal{O}}
and
{\widetilde{C}}\supset{\widetilde{\widetilde{C}}}\in{{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}}
maximal such that
{\widetilde{\widetilde{C}}}=D\cup E
for
D,E\in{\mathcal{O}}
, we also have
{\widetilde{\widetilde{C}}}\cap C={\widetilde{C}}\cap C
for all
C\in{{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}}
(unless
{\widetilde{C}}\supset C\supset{\widetilde{\widetilde{C}}}
, but then
C\notin{{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}}
). It follows that the two operations commute, hence,
({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cap}})_{\cup}=({{\mathcal{O}}_{\cup}})_{\cap}
.
∎

After having reduced our observable algebra by means of Lm.
17
and Lm.
19
, we may further coarse-grain minimal generating sets as follows.

Lemma 21
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an observable algebra,
{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
its event algebra and
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\circ})}
the set of minimal contexts. Then
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
is isomorphic to the context category generated by
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathbbm{1}\}\cup\{p_{C}\}_{C\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\circ})}}
with
p_{C}=\sum_{i}p^{C}_{i}
and
\{\mathbbm{1},p^{C}_{i}\}_{i}
a minimal generating set of
C
,

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}={\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})})\cong{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{P})\;.

(17)

In particular,
{\mathcal{O}}
is KS noncontextual if and only if
{{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{cg}}}={\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{P})
is KS noncontextual.

Proof.

Recall that
{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}})}
is a (not necessarily minimal) generating set for
{\mathcal{O}}
(see App.
D.2
). Hence, Eq. (
17
) holds by minimality of
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{min}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\circ})}
, that is,
{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\circ})\ni C\subset C(\{p^{C}_{i}\})
implies
C=C(\{p^{C}_{i}\})
. But then the second statement follows since, by Thm.
1
, KS noncontextuality is a property of the partial order of contexts only.
∎

Combining the arguments in this section, we obtain a more economical representation of “contextuality scenarios”
[
82
]
represented by generic context categories.

Theorem 12
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be an observable algebra with partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
. Then
{\mathcal{O}}
is KS noncontextual if and only if
(({\mathcal{O}}^{\circ}_{\cap})_{\cup})_{\mathrm{cg}}=(({\mathcal{O}}^{\circ}_{\cup})_{\cap})_{\mathrm{cg}}
is KS noncontextual.

Proof.

This follows from Thm.
5
, together with Lm.
17
, Lm.
19
, Lm.
20
and Lm.
21
.
∎

Thm.
1
shows that the essence of Kochen-Specker contextuality lies in the partial order of contexts
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}

[
67
,
76
]
, and extracts the relevant information in terms of constraints imposed on context connections, and expressed along context cycles (see Eq. (
4
)). Thm.
12
reduces the representation of
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}
with respect to these constraints, and thus highlights redundancies in the representation of partial orders of contexts generated from generic sets of observables or events as in Def.
32
. Moreover, the reduction in Thm.
12
is optimal in that removing further contexts will generally reduce the number of constraints imposed by KS noncontextuality.

Appendix E
On realisations of orthogonality graphs

Thm.
3
characterises Kochen-Specker contextuality of an observable algebra in terms of the chromatic number of the orthogonality graph defined by its minimal projections (see also Thm.
9
, Thm.
10
and Thm.
11
). In turn, this suggests a way to search for contextual arrangements in quantum theory, namely in terms of (critical)
k
-colourable graphs.
85
85

85

An (undirected) graph is called critical if removing any vertex or edge reduces its chromatic number. For instance, the 5-cycle in Ref.
[
188
]
is
3
-critical, whereas the orthogonality graph corresponding to the provably minimal KS set in dimension
d\geq 4
in Ref.
[
48
]
is not
4
-critical.
More generally, the (hyper-)graph approach
[
57
,
82
,
52
,
11
,
12
]
identifies additional graph-theoretic invariants for the study of contextuality.

An application of interest is to use such graph-theoretic invariants for the construction of novel contextual arrangements. In turn, this poses the following question:

Problem
.

Which simple graphs are realisable in a finite-dimensional observable algebra?

Of most interest are orthogonality graphs that are quantum realisabile.

Problem
.

Which simple graphs admit a finite-dimensional quantum realisation?

In this section, we discuss these problems and pose a number of (to our knowledge) open questions for refinements of the type of (quantum) realisation considered. In App.
D.2
, we have seen that an orthogonality graph is the event algebra of an observable algebra if and only if it has a unital realisation. This first raises the question when a unital realisation exists. We will consider both the general and the quantum case in turn.

Unital realisations in general observable algebras.
The existence problem for general observable algebras is tied to its (minimal) dimension. Indeed, we obtain an embedding of
G
in a (finitely generated) finite-dimensional observable algebra if there exists a dimension function
\dim:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
for
G={G({\mathcal{O}})}
(cf. App.
B
) such that,

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in\mathrm{C}}\dim(v)=\dim(\mathbb{I})\;,

(18)

for all maximal cliques
\mathrm{C}\subset G
. We can write this as a homogeneous system of linear Diophantine equations in
|V|+1
variables of the form
Ax=0
, where
A\in M_{|C|\times(|V|+1)}(\mathbb{N})
is the matrix given for all maximal cliques
\mathrm{C}\subset G
by
A_{\mathrm{C},0}=d
,
A_{\mathrm{C},v}=-1
if
v\in\mathrm{C}
and
A_{\mathrm{C},v}=0
otherwise. The existence of solutions to Eq. (
18
) can thus be checked efficiently by computing the Smith normal form of
A
. If a solution exists,
G
has a realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
with
{\mathcal{O}}
a finite-dimensional observable algebra. Moreover, a minimal realisation simply corresponds with solutions for which
d
is minimal.

Unital quantum realisations.
Given a quantum realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
of an orthogonality graph
G
we obtain another quantum realisation by unitary conjugation
\pi_{U}=U\pi U^{*}
for any
U\in{\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H})}
. Both realisations contain the same quantum states and we may thus identify such realisations. However,
G
may also admit quantum realisations that are not identifiable in this way.

Problem 1
.

Given a simple graph
G
, characterise all inequivalent unital realisations
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
in dimension
\dim(\mathcal{H})=d
.

Furthermore, one may ask how realisations in different dimensions are related. For instance, given a realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
with
\dim(\mathcal{H})=d
we obtain another realisation in
\dim(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})=2d
by doubling the dimension of every subspace. However, beyond such simple constructions, the relationship is not clear and a given orthogonality graph may thus lead to genuinely different realisations. Fortunately, the distinction between inequivalent unital realisations bears no relevance for the study of KS contextuality.

Proposition 4
.

Let
G
be an orthogonality graph with two unital realisations
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
and
\pi^{\prime}:G\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})
. Then
{\mathcal{O}}={\mathcal{O}}(\pi(G))
is Kochen-Specker contextual if and only if
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{O}}(\pi^{\prime}(G))
is Kochen-Specker contextual.

Proof.

Since
{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}})}\cong{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
this follows immediately from Thm.
1
.
∎

Prop.
4
holds for all inequivalent realisations (in all dimensions). Note also that we do not need to restrict to rank-
1
projections as if often done. Using Prop.
4
, we can thus reduce Problem
1
to the mere existence problem (cf. Ref.
[
101
]
).

Problem 2
.

Which simple graphs admit a unital quantum realisation?

Problem
2
remains generally open. Even if
G
has a unital realisation in a (finite-dimensional) observable algebra, this does not imply that
G
has a unital quantum realisation; indeed, Lm.
11
in App.
C
provides an explicit counterexample.

Freely completable quantum realisations.
For realisations with faithful completion Problem
2
can be further simplified. Specifically, in order to apply the characterisation of SI-C sets in terms of the chromatic number of the orthogonality graphs of their faithful completion
\overline{G(d)}^{*}
, one is faced with the following:

Problem 3
.

Which simple graphs admit a freely completable quantum realisation?

In Ref.
[
101
]
it was shown that every finite simple graph
G
admits a (faithful) quantum realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
in a Hilbert space of dimension
\dim(\mathcal{H})<|V|
. Clearly, any realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{O}})}
must also satisfy the lower bound
\omega(G)\leq\dim(\mathbb{I})
, where
\omega(G)
is the size of the maximal clique in
G
. Moreover, both bounds are tight in general since they coincide for complete graphs, that is, maximally connected graphs
G=(V,V\times V)
. It would therefore be interesting to see if the construction in Ref.
[
101
]
can be lifted to provide an answer to Problem
3
.

Minimal quantum realisations and reduction to simple algebras.
Since Thm.
3
, Thm.
10
and Thm.
11
all depend on the dimension (of the realisation of an orthogonality graph), it is natural to ask for the minimal quantum realisation of a simple graph.
86
86

86

Still, the respective results hold irrespective of whether the realisation is minimal or not.
To this end, note that a simple graph
G
may have a realisation with respect to a non-simple quantum algebra of the form
\pi_{m}:G\rightarrow\bigoplus_{n=1}^{N(\pi)}\bigoplus_{i_{n}=1}^{I_{n}(\pi)}M_{ni_{n}}(\mathbb{C})
with
\dim(\mathcal{H})=\sum_{n=1}^{N(\pi)}nI_{n}(\pi)
and where
I_{n}(\pi)\in\mathbb{N}
counts multiplicities. This motivates to consider a fine-grained (partial) ordering on the space of matrix algebras,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}\preceq{\mathcal{B}}\quad:\Longleftrightarrow\quad\exists\phi:{\mathcal{A}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{B}}\;,

(19)

where
\phi
is an embedding, that is, an injective (
C^{*}
-)algebra homomorphism.
87
87

87

The order in Eq. (
19
) can be visualised using Bratelli diagrams for
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}
.
Clearly, Eq. (
19
) includes the natural embedding of simple algebras
M_{m}(\mathbb{C})={\mathcal{A}}\preceq{\mathcal{B}}=M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
if and only if
m\leq n
. Moreover, it implies an ordering between non-simple algebras acting on the same Hilbert space, e.g.
M_{1}(\mathbb{C})\oplus M_{2}(\mathbb{C})\prec M_{3}(\mathbb{C})
. Finally, the ordering is partial, for instance, let
{\mathcal{A}}=M_{2}(\mathbb{C})\oplus M_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
{\mathcal{B}}=M_{1}(\mathbb{C})\oplus M_{3}(\mathbb{C})
, then neither
{\mathcal{A}}\prec{\mathcal{B}}
nor
{\mathcal{B}}\prec{\mathcal{A}}
. We now define a minimal realisation in the following way.

Definition 37
.

Let
G
be a simple graph. Then a (quantum) realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{A}})
with respect to a finite-dimensional matrix algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
is called minimal if for any other realisation
\pi^{\prime}:G\rightarrow\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{B}})
there exists an injective homomorphism
\phi:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
.

Note that the notion of minimal realisation in Def.
37
does not distinguish between inequivalent realisations in the same (Hilbert space) dimension as in Problem
1
. Since
\prec
is a partial order, Def.
37
a priori allows for minimal realisations with respect to incomparable algebras under the ordering in Eq. (
19
). Nevertheless, this is not the case.

Lemma 22
.

Let
G
be a simple graph with minimal quantum realisations
\pi:G\rightarrow\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\pi^{\prime}:V\rightarrow\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{B}})
. Then
{\mathcal{A}}\cong{\mathcal{B}}
.

Proof.

For every summand
{\mathcal{A}}_{k}=M_{n_{k}}(\mathbb{C})
in
{\mathcal{A}}=\oplus_{k}{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
, there exists a subgraph
G_{k}\subset G
such that
\pi_{k}=\pi|_{G_{k}}:G_{k}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{k})
with
\dim(\mathcal{H}_{k})=n_{k}
is a realisation of
G_{k}
. Moreover, we have
G=G_{k}\times G_{\overline{k}}
if
\pi
is faithful and constraint-preserving (and
G\subset G_{k}\times G_{\overline{k}}
otherwise), and where
\times
denotes the join of two graphs (defined from the union of the respective graphs, where every vertex in one factor is additionally adjacent to every vertex in the other). By minimality of
\pi^{\prime}
, we must then have
\pi^{\prime}(G_{k})={\mathcal{B}}_{k}
for some summand
{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
in
{\mathcal{B}}=\oplus_{k}{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
. Consequently, there exists a unitary
u\in{\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H})}
such that
u{\mathcal{A}}u^{*}=\oplus_{k}u{\mathcal{A}}_{k}u^{*}=\oplus_{k}{\mathcal{B}}_{k}={\mathcal{B}}
.
∎

By decomposing a graph into parts
G_{k}
according to the decomposition of a minimal realisation, Lm.
22
therefore allows us to reduce the search for quantum contextuality scenarios to simple algebras. In particular, we arrive at the following graph-theoretic refinement of the “Kochen-Specker problem” (defined in Eq. (
*
) in Ref.
[
76
]
), which asks to characterise all observable subalgebras
{\mathcal{O}}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathrm{sa}}
that are KS contextual.

Problem 4
.

Characterise all simple graphs
G
with minimal quantum realisation
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})}
in a given dimension
3<\dim(\mathcal{H})
.

Finally, minimal realisation in Prob.
4
will generally not be composed of rank-
1
projections. Consequently, quantum realisations restricted to rank-
1
projections will generally have larger dimension than minimal quantum realisations without this restriction. Moreover, even if a quantum realisation exists it is not clear whether
\dim^{*}(\mathbb{I})=\dim^{*}(\mathbbm{1})
.

Appendix F
Critical comparison with results in Ref.
[
46
,
160
,
51
]

In this section, we show that Thm. 1 in Ref.
[
46
]
and Thm. 4 in Ref.
[
51
]
which also follow from Thm. 1 in Ref.
[
160
]
are false without restricting to faithful graph completions. In short,
\chi({\overline{G(\pi)}}^{*})>d
does not imply
\chi(G)>d
for general realisations
\pi:G\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{H})}
.

We first remark that Ref.
[
160
,
51
]
consider the completion of orthogonality graphs and their (not necessarily unital) realisations implicitly.
88
88

88

We point out that Thm. 1 (specifically, the measure of contextuality defined in Eq. (1)) in Ref.
[
160
]
already (implicitly) considers the completion of an orthogonality graph
G
. In particular, it compares quantum and classical correlations with respect to
{\overline{G(\pi)}}
- yet, note that, by Prop.
3
, classical correlations on
{\overline{G(\pi)}}
are generally unrelated with classical correlations on
G
(see also App.
F
).
In particular, the distance defined in Eq. (1) in Ref.
[
160
]
is with respect to classical states on the completion of the respective orthogonality graph. However, classical correlations on
{\overline{G(\pi)}}
generally do not restrict to classical states on
G
in a straightforward manner (see App.
D.2
), indicating that Thm. 1 in Ref.
[
160
]
does not apply without considering completions.

An explicit counterexample is shown in Fig.
6
, and is based on the SI-C set in Ref.
[
188
]
, which is the completion of the following
13
vectors (see Fig.
6
(a))

\displaystyle z_{1}

\displaystyle=(1,0,0)

\displaystyle z_{2}

\displaystyle=(0,1,0)

\displaystyle z_{3}

\displaystyle=(0,0,1)

\displaystyle y^{+}_{1}

\displaystyle=(0,1,1)

\displaystyle y^{+}_{2}

\displaystyle=(1,0,1)

\displaystyle y^{+}_{3}

\displaystyle=(1,1,0)

(20)

\displaystyle y^{-}_{1}

\displaystyle=(0,1,\bar{1})

\displaystyle y^{-}_{2}

\displaystyle=(1,0,\bar{1})

\displaystyle y^{-}_{3}

\displaystyle=(1,\bar{1},0)

\displaystyle h_{0}

\displaystyle=(1,1,1)

\displaystyle h_{1}

\displaystyle=(\bar{1},1,1)

\displaystyle h_{2}

\displaystyle=(1,\bar{1},1)

\displaystyle h_{3}

\displaystyle=(1,1,\bar{1})\;,

where
\bar{1}=-1
. These vectors give rise to the following
16
contexts:

\displaystyle C(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})

\displaystyle C(z_{1},y^{+}_{1},y^{-}_{1})

\displaystyle C(z_{2},y^{+}_{2},y^{-}_{2})

\displaystyle C(z_{3},y^{+}_{3},y^{-}_{3})

\displaystyle C(h_{0},y^{-}_{1},x_{01})

\displaystyle C(h_{1},y^{-}_{1},x_{11})

\displaystyle C(h_{2},y^{+}_{1},x_{21})

\displaystyle C(h_{3},y^{+}_{1},x_{31})

\displaystyle C(h_{0},y^{-}_{2},x_{02})

\displaystyle C(h_{1},y^{+}_{2},x_{12})

\displaystyle C(h_{3},y^{+}_{2},x_{32})

where

denotes the context spanned by the projections onto the rays defined by the vectors
v_{1}

and

v_{3}

v_{1},v_{2}
. It follows that the same SI-C set arises from the completion of the

6
(b), where

,

and

. Yet, for the respective orthogonality graphs (see Fig.

) we find
\chi(G_{\mathrm{YO}})=4
while
\chi(G^{\prime}_{\mathrm{YO}})=3
. Consequently,
\chi(G)>d
is not a necessary criterion for the graph completion
\overline{\pi(V)}=\langle\pi(V),\mathbbm{1}\rangle
of
G
with respect to a generic realisation
\pi
to be a SI-C set.
